# Dedication

*For the ones who made the first writing on Earth — and for their grandchildren, who can still read it.*

*The world told a story about you: that you were too simple to have built your own wonders, so it
gave them to the sky. This book is a small attempt to hand them back. The cities were yours. The
writing was yours. The first time anyone wrote down why the work hurts and why we die — that was
yours. No one came down from a planet to make you. You made the planet legible.*

---

*And for AJ — from The Why Files.*

*Watch the official story all the way to the end — the alien one, the grand one, the one that's the
grandfather of the whole genre. Then read the actual clay, in the actual language, and watch it come
apart in your hands, gently, with love for everyone who needed it to be true. Take the debunk
seriously. Take the wonder seriously. Take the faith seriously. Live in the gap between them, where
it's actually interesting.*

*The word doesn't mean "those who came from the sky." It means the princely offspring. And you can
see Jupiter tonight.*

*So. Roll the tape.*

---

---

# Foreword

### *by Kelvin F. Girin* †

I have spent the better part of forty years in a room full of clay, and I want to begin by telling you the
single most astonishing thing I know, because almost nobody will let me say it plainly. It is not that the
gods came down from a planet. They didn't. It is this: *the oldest people we can read wrote the first draft
of your Bible, and they wrote it more than a thousand years before there was anyone we would call a Hebrew to
read it.*

That sentence is not fringe. It is not a provocation. It is what the tablets say, and I have held the tablets.
A man named George Smith — a banknote engraver's apprentice who taught himself to read the dead script in his
lunch hours — sat down in the British Museum in 1872 with a broken piece of seventh-century clay, and read a
flood: a man warned by a god, a boat built to a measure, a mountain, a dove sent out and returning, a raven
sent out and gone, a sacrifice, and the gods gathering over the smoke of it *like flies.* He was so undone by
what he was reading that, by the account of the man who knew him, he leapt up and began to take off his
clothes. I have always loved him for that. He had found Noah, sleeping in the clay, eleven centuries older
than Genesis — and he had the decency to be *amazed.*

Now. Here is where almost everyone, on both sides, runs for the exits, and I want to ask you not to. On the
one side stands the believer in the visitors — the gold-mining astronauts, the twelfth planet, the slave-race
bred from apes — and he holds the *wonder* and drops the *rigour*, and reaches past the staggering true thing
for a cheap one from the sky. And I understand the reach, I do; I will be tender with it in a moment. But it
is wrong, and it is wrong in a way you can check in an afternoon. The word he builds his cathedral on,
*Anunnaki*, does not mean "those who from heaven came." It means *the princely offspring* — the high-born
gods, the children of the sky and the earth — and across two thousand years of clay it drifts, quietly, from
the great deciders of fate to the grey judges of the dead, and it is never, not once, hitched to a planet. The
planet he wants, *Nibiru*, is a word that means *crossing* — a ferryman's crossing, a star's crossing of the
meridian — and when it is a planet at all it is Jupiter, and you can walk outside tonight and see it. The
"orbit of three thousand six hundred years" is a *number*, the Sumerian word for thirty-six hundred, the same
round figure their scribes used for the reigns of kings before the Flood. It is arithmetic mistaken for
astronomy. The whole sky-ship comes apart in your hands the moment you read the actual language — which is the
one thing its prophet never did.

On the other side stands the debunker, and he holds the *rigour* and drops the *wonder*, and having dismissed
the aliens he thinks he is finished, and walks away — and in walking away he steps straight past the real
thing, which is far stranger and cuts far closer. Because the gods those people made were not aliens; they
were an *answer* — the first written answer to the oldest questions a working, dying body asks: *why does the
labour hurt, why must we serve, why do we die?* And the answer they wrote down, in the cold dignity of clay,
is that the gods grew tired of their own toil and made us out of river mud and the blood of a god they had
killed, to carry the work for them, and built our dying in from the first day. That is not a comfortable
story. It does not flatter us the way the astronauts do — *designed, wanted, important enough to engineer.* It
is bleaker and it is braver, and it is *ours,* and it is the thing the sky-ship was invented to avoid looking
at.

And then — this is the part that still stops my breath in the gallery at closing time — a small, beaten people
were marched in chains to Babylon, the very city whose libraries held the older draft of the whole human
beginning, and instead of being swallowed they did the most extraordinary thing in the ancient record: they
*took it.* They took the conqueror's flood and his garden and his tower and his murdered-dragon sea, and they
bent every beam of it toward one God and a strange new insistence that the human being is not the gods'
slave-labour but the gods' *image* — and they carried it home and built it into the book half the planet now
lives by. That is not plagiarism, and any fool who tells you it is has understood nothing. It is *survival by
authorship.* It is a people who lost their temple and kept their soul by re-writing the empire's own story
until it served their God instead of Babylon's.

The author of this book is that rarest thing, the one who will stand in the gallery and hold both hands open
at once: *this is exactly as old as the careful men say, and it overturns the order we thought our story ran
in, and I will not flee it into aliens and I will not flee it into a shrug.* He gives the diggers and the
decipherers their full and honest due — he reveres the patient men who proved, in 1857, that they could read
the script at all, by sealing their four translations in four envelopes and finding they *agreed* — and he is
right to, because that blind sealed test is the exact opposite of the prophet's method, which is to choose the
answer first and bend the glyphs until they fit. But he is tender, too, where tenderness is owed: to the
lonely autodidact who needed the gods to be astronauts, and to the faithful who hold the old book as holy, and
he never once mistakes *a text has a history* for *a text is a lie.* Those are not the same sentence. He holds
them apart like glass.

There is a quiet man in here, too, far from his own country, who reads a made thing the way I have spent my
life trying to read the clay — who looks at the most famous "alien" seal in the world and sees, plainly, an
ordinary man kneeling before his god, and asks the only question that has ever opened anything: not *who made
it,* not *is it real,* but *what is it for.* I have watched clever people spend whole careers on the first two
and never think to turn the third, and the third is the key to the whole locked room.

You may close this book certain the old order is overthrown. You may close it sure we know far less than we
pretend, and that someone, somewhere, has been quietly deciding for a long time which of these stories the
rest of us are allowed to hear. If the author has done his work — and he has — you will close it as something
better and harder than either: a person willing to stand in front of a slab of river mud with the beginning of
the world cut into it, and to feel the whole vertigo of where it came from and where it went, and to say *I
don't know,* and to find in those three words not an ending but the oldest feeling we have — the one that very
likely made the gods in the first place.

Go and stand in front of the clay. There is a gallery in Bloomsbury, and there is a cradle in the south of
Iraq, and the people who made the first writing on Earth have living grandchildren who can still read it. Go.
The answer is allowed to stay a maybe. That it is allowed to is the whole gift of this book — and, I think,
the whole of what I have loved about the clay for forty years.

*— Kelvin F. Girin*

---

*† **“Kelvin F. Girin” is an anagram of IRVING FINKEL** — the British Museum Assyriologist and beloved
keeper of the cuneiform collection, who has himself translated a Babylonian flood tablet — borrowed here in
homage and affection. **This foreword is a work of fiction. It was written by the author of this book. Irving
Finkel did not write it, was not involved in it, and is not associated with or endorsing this book in any
way.** The name is rearranged precisely so that the tribute can never be mistaken for a real attribution; it
is a salute, not a claim.*

---

# Chapter 1 — The Most-Read Story on Earth

The thing under the glass was the size of a cigarette packet, and it had outlived every empire that ever sneered at small things.

Naila stood at the rope in Room 55 and did not look at the streaming crew yet, though she could hear them — the soft authoritative murmur of a man being recorded saying something he wanted very much to be true. She looked at the tablet. K.3375. Sixteen lines a side and a corner gone, the surface the colour of weak tea, the wedges packed so tight they read like a field of barley flattened by one careful wind. She let her eye fall into them the way she fell into a train timetable or a folded map: not reading, the cuneiform sat just past the edge of her Akkadian, but counting. The columns. The breaks. The rhythm of a scribe who had done this ten thousand times and was bored and excellent, both at once.

Most-read story on earth, the little label said, more or less. It was not bragging.

Behind her the city did what cities did, eleven million people pressing against the museum's quiet, and she kept her back to the room and her hand loose at her side and breathed through the part of her that wanted to count the people too. There were too many. There were always too many. The trick was to find one fixed true thing and stand near it. The tablet was a fixed true thing. The tablet had been a fixed true thing for two and a half thousand years.

"Roll it from the wonder," said a voice she would come to hate, gently, in a way that took her a moment to even register as hostile. "Don't open on the woo. We earn the woo."

She turned her head a degree.

The doc crew had set up across the room near the relief of the dying lion, which was either ignorance or cheek. A presenter in a charcoal henley, three days of designed stubble, the kind of warm low voice that sells a mattress. A DP with a gimbal. A producer in expensive trainers murmuring into a headset. And the light. They'd brought a single hard source and stood it low and raking, so it skidded across the tablet's face and threw every wedge into a long blue shadow, and the surface that was the colour of weak tea went the colour of a planetarium ceiling. Pinpricks. Constellations. A star chart for anyone who already wanted a star chart.

Edith saw it first. Naila felt her arrive at her shoulder before she heard her — the particular stillness of a person who looks for a living, gone very quiet.

"That's a key light," Edith said, low. "At about thirty degrees. You light a coin like that to read the date. You light *that*—" a tilt of the chin at the tablet "—like that to make a punter see Orion."

"It's the most-read story on earth," Naila said. "And they've lit it so nobody can read it."

"That's not an accident. That's the job." Edith's hands stayed at her sides. She had a camera on a strap and she was not lifting it, and Naila had learned, in the few days she'd known her, that Edith not lifting the camera was its own kind of sentence.

The presenter took a breath. The producer counted him in with two fingers.

"This," said the warm voice, and the room hushed for him because that is what the room is trained to do, "is the Flood Tablet. Most of you were taught it's a Bible story. Noah, the ark, two of every animal. What they *don't* teach you—" he let it land, the *they*, the whole conspiracy of teachers and curators and the people who'd kept the lights on in this building for two centuries "—is that this clay is a *thousand years older* than your Bible. And it doesn't just talk about a flood. It talks about the gods who *made* that flood. The Anunnaki. The ones who came down from the sky."

Frik made a sound.

He was three feet to Naila's left, hands jammed in the pockets of a jacket he'd zipped to the throat because crowds made the tics worse and he knew it, and the sound that came out of him was not a word, just a hard click of the soft palate, twice, and then his shoulder ticked up toward his ear and stayed a half-second too long. A woman near him stepped away. Naila did the thing she did, which was move a quarter step so she was between Frik and the woman, not a wall, just weather, just a shift in the room's pressure. Bashir was already on his other side, saying nothing, standing close enough that Frik had two known bodies in his peripheral and the room was smaller and the smaller a room the safer it was.

"—came down to mine our gold," the presenter went on, and his hand found the air over the glass like a faith healer's. "Think about that. A species, advanced beyond anything we can imagine, and they needed *workers*. So they made us. The book says God formed man from the dust of the ground. The *older* book—" again the *older*, like a knife sliding under a fingernail "—the older book tells you *who* did the forming. And it wasn't God."

"It is so beautiful," Bashir said, and he meant it, and it took Naila a second to understand he was not talking about the speech.

He was looking at the tablet. He'd come up on her right without her clocking it, which almost no one managed, and his head was tilted and his lips were moving, and she knew that movement. Bashir reading. Bashir's eyes did to language what hers did to number — fell into it, drowned a little, came up changed. He had once told a border guard in Trieste that he was three different nationalities in the space of forty minutes and walked out the far side of all three. He read the way other people breathed and he could not always stop, and he was reading the wedges now, the ones the doc crew had lit into oblivion, and he wasn't seeing stars. He was seeing a flood. The old flood. Six days and seven nights the wind blew, the storm and the flood overwhelmed the land. He had told her last night that it was the most frightened writing he knew, frightened in a way the king-lists and the beer-rations weren't, a man's hand shaking out the end of the world.

"They lit it like a planetarium," Naila said to him, quiet, "and you're reading the rain."

"Someone is always reading the rain," Bashir said. "That's why it's still here."

---

Here is the part nobody films, because it isn't slick, and Naila loved it more than she loved most living people.

In 1872, a man named George Smith sat in a back room of this same building, thirty-two years old, no university, no Greek, no Latin, no money — he had left school at fourteen and apprenticed as a banknote engraver, which is a trade about putting tiny careful marks on flat surfaces so that they cannot be faked. He used to spend his lunch hours upstairs at the galleries because he could not afford to spend them anywhere that cost anything, and a man named Birch noticed the engraver who kept staring at the dead languages, and gave him fragments to sort. Smith taught himself cuneiform off broken clay on his lunch breaks. There is no slicker way to say it because there isn't a slicker version. He just did it.

And one afternoon he was working through a pile of unbaked tablet fragments that had come out of the ground at Nineveh — the library of an Assyrian king who'd been dead two and a half thousand years and had wanted, the way men with too much power sometimes want, to own every story ever written — and Smith pieced together a broken line, and then the next, and the line said that a ship had come to rest on a mountain, and that a man had sent out a dove, and the dove had found no resting place and returned.

He was, by the account a colleague left, reading the eleventh tablet of the Epic of Gilgamesh, and he came to that line, and he stood up.

The colleague's name was Budge, and the line Budge left is the one Naila kept the way some people keep a saint's relic. Smith, said Budge, *jumped up and rushed about the room in a great state of excitement, and, to the astonishment of those present, began to undress himself.*

That was the whole of it. A self-taught engraver found Noah eleven centuries early, in a hand older than the Hebrew, and was so undone by it that he started taking his clothes off in the British Museum. Not because he'd disproved the Bible. Because he'd found that the story was *older and bigger and stranger* than anyone had known, that it had been waiting in the clay the whole time, and a man with no business reading it had read it, and the proper response to that — Naila was absolutely certain of this, it was the truest thing she knew — the only proper, sane, human response to that was to be amazed past the edge of your own dignity.

The doc crew would not be putting George Smith in the segment. There was no woo in a man who got it right and wept.

---

"Run me the line again," the presenter said. "The big one. About the workers."

The producer in the expensive trainers stepped in, and Naila saw Edith go still in the new way, the working way, and Edith's hand drifted toward the camera at her hip and did not lift it. "He says they made us to *bear the toil*," the producer said. "We'll cut to the gold-mine graphics. Spaceships, the lot. But land the toil. People feel the toil."

And the thing was — the thing that made Naila's jaw set, the thing she would still be turning over in two days when she stood in front of a worse room and a worse light — the thing was that they almost had it.

*Bear the toil.* That part was real. That part was in the actual clay, older than the flood tablet even, in a poem called Atrahasis where the lesser gods went on strike — went on *strike*, downed tools, surrounded the storehouse at night because they were sick of digging the river-channels for the big gods forever — and the high gods, panicking, hit on a solution: make a new thing to carry the work. Make it out of clay. Mix the clay with the blood of a slaughtered god so the thing would have a god's spark in it and a clay's weakness, and set it to the toil, so that the gods could rest.

That was the true story, and it was the bleakest story Naila knew, and the doc crew was three feet from it and walking the other way as fast as they could go.

Because the true story is that you were made to *work*. Made of mud and a murdered god, made tired, made mortal, made to dig the canal and feed the temple and die. There is no flattery in it anywhere. And the alien version took that exact bone — made to bear the toil — and dressed it in chrome. Made you a project. Made the sky want you. Made the toil into gold-mining, which at least is a *resource*, which at least means you were worth coming light-years for. The alien story whispered: you were *designed*. You were *wanted*. Somebody important enough to have a spaceship looked at the empty earth and thought, *I'll make something here.*

It was the kindest lie in the world, and the clay refused to tell it. The clay said: mud, and a dead god, and the canal won't dig itself.

They'd swapped the bravest story humans ever told themselves for a more comfortable one and called the comfortable one the *hidden truth*. Naila didn't have a word for what that did to her chest. Bashir would have had four words for it in four languages. She just stood there counting columns and feeling it.

---

"Six lines," she said, to nobody, to Edith, to the tablet.

"What?"

"The gold." Naila kept her voice under the presenter's. "Watch the segment they're building. It's the whole thing. The gods made man to mine gold. That's the spine of it, that's the whole twelfth-planet theory in one sentence, and it's the part they'll never put on the clay because it isn't *on* any clay. The toil is on the clay. Atrahasis, the Igigi, the strike, the blood, the mud. All real, all there, you could photograph it. But the gold—" she shook her head once "—the gold is the one thing they need, and it's the one thing they had to bring with them."

Edith was looking at her now and not at the doc crew. "How do you know it's not there?"

"Because if it were there it would be the most famous line in Assyriology and they'd have it on a mug in the shop." She nodded at the gift shop's far doorway. "It's not on a mug. It's nowhere. The gods are *exhausted* in the poems. They want to *rest*. That's the want. Rest, not riches. You make a slave because you're tired, not because you've found a seam." She heard her own voice flatten the way it did when the number wasn't adding and she was the only one in the room who could see the column not balancing. "They built a planet-sized story on a load that the freight doesn't carry. The toil is freight. The gold's a stowaway."

"Say that on camera," said Edith, very dry, "and they'd cut you for being boring."

"I am boring," Naila said. "That's why I'm right."

Edith almost smiled. Almost. She'd photographed three wars and a fourth she didn't name, and the almost was as far as her face went these days, but it counted, and Naila counted it.

---

The presenter was building to the close. The producer had her two fingers up. The DP had floated the gimbal in low and slow across the glass so that on the eventual cut the tablet would seem to *turn*, to reveal itself, the pinpricks sliding under the raking light like a sky rotating, and a viewer at home with a beer would feel, in the animal back of the brain, that they were looking at the heavens.

"So the next time someone tells you the Flood is just a Bible story," the warm voice said, and Naila could already hear the swell of the music that wasn't there yet, "ask them why the older book — the *original* book — says the gods came down. Ask them what those gods were *mining*. And ask yourself—" the pause, the killer pause, the one that paid the rent "—who taught a Bronze Age people about the solar system? Who told them about a planet you can't even *see*?"

And Frik, who had held it for nine minutes, who had clicked his palate and ticked his shoulder and stood between his two known bodies and breathed, said, loud and clear and helpless into the hush the producer had so carefully built:

"*Nobody — it's a numeral —*"

The room flinched. The producer's head came round. The presenter lost his line.

Frik's hand had come up to his own mouth, too late, the way it always came too late, his eyes wide with the particular horror of a man whose body has told the truth in front of strangers again, and Bashir's hand was already flat between his shoulder blades and Naila had already moved her quarter step and Mira — Mira, who'd been reading the whole room with her back to the wall, who hadn't heard the presenter's voice but had read every centimetre of the producer's spine and the DP's hands and the way the light fell — Mira's eyes went to Frik and then to Naila and then to the door, a clean three-point question: *him, then us, then out.*

"*Three thousand six hundred,*" Frik said, lower now, the dam down, the sentence finishing itself because it had to. "*The sar. It's three-six-zero-zero. Base sixty. It's how they wrote numbers. It's not an orbit, it's a—* sorry. Sorry. It's not an orbit."

"Sir," the producer said, "we are *filming*—"

"I know," Frik said miserably. "I'm sorry. I can't always—" the shoulder ticked. "I can't always not."

---

They moved him out the way the crew had learned to move, which was not as a unit, because a unit gets stopped. Bashir drifted Frik toward the Assyrian reliefs at an angle that read as *wandering*. Mira went the other way and stood by the great winged bull where she could see the door and Edith's face both, her hands resting loose, ready to sign the all-clear or the abort. Yusuf — Naila hadn't even seen him in the room, Yusuf had a gift for being the man no one logged — peeled off toward the producer with his palms open and his voice already apologising, *so sorry, my brother, neurological thing, completely involuntary, we'll get out of your light,* the money-man's voice, the voice that unblocked things, and the producer's shoulders came down a degree because Yusuf had given her a story she could tell her boss.

Naila stayed at the rope one more second.

The presenter was getting reset. The light was getting re-aimed. The blue shadows slid back across the wedges. And the tablet sat there under all of it being exactly what it was, the most-read story on earth in its oldest surviving hand, a man's shaking record of the end of the world and the dove that came back with nothing and the dove that came back with an olive, written down by people who knew the river could kill them and had decided to write it down anyway, to say *we were here, the water came, a few of us lived.*

Frik was right and Frik was not allowed to be right out loud, which was the whole shape of the world in one room. The *sar* was 3,600. It was a numeral. It was how you wrote a big round number when you counted in sixties, the way they did, the way the clock on her phone still did, sixty seconds, sixty minutes, three hundred and sixty degrees, the dead Sumerian arithmetic still ticking under everyone's wrist. Someone had taken that numeral and called it an orbit and called the orbit a planet and called the planet a homeworld and called the homeworld a destiny, and at no point — at no single point in that whole climbing tower — had they gone back down to the bottom and checked whether the first brick was even a brick.

It wasn't. It was a number. It was the careful boring true thing, and the careful boring true thing was always the first casualty, and Naila found that she was angry in the specific way she got angry, which was quiet and patient and good with files.

There was a file. There was already a file. There would be more of a file by tomorrow.

Edith fell in beside her as they walked. She still hadn't lifted the camera. "You clocked the gold and I clocked the light," she said. "Same hole. Two ends."

"The segment was supposed to prove it," Naila said. "It just showed me where it breaks. Both places. You don't light a thing like that if the thing speaks for itself."

"No," Edith said. "You light it like that when you're selling it. The question is what." She glanced back once at the doc crew, at the producer murmuring into the headset, at the slick warm machine of it humming back to life over the rope. "Who pays for a museum shoot to tell people not to read the most-read thing on earth?"

Out past the colonnade, in the grey London afternoon, a man in good sunglasses was leaning against a bollard with his arms folded, perfectly at ease, watching the doors. He wore a clean shirt and the kind of stillness that doesn't photograph. When Edith's eye went to him on reflex — old habit, the conflict shooter's habit, find the man not looking at anything — he was already looking somewhere else, and her thumb found the camera and did not press it, and Naila understood that she'd just declined a shot, and that the declining cost her, and that she'd file *that* away too.

"He brought us here," Edith said, which was all the answer she gave about the man, and it wasn't an answer at all.

Behind them the warm voice started again from the top, earning its woo.

In front of them Frik had his breathing back and Bashir's hand off his shoulder and was already saying, low and shamed and a little wild, *it really isn't an orbit, though,* and Mira signed something at him that the crew read as *we know, you absolute disaster, we love you,* and Yusuf came out of the building grinning the grin of a man who'd just talked a producer out of calling security, and the man in the sunglasses was gone from the bollard as though he'd never had weight.

"Naila," Yusuf said. "Tonight. The file. You said you had the case all in one place."

"I have it in one place," Naila said.

The numeral. The crossing-point. The man named Dubsiga who'd had his solar system stolen and was about to get it back. She had the bottom brick, and she'd been waiting a long time for a room that wanted to see the whole tower come down honestly, with love, in the original language, which was the only honest place there was.

"Then start at the bottom," Yusuf said. "Start with the planet that isn't a planet."

"It's a planet," Naila said. "That's the good part. It's just not *that* planet."

And she went to get her file.

# Chapter 2 — Crossing-Point

The flat over the kebab shop on Caledonian Road had two exits and Naila had counted them before she took her coat off. The front door to the stairs. The fire escape past the bathroom, the bolt stiff with paint, twelve seconds to clear if she'd oiled it, which she had, last Tuesday, with the WD-40 Yusuf kept under the sink for the Beast's hinges three thousand miles away. Two exits. The number sat where it needed to sit and let the rest of her come into the room.

The rest of the room was the crew, and the file.

The file was good. The file was the best thing she owned. It lived in a green box-folder soft at the corners, and tonight it lived open on the table between the teapot and Frik's elbow, and she had laid it out in the only order it could go in, which was the order of the clay itself: oldest underneath, the fiction on top, so you peeled down through two thousand years to get to the man.

"Lights," Mira signed, and pointed at the standing lamp, and Bashir got up and angled it off the ceiling so it stopped glaring on the laminated sheets. Mira sat where she could see all their faces and the table both, which was the chair Naila would have wanted and had given her without a word the first week, because the woman who reads faces gets the chair that reads faces.

"Okay," Yusuf said. He had the look he got when a thing was finally about to be free. "You said you had the planet that isn't a planet."

"It's a planet," Naila said again. "I keep telling you. It's a very good planet. You can see it tonight."

She turned the top sheet over.

She had thought, for years, about how to do this without doing the thing she hated. The thing she hated had a sound to it, a particular curdle, the sound of a clever man on a stage saying *and of course it's all nonsense* with his chin up, getting his little laugh. She had watched a hundred hours of that man, that voice, on a hundred channels, and she had hated him as much as she hated the warm credulous one, because they were the same coin spent two ways, and neither of them had ever once read the clay.

So she did not start with *nonsense*. She started with the want.

"Here's the story they're selling," she said. "And it's a good story. I want to say that first, because I'm going to take it apart, and I don't want anyone in this room thinking I'm sneering at it. I'm not. It's a *beautiful* story. A planet called Nibiru, the twelfth planet, swings in on a long elliptical orbit — three thousand six hundred years to come round — and the last time it came round, the beings on it landed in Mesopotamia, and they were our gods, the Anunnaki, and they made us. Out of an ape and their own blood. To dig their gold."

"To dig their gold," Yusuf repeated, very quietly, and Naila heard the weight in it and let it stand. He'd wanted that story once. Maybe he still half-wanted it. That was allowed at this table.

"And it explains everything," she went on. "Why we got smart so fast. Why every culture has gods coming down. Why the King List gives the old kings these insane reigns — twenty-eight thousand years, you can't reign twenty-eight thousand years unless you're not really human. It explains the gap. It makes us important. Somebody came an unbelievable distance, for us." She put her finger flat on the top sheet, on the printed diagram, the planets in their pretty arc. "That's the want. Hold onto the want while I read you the clay. Because the want is the only part that's real, and it's *ours*, and I'd never take it off you."

Frik's foot was going under the table, heel-toe, heel-toe, a small private engine. He had the under-sheet pulled toward him already, the photograph, the one with the cuneiform on it. His lips were moving over nothing.

"Nibiru," Naila said. "Start there. It's the whole brand. It's on the book covers, it's the channel name, it's a tattoo on at least four men I've argued with online. So. The word's real. It's *attested*, it's in the texts, you can look it up." She slid the next sheet out — her own transliteration, her own hand under the printed line, because she didn't trust a printed transliteration she hadn't checked. "*Né-bé-ru.* From the verb, *ebēru.* To cross. To ford a river. A *nēberu* is a crossing-place. A ford. A ferry-point. The spot where you get across the water."

Bashir made a sound, not quite a word, the small glad noise he made when a word opened its hand. "It's a *place*," he said. "Not a body. A function. The crossing."

"The crossing," Naila said. "And in the astronomy texts — and Mesopotamia had real astronomy, centuries of night-after-night records, the best naked-eye sky-watching in the ancient world — *nēberu* is what they call the point where a planet crosses. Where it sits at a particular station. And the planet they hang it on, mostly, the one called the crossing-point —" she let it land soft "— is Jupiter."

She watched it go round the table. She loved this part and she made herself love it slowly.

"Jupiter," Yusuf said.

"Marduk's star," Naila said. "Babylon's god, up in the sky as the biggest brightest planet there is, and they called it the crossing-point because of where it sat in their year. That's *nibiru.* That's the twelfth planet. It's Jupiter. And here's the thing —" she got the next sheet, the one she'd printed off the planetarium site that afternoon "— it's up right now. Tonight. East, after dark, brightest thing that isn't the moon. You can walk down to the canal and look at the twelfth planet. It's been crossing every single year for the entire history of the human eye. It never went away. It never comes *in.* It's just there, doing exactly what it's done, and somebody read a Babylonian word for *the ford* and built a returning death-star out of it."

"Is it ever," Frik said, fast, his voice catching the way it did, "is it ever attached to the Anunnaki. In the texts. Is the crossing-point ever the Anunnaki's planet."

That was the whole game in one sentence, and he'd asked it the way he asked everything, like the question had jumped him from behind.

"No," Naila said. "That's the cleanest part. Never. Not once. The whole structure — Nibiru-is-the-Anunnaki's-homeworld — there's no tablet where those two things touch. You've got a word for Jupiter's crossing over here, and you've got the Anunnaki over there, and the bridge between them was built in the nineteen-seventies. On a typewriter. In English."

Mira had been watching the file and watching Naila's mouth and watching Yusuf's face, all three at once, the way she did, and now she lifted her hands. *And the Anunnaki,* she signed. *What's the word actually mean.* Bashir voiced it under his breath for the room, a beat behind her hands, because that was the rule they'd settled into — Mira led, the voice followed, never the reverse.

Naila reached for the word like reaching for a warm thing.

"This is my favourite," she said. "*Anunnaki.* *A-nun-na-ki.* The princely seed. The princely offspring. The *ki* on the end is *earth* — the offspring of the prince, of An, the sky-father, brought down to earth. The high gods' noble children. That's what the word *says.*" She paused. "And here's what nobody puts on a book cover, because it ruins the brand. The word *drifts.* Over two thousand years it slides. By the late period the Anunnaki aren't even the headline gods anymore — they get demoted, sort of, they become the gods of the *underworld.* The judges of the dead. The princely offspring start out as the bright nobility of heaven and end up as the bureaucracy of hell, and that drift took twenty centuries, and you can *track* it, tablet by tablet, because these people wrote everything down." She sat back. "That's not a spaceship crew. That's a word doing what words do over two thousand years of a living religion. They turned a theology into a flight manifest. You can't do that to a word that was alive that long. It's like reading 'host' on a Communion wafer and deciding the early church meant an *army.*"

Frik's foot stopped. He had the photograph an inch from his face. "Three thousand six hundred," he said. "The orbit. The *sar.*"

"The sar," Naila said, and now she got out the pencil.

This was the part she could do with her body, and she liked the parts she could do with her body, because pattern in the hand was quieter than pattern in the talk. She turned a sheet over to its blank back and drew a wedge. One clean wedge, pressed-in at the head, tailing off.

"Base sixty," she said. "They counted in sixties. That's why we have sixty seconds and sixty minutes and three hundred sixty degrees in a circle — that's *them*, that's still on your phone right now, four thousand years later. And in base sixty, you've got signs. A unit. A ten. And a big one." She drew it — the *sar*, the great circle-sign, fat and certain. "Sixty sixties. Three thousand six hundred. It's a *number.* It's the numeral for three thousand six hundred, the way that —" she tapped the blank corner "— a comma and three zeros is *thousand.* It's notation."

She turned back to the file and pulled the King List sheet, the famous one, the one that always got quoted.

"And here's where the alien case eats itself," she said. "Because they *love* the King List. The huge reigns, the antediluvian kings ruling tens of thousands of years — proof, they say, that the early rulers weren't human. And the reigns are written *in sars.* This king reigned ten sars. Eight sars. The numbers are colossal and that's the whole thrill of it." She put the pencil down. "So you can't have it both ways. Either the sar is a number — three thousand six hundred — and the reigns are just very big numbers, big the way myth makes everything big, a kingship measured in cosmic units to say *the old world was vast and we are small.* Or the sar is a planet's orbit. It can't be a planet's orbit in one sentence and a count of years in the next sentence on the *same tablet.*" She let it sit. "It's a number. It was always a number. It's the number the priests used for *king reigned a very long time* and the number a modern man pointed at and said *orbit.* He needed an orbit. The text was just doing arithmetic."

"The sar is a number," Frik said, and it came out of him almost gentle, almost like relief, the truth let off its leash with nowhere bad to run. "Not an orbit. It's the number."

"It's the number," Naila said. "You absolute disaster. Yeah."

Mira's face did the thing it did, the corner of it, and she signed *we know we love you* and Frik caught it this time and ducked his head and was, for one second, in a room that wasn't a problem.

Yusuf had gone quiet in the deep way. He was looking at the King List sheet, at the impossible reigns, and Naila knew the look because she'd worn it herself, years back, in the bad small flat before this one, when the file was thin and the want was thick. He'd had the long orbit on his wall once. The thirty-six-hundred-year clock. The countdown. It had been a door out of an ordinary life, and she'd just put a pencil through it, and she did the next part for him without saying it was for him.

"The want's still real," she said, to the table, to all of them, to him. "I want to be really clear. The man who built this — he wasn't a con man. I've read everything he wrote. He *loved* this stuff. He sat with the texts longer than almost anyone alive who wasn't paid to. He wanted us to be wanted. He wanted somebody to have crossed a vast dark for *us.* That's not stupid. That's the most human thing there is. The aliens are a way of not being alone." She tapped the file. "It's just not what the clay says. And what the clay says is *worse,* and that's the thing I can't get over, that's the thing I keep coming back to —"

"Show them VA 243," Bashir said softly. "Show them the man."

So she did the last one. The reach's crown jewel. The cylinder seal in Berlin, the little stone you rolled across wet clay to make your signature, four thousand years old, the one that's on the cover of the most famous book in the genre because it has, in its top corner, a star with dots around it — and somebody counted the dots and said: *the solar system. Carved before they could have known. Proof.*

"This is the one that does it for people," she said. "Every time. It's a *picture* of the solar system, they say, with the right number of planets, on a seal from four thousand years ago. And I get it. I do. It looks like a sun with planets. You want it to be that."

She laid the seal photograph down, and beside it, her own line, her own hand under the signs, slow and certain.

"It's a seal," she said. "A signature stone. And these things almost always say whose they are — they're *property*, they're *I am so-and-so, servant of such-and-such god.* That's most of what's written on the planet, by weight, in this entire civilisation. Not prophecy. Not star charts. *Receipts. Names. Whose this is.*" Her finger moved along the wedges. "And this one has an inscription, a little panel of cuneiform, and the inscription is a dedication, and it gives a name. *Dubsiga.*" She said it carefully. She always said it carefully, this name, because nobody else ever had, for four thousand years, until a few scholars and now this table. "It says, more or less — this belongs to Dubsiga, and below it the name of the man who *owned* the seal, who's presenting himself before his god. A servant before his lord. That's the scene. That's what the picture is. Not a star map. A man kneeling. Bringing his offering. Saying *I am yours.*"

"And the dots," Frik said. "The sun and the dots."

"A star symbol," Naila said. "A standard one. Divine. You put a star to mark *this is a god, this is the holy thing.* The dots are dots. It's decoration around a god-sign on a man's signet. Somebody counted the dots and found his solar system in another man's name-stamp." She sat very still. "His name was Dubsiga. He had a god he answered to. He had a seal made, beautiful work, and he rolled it in the clay so people would know his word was good. And four thousand years later we took his stone and rubbed his name off it and wrote *solar system,* and put it on a book, and made him scenery. That's the part I can't — that's the —" The word didn't come. She let it not come. "We had a whole person in our hands and we turned him into a UFO."

The kebab shop below sent up a gust of frying onion through the floor. Somewhere out on Caledonian Road a bus knelt and hissed. Nobody said anything for a moment, and the not-saying was the right size, and Mira held it open with her hands in her lap, not signing, just letting the room be quiet on purpose, which was a thing only Mira could do at this table, make a silence that meant *yes.*

"That's the case," Naila said at last. "All of it. There's more — there's always more, there's the bit where someone reads a school exercise about beer rations as a cargo manifest — but that's the floor. The crossing-point is Jupiter. The princely offspring are gods whose name drifted for two thousand years. The orbit is a numeral. The solar system belongs to a man named Dubsiga. Every single 'decode' is a bad translation. Every one. You just have to read it in the language it was written in, because that's the only honest place there is. You can't catch the cheat in English. The cheat lives in the gap between the languages and it dies the second you close the gap."

She had said it. The whole tower, down, in order, with love. She felt the clean of it, the particular relief that was hers and not like anyone else's relief — the relief of a thing that *resolved*, a transliteration with no slack in it, every sign accounted for, the ledger balancing to the wedge. It was the best feeling she had. It was better than being right. It was being *exact.*

And then it turned over in her, the way it always did, into the thing she couldn't flatten.

"But," she said.

"But," said Bashir, who knew. He was smiling the loneliest happiest smile in the building.

"The real thing is more astonishing than the fake thing," Naila said. "That's what nobody warns you about when you go to debunk this. You go in thinking you'll find dust and you find — God. The real texts out-wonder the fiction so badly it's almost rude. We make people from clay and a god's blood. Not to be loved — to *work.* To bear the gods' toil so the gods could rest. There's a flood and a man told to build a boat and a bird sent out to find land — a thousand years before Noah, in the same shape, in the same part of the world." She heard the next thing come up and she said it the careful way, the way that was true and didn't take anyone's God off them. "Not copied. Not identical. Different boat, different mountain, different birds, different days. But the same *room.* The same conversation. People telling each other the world's oldest story across a thousand years and a couple of languages, handing it down, arguing with it, changing it." She spread her hand over the whole file, the fiction and the clay together. "The fiction flatters us. *You were designed. You were wanted. Somebody crossed the dark for you.* And the real one says, *you were made out of mud to do the work nobody else would do, and you die, and you knew that, and you made the most beautiful writing in the history of the world about it anyway.* That's braver. That's so much braver. The fake one's a hug. The real one's a *people.*"

She stopped because her throat had got tight in the place it got tight, and she didn't perform the tight throat, she just waited it out, and the crew waited it out with her, which was the whole reason there was a crew.

Yusuf reached over and turned the King List sheet face-down, gently, the way you'd close someone's eyes. He didn't say it was for himself either. He said: "So why doesn't anyone *know* this. I had the long orbit on my wall for four years. Four years. And the man who could've told me in one sentence — *the sar's a number* — that man's got no audience and the orbit's got eleven million views."

"Because the fake one's a better watch," Edith said, from the windowsill, where she'd been sitting with the camera dark in her lap. She'd shot the file open on the table earlier, asked first, and then she'd stopped. "I framed alien tablets for a glossy outfit once, years back, before — I know exactly how you light a school-text about beer to make it look like it came off a saucer. It's not even hard. You underexpose, you go low and raking so the wedges throw shadows, you never, ever show the line that says *this is Dubsiga's.*" She turned the dead camera over in her hands. "The truth's a wide shot in flat light with a footnote. Nobody buys the footnote."

"There's a thing, though," Bashir said slowly, and they all looked at him, because Bashir went slow only when a thing had been bothering him sideways. He had his thumb on the photograph of the Flood Tablet — he'd carried the image out of Room 55 in his eye the way he carried words. "The doc crew today. They had a *list.* I saw the call-sheet on the producer's clipboard when Yusuf was charming her. The tablets they're shooting this whole series. And it's all —" he searched for it "— it's all the loud ones. VA 243's on it. The 'twelve planets' one. Every object that *photographs* like a mystery." He frowned. "And the Flood Tablet's on it but only the part that says *the gods sent the flood.* Not the part Smith actually wept over. The story. Just the woo-shaped slice of the story."

"That's just what sells," Yusuf said. "We just said that."

"Maybe." Bashir kept his thumb on the clay. "But I've watched three of these series get made now, doing the money for one of them. And it's never the *same* tablets that go quiet. It's always the boring true ones. The disproving ones. The school-text about beer rations — the actual evidence, the dull stuff that kills the case the second somebody reads it. Those don't get lost loud. They just —" he opened his hand "— stop being available. Stop getting filmed. Slide off the list. Every time. Like somebody keeps choosing which ones the world gets to see, and the choosing all leans one way."

The onion-gust came up again through the floor. Naila looked at the green folder, soft at its corners, the whole tower laid down in order, and felt the pattern try to assemble itself and refuse — too few data points, a shape with the start of a shape but no closure, the worst kind, the kind that itches.

"That's a coincidence," she said, because it was the honest thing to say. "Probably. Three series isn't a sample."

"Probably," Bashir agreed, and nobody at the table believed it all the way, and nobody could prove a single inch of it, which was exactly the trouble.

Yusuf's phone buzzed on the table, walked an inch, stopped. He read it and his whole face changed register. He looked up, and there was the grin from the museum steps, the talked-the-producer-down grin, but under it something straighter.

"Speaking of which," he said. "He's back tomorrow. Wants the file, Naila. The whole tower. Because we're not staying in London with it."

"Where," said Mira, reading it off his mouth before he aimed the word at her.

"Erbil," Yusuf said. "There's a woman there who can do to my pattern what Naila just did to the orbit. Reads the language. Reads the *meaning.* And she's already said no to us once."

Naila looked at the two exits, front and fire-escape, and then at the file, and then — past the kebab-shop window, out over the orange wash of the road — east, where Jupiter was up, crossing, doing what it had always done, while down here the question of *who keeps choosing what we get to see* sat on the table with no answer and the start of a shape.

"Then we'd better pack the clay," she said, "in the right order."

# Chapter 3 — The Man Who Reads the Made Thing

The thing about Jakobus, Yusuf had decided, somewhere over the dark of eastern Turkey, was that you never caught him arriving. You caught him already there.

The flight into Erbil came down through a brown haze of late afternoon, and the airport was new and chilled and wrong, all marble and cologne and a duty-free wall of watches, the kind of place that has been built recently and at speed to tell the world a story about itself. They cleared the visa-on-arrival desk in a slow Kurdish queue, Bashir doing the talking, switching from his careful Sorani to the officer's Arabic to a soft word of Aramaic that made the man look up once, surprised, and stamp the passport a half-second kinder. Mira watched the officer's hands instead of his mouth and told Bashir afterward, *He liked you,* and Bashir said *He liked the Aramaic,* and Frik said *He liked the Aramaic,* a half-beat behind, and nobody minded because nobody ever minded the echo from Frik, it was just the room saying a thing twice.

And then they came out through the sliding doors into a wall of heat that smelled of diesel and dust and somebody's cardamom, and there, leaning on the bonnet of a dust-coloured Land Cruiser that had no business being in a Kurdish airport car park, was the Beast.

Yusuf stopped.

He'd known it was shipped. He'd signed two of the carnet forms himself, in a logistics English so dry it could start fires, and he still stopped, because there is knowing a thing is coming and there is the thing already arrived and weeping water from a canvas bag lashed to its wing mirror, beading, dripping, the oldest trick in the desert — wet the canvas, let the heat steal the water, let the stealing cool what's left inside. The bag swung a little. Somebody walking past it would not have looked twice. Yusuf looked three times.

The man wasn't at the truck.

He was over by the taxi rank, crouched on his heels in the shade of a concrete pillar, and he was not doing anything, which was the part you couldn't film. He had a small tin in one hand, the lid off, and he was tipping a measure of something rust-red into a paper cup of tea a porter had given him — cayenne, Yusuf would have bet his fee on it, cayenne in the tea in this heat, a desert thing, sweat-to-cool-you-down, *makes-the-skin-do-its-job* — and he and the porter were not exactly talking. They were sitting in a quiet together. The porter said something and Jakobus answered with two words and a slow tip of the chin, and the porter laughed, and it was the laugh of a man who has just been seen by a stranger and not asked for anything.

"Spas," Jakobus said. *Thank you.* Kurdish. A courtesy handful, the right one, no more.

"He's already met the airport," Bashir said, beside Yusuf, dragging two cases. "Look. He's met it."

"He met it before we landed," Yusuf said.

—

They drove in toward the city with the light going long and amber and the Beast's air-con turned off, all the windows down, because the man wouldn't run it. *You don't learn a place from inside a fridge,* he'd said once, in the Namib, and it was the only time Yusuf had ever heard him explain anything, and even that wasn't really an explanation, it was a fact he'd put on the table for them to take or leave.

So they took the heat. Bashir up front, naming road signs out loud in three scripts. Mira behind him with her face to the window, reading the whole moving city — the men at the petrol queue, the woman arguing a price down at a fruit cart with her chin and her wrist and not one word Mira could hear, the boy who flagged them and got a flat palm, *no,* from Jakobus and grinned and flagged the next car. Frik with his hands flat on his thighs, pressing, the long-haul tic-load coming off him in the safe car the way it never came off in the airport queue, a soft *hut,* *hut,* a roll of the shoulder, and Edith beside him with her camera in her lap, lens cap on, not shooting.

She'd asked. That was the new thing about Edith, the thing Yusuf had watched grow over three jobs like a callus over a burn. At the airport she'd lifted the camera at the weeping water bag and then she'd lowered it and looked at the back of Jakobus's neck, and he hadn't turned round, and she'd put the cap back on. Nobody had said anything. It was a conversation that had happened entirely without a sentence in it, and Mira had watched the whole thing and signed, badly, to Yusuf, *She asked. He didn't answer. So no.* And that was right. That was exactly the shape of it.

"He's not going to let you get him," Yusuf told her now, low, over the engine.

"I know," Edith said. She wasn't unhappy about it. "I've shot warlords who wanted it more than he doesn't." She turned the camera over in her lap. "There's no frame. I keep finding the frame and it's empty. Like he stepped out of it on the way in."

"That's the man," Yusuf said.

—

Naila had not spoken since the airport.

She sat in the very back, the file across her knees in its grey box, both hands flat on the lid, and she was doing the thing she did, the thing that wasn't magic, that Yusuf had learned the hard way not to call magic, because the one time he'd called it that she'd gone very flat and very polite and said, *It's arithmetic. Please don't make it a séance.* She was counting the city. Not consciously, maybe. Telephone poles. The repeat of a shopfront font. The interval of the streetlights coming on as the dusk dropped. When the intervals held she eased. When the city did something irregular — a roundabout, a wall of car horns, a knot of bodies at a junction with no clear way through — Yusuf watched her hand tighten on the box and her eyes go to the doors.

"Two minutes," he said to her, because that was the deal, you gave her the time and the exit and you did not narrate her nervous system at her. "We're nearly up the hill. It opens out."

It opened out.

The road climbed and the city fell away below them in a wash of low concrete and palm and minaret and the long industrial glow of the new malls, and then ahead, lifted up out of all of it on its own mound, lamp-lit, gold against the bruise-blue east, was the Citadel.

Bashir said the number before anyone asked. He couldn't help it; it was his version of Frik's echo.

"Six thousand years," he said. "Maybe more. People have lived up there for six thousand years without stopping. Not a ruin you visit. A roof somebody slept under last night." He turned in his seat. "Oldest continuously inhabited place on earth, they argue. Jericho fights them for it. Damascus fights them for it." He grinned. "They can fight. It's old."

And Naila looked up the hill at the lamplit wall of it, at the thing that had been a town when writing was young, and her hand came off the box.

"Okay," she said quietly. "Okay. That's — okay."

It was the most Yusuf had ever heard her say about beauty.

—

The uncle's place was below the Citadel, off the bazaar, a flat-fronted house with an awning of corrugated tin and grape vine, two plastic chairs, a low table, and a samovar already going. They were not staying there — that was a hotel near the souk, all arranged, Yusuf had arranged it — but Jakobus took them here first, to the awning and the tea, before the rooms, before the work, and that was not an accident either. The uncle was Narin's; Yusuf knew that much and not much more. An old Assyrian man with a white moustache and reading glasses pushed up into his hair, who came out and clasped Jakobus's hand in both of his like a man greeting weather he'd been told was coming, and said something in Syriac that Bashir caught and didn't translate, only went still, the way Bashir went still when a language did something to him in a place too tender to repeat.

"Tea," the uncle said, to all of them, in English. "Sit. You came on the road. Sit."

So they sat in the dark under the vine with the bazaar noise coming up the lane, and the man poured tea into little waisted glasses, two-handed, the dark stewed *çay* over the sugar, and Jakobus sat among them and did the thing he did with his hands around a hot glass in the heat, which made no sense and which Yusuf had stopped trying to make sense of.

"He's not here," Edith said suddenly, very low, only to Yusuf, watching the table. "Narin. The uncle keeps glancing at the lane. He's expecting her and she's not coming tonight."

"She already said no once," Yusuf said.

"She'll say no again," Edith said. "You can see it in the way the old man's bracing for us. He knows she's going to be hard on you and he's sorry about it in advance." She turned the camera, still capped, in her hands. "Don't film the uncle. Whatever happens. He poured us tea. You don't pay a man back for tea by taking his picture."

"No," Yusuf agreed. And then, because it was the kind of thing he'd never have said three jobs ago: "Good catch."

—

Here is the part Yusuf would tell people about, after, the part that he could never quite get to land because it had no event in it.

After the tea, Jakobus took the grey box from Naila's knees — *asked* for it, palm up, and waited until she handed it, which she did, which surprised Yusuf because she handed almost nothing to anyone — and he set it on the low table and lifted the lid, and inside, on foam, in a sleeve, were the prints. Naila's working prints. Big crisp photographs of the clay, of the tablets and the seals, of the rolled-out impression of the one everyone was here for in the end, the seal they call VA 243, the one that has launched a thousand websites, the one with the man and the star and the little circles that the whole alien story hangs on like a coat on a single nail.

The streaming-doc version of this object — Yusuf had watched it on the plane, he made himself watch the competition — the streaming-doc version put it on screen with a low synth drone and a slow push-in and a voice saying *some researchers believe* over a graphic of the solar system, the little circles lit up one by one like a planetarium, *and here, four thousand years before Copernicus, is a complete diagram of the planets — including a world modern astronomers have yet to find.* And then the synth swelled and they cut to a man in a good shirt in front of a bookshelf saying you couldn't rule it out.

That was the easy version. The flattering version. The one that says: somebody knew. Somebody up there knew, and drew it for us, and we are the kind of creatures worth drawing for.

Jakobus looked at the print for a long time.

He did not say *solar system.* He did not say *aliens,* and he did not say *nonsense,* which was the other cheap thing, the sneer, the *what-idiots* that Yusuf had heard too many clever men reach for. He just looked, with his glasses on the table and his thumb not touching the surface of the photograph, and the bazaar went on past the awning, and the uncle watched him look.

"Who is he," Jakobus said.

Not *what is it.* Not *is it real.* *Who is he.* He'd found the man in it. Past the star, past the little circles, past the whole apparatus of wonder the West had bolted onto the thing — he'd gone straight to the small figure on the left, seated, one hand lifted.

"That's the part nobody films," Bashir said slowly, leaning in. "Look. He's not a chart. He's a man. He's *seated.* There's a figure standing, bringing him forward — that's the introduction posture, that's a worshipper being led in. And the seated one with the horned cap — that's the god. The little circles are around the god's shoulder, by his star, his emblem. It's not a diagram of where things are. It's a picture of a man being brought before his god." He sat back. "It's the most ordinary thing in the world. It's the single most ordinary scene in all of Mesopotamian art. A man, and his god, and the introduction. They made thousands of them."

"And the writing?" Jakobus said.

"There's writing," Bashir said. "Right there in the field. There's a name." He looked at Naila. "There's always a name. These were signatures. You rolled them in wet clay to say *this is mine, this is sealed, this is witnessed.* They're not theology. They're not astronomy. They're the Bronze Age equivalent of the franking on an envelope. And somewhere in the cuneiform on this one there is a man's name, and he is a real man, and he had a god he was being introduced to, and he sealed his letters with the two of them together because it mattered to him, the way you'd put your father's photo on your desk."

Frik had been quiet under the vine, pressing his palms. Now the over-reach came at the table — not from Frik, from the doc still playing in Yusuf's head, the *world astronomers have yet to find,* the voice swelling — and Frik flinched as though it had been said aloud in the room, and his head jerked, and out it came, fast, involuntary, true:

"It's a *receipt.*"

A beat.

"It's a receipt," he said again, quieter, his own echo, his shoulders coming down. "Sorry. It's. It's a receipt with a god on it."

And the uncle laughed. The first laugh of the night out of the old man, a short surprised bark, and he reached over and put his hand on Frik's forearm, light, and said, "Yes. A receipt. We had receipts before we had the wheel, habibi. We had receipts before we had *kings.*"

"That's the joke," Naila said. She'd come on, the way she came on when the pattern closed, flat and certain and lit up. "That's the whole — the alien version says it's a star map four thousand years ahead of its time, and it's so much *better* than that, it's so much stranger, it's a man clocking in. People had been keeping accounts in this clay for a thousand years before this seal got cut. Tokens, tallies, who owes whom how much barley, how much beer. Writing didn't start with poetry or prayer. It started with *inventory.* They invented the first writing on earth to keep track of *stuff.*" She put her finger an inch above the print, not touching. "This — this whole language, the one that the alien story says aliens had to come down and give us — we built it ourselves to count sheep and not get cheated."

She said it the way other people said *I love you.*

—

Jakobus put the print back in its sleeve. Settled it on the foam. Closed the box, and the bazaar noise came back up into the gap his attention had been holding shut, and Yusuf realised that for two full minutes nobody at the table had been thinking about anything but a man and his god in a piece of clay, and that this was the whole trick of him, the thing no camera got — he didn't tell you what to see. He stood next to the thing and asked it the right question and then got out of the light so you could look.

"It does a job," Jakobus said. To no one. To the box. "Everything that lasts does a job. You find the job, you find the man." He pushed the box an inch back toward Naila. "This does a man's job. Saying *I was here, this was mine, this was witnessed.* Four thousand years and we can still read the I-was-here." He stood, knees creaking, and pressed the always-clean hanky once to his neck and folded it away, and looked down at the six of them under the vine. "You can read the made thing. You read it better than the people who made the show about it."

"But not as well as her," Bashir said.

It wasn't a guess. It was Bashir naming the firewall before anyone could pretend it wasn't there.

"Not as well as her," Jakobus agreed, and there was no contest in it, no apology, just a fact set on the table like the man set every fact on the table — for you to take or leave. "I can tell you it does a man's job. I can't tell you the man's *name.* I can't read the franking. I can't hear the joke."

"The joke," Edith said.

"Bashir says there's always a joke," Jakobus said, and the corner of his mouth moved, the closest he came. "In a language he can feel and I can't. So." He nodded down the dark lane, where the uncle had glanced all night. "We're at the end of what we can do alone. The people who invented this clay — they're not gone. They're up the hill. They're under this vine. They poured you the tea." He looked at the old man, who looked back, steady. "And one of them reads this better than anyone alive, and she's already told you no."

—

He didn't stay for that. He never stayed for the part that wasn't his.

He took the empty glass to the samovar himself, and rinsed it, and set it upside down to drain, and clasped the uncle's hand again in both of his and said the Syriac word back to him that Bashir wouldn't translate, badly, but said, and the old man's eyes went bright and he held the hand a moment longer than the handshake needed. Then Jakobus went out under the awning to where the Beast sat ticking and cooling with its water bag weeping into the warm night, and he checked the bag with the back of two fingers the way you'd check a child's forehead, and he was, for a moment, lit orange under the bazaar lamp — and Edith had the camera up.

She'd uncapped it without anyone seeing. Old reflex. The frame was perfect: the man, the truck, the citadel gold above, the whole story of the desert man arrived in the cradle of writing, the shot that runs the credits, the shot that makes a career.

She held it.

He didn't turn around. He never turned around. But something in the set of his shoulders changed, an awareness with no face on it, and Yusuf watched Edith's finger lie on the shutter and not press it.

She lowered the camera.

"Empty again?" Yusuf said.

"No," Edith said. "Full. It's a beautiful frame." She put the cap back on. "It's just not mine to take." She looked at the uncle's lit doorway, the tea glasses draining, the lane where the woman hadn't come. "I shot this place once before. In oh-three. I shot the sacks coming out of the museum in Baghdad while the whole — while it burned. I've got a Pulitzer-shortlist of somebody else's heritage in a hard drive in London." She said it flat, the way you say the thing you've decided to stop carrying as a secret. "Some shots you take. Some shots you've already taken enough of." She nodded at Jakobus folding himself into the Beast. "He poured the old man's glass out himself. You don't take the picture of the man who washes up after himself."

—

In the Land Cruiser, down the hill, windows down, the city coming up around them again all horn and neon and the smell of grilled fat, Naila held the grey box on her knees and was quiet, and then she said, to the box, the way she said her best things to objects:

"She's going to be right about something we're wrong about. Tomorrow. She'll out-read us. I can feel the shape of it. I don't even mind." A pause. "I usually mind."

"Why don't you mind?" Bashir asked.

"Because the texts are better than the lie." Naila turned her face to the window, to the old town going up behind them into the dark, lamp by lamp. "Every single time we open them up they're better than the lie. The lie says somebody came down and gave it to us. The clay says we made it ourselves, in the mud, to count sheep, and then we used it to write the oldest poem on earth about a man who couldn't beat death and made peace with it." She breathed out. "I don't want the version where it was a gift. I want the version where it was ours. And she's the one who can give us that. So." Her hand was steady on the box now. "She can be right at me all day."

Up front, Jakobus said nothing, which was his way of agreeing.

Yusuf watched the uncle's lane shrink in the wing mirror, the awning light, the vine, the place where a woman who reads cuneiform and speaks the dying language of the men who cut it had not come tonight and would, the uncle's whole braced body had promised it, come tomorrow — to tell these foreigners no, and then, maybe, if they were lucky, and quiet, and didn't film her uncle, to tell them the name of the man kneeling in the clay.

And under everything, under the tea and the heat and the beautiful frame Edith had refused, the other thing sat in Yusuf with no answer and the start of a shape: that somebody had paid good money to put a synth drone behind a Bronze Age receipt and call it a star map, and that the loud lie travelled the world on a streaming service while the quiet truth waited under a grape vine for permission. *Who keeps choosing what we get to see.* He didn't say it out loud. There was nobody to say it to who could fix it.

The Beast went down into the new city, weeping water into the warm dark, and behind them the oldest roof on earth held its six thousand years of lamplight, and somewhere up there, or down here in the diaspora flat she worked from, a woman was deciding exactly how hard to say no.

# Chapter 4 — The One Who Owns the Clay

She came up the lane on foot, and Edith heard her before she saw her — the wrong shoes for the gravel, hard heels, a city sound the lane wasn't built for, and under it the uncle's voice dropping from his after-prayer murmur into something fast and low and Aramaic, a warning maybe, or just *they're here, they're already here, behave*.

Edith had her camera on the strap and her hands nowhere near it. She'd promised herself that. The light at this hour was obscene — the citadel above them going from honey to bronze, the vine throwing its lace across the whitewash — and she kept her hands away from the camera the way an addict keeps her hands off a glass, knowing the want and naming it and sitting on it.

Then the woman came round the corner of the wall and Edith forgot the light entirely.

Dr. Narin Youkhanna was not tall. That was the first wrong thing about the photograph Edith had already half-built in her head — the scholar against the tablets, lit from the side, gravely beautiful, a National Geographic spread. The real woman was small and quick and dressed for a meeting she did not want to have, a grey jacket, a phone in her hand she did not put away, and her eyes went round the courtyard in one pass and counted them. Edith watched her do it. One, the loud one with the notebook clutched to her chest like a hot-water bottle — Naila, who had not slept. Two, Frik, who was already doing the small economy of motions that meant he was working hard at stillness. Three, four, five. Bashir near the wall, very deliberately not at the centre. Yusuf with his hands open and visible, the posture of a man who knew how to walk into rooms that didn't want him. Mira at the edge, watching faces, always watching faces.

And then Narin's gaze found Edith. Found the camera on the strap. Stopped.

Held.

Edith felt it land on her like weather.

"Pala," the uncle said, gentle, a hand half-raised, and there was a current of family in it, *be welcome, sit, these are guests* — and Narin answered him without looking away from Edith, two words, and the uncle subsided.

"You'll forgive my uncle," Narin said, to the courtyard, in English clean as a knife. "He has the hospitality of a saint and the security instinct of a man who thinks the worst people in the world wear bad shoes." A small, controlled smile that did not reach her eyes. "I do not share it. I think the worst people in the world wear very good shoes and carry very good cameras."

Nobody breathed for a second.

"Tea," said the uncle, and fled toward the brazier, and the spell broke into ordinary motion, and the crew did the thing Edith had watched them do at three airports and one museum basement now — they made room. Not a word about it. Naila to the inner corner with her back to the wall and an exit in her sightline, because someone she didn't have to manage had taken that decision for her years ago and it was simply how the furniture got arranged now. Frik between the wall and Yusuf, where his hands had something to face. Mira angled so the light fell on every mouth. The geometry of a found family is a thing you can see if you know to look, and Edith photographed it only with the part of her that could not be confiscated.

Narin sat. She put the phone face-down on the low table, which Edith understood was a concession the size of a country.

"So," she said. "You're the ones who want to film my ancestors as space monkeys."

"No," said Naila.

It came out flat and immediate and a little too loud, and Naila's hand tightened on the notebook, and Edith saw Yusuf shift his weight to be ready and then not need to be, because Narin had turned the full beam of her attention on Naila and Naila, for once, did not flinch from it. They looked at each other, the woman who read the system and the woman who owned the clay, and something passed between them that Edith had no name for and did not try to caption.

"No?" said Narin.

"The opposite." Naila opened the notebook. Her fingers found a page she had clearly found a hundred times. "VA 243. The Berlin tablet. The one they put behind a synth drone and called the solar system carved before telescopes." She turned the book round and pushed it across the table, and on the page in her cramped careful hand was a drawing of the seal, and around it her own arithmetic in three colours. "There are eleven dots. If it's the planets you have to invent a moon and ignore the fact that the sizes are wrong and pretend a Mesopotamian engraver who couldn't see Neptune drew Neptune. And then." Her finger stopped on the cuneiform at the top of the seal, a little box of wedges. "And then there's this. The writing. Which the documentary cropped out. Which I can't read."

Silence.

"I can read the pattern," Naila said, and her voice did something Edith hadn't heard from her — it dropped, it asked. "I can count dots. I can do the base-sixty and prove the three-thousand-six-hundred is a number and not an orbit. I brought all of it." She tapped the notebook. "But I can't read what the man who made the thing wrote on the thing. So I came to the person who can."

For a long moment Narin did not touch the notebook. She looked at it the way you look at a photograph someone has taken of your house without asking.

Then she picked it up.

Edith watched her face and so did Mira — Edith clocked Mira clocking it, the two readers of the unspoken triangulating the same closed mouth from two angles. Narin read down the page. Her thumb moved over Naila's arithmetic, the base-sixty conversion, the place where Naila had written *sar = 3600 = a numeral, a BIG one, NOT a planet's year* and underlined it twice and then, smaller, *(unless?)* and crossed the unless out.

"You crossed out the *unless*," Narin said.

"Because there isn't one," Naila said. "I checked it forty times. Sixty squared. It's how they wrote big. It's beautiful. It's *base sixty,* it's why we have sixty minutes in an hour, it's the oldest piece of arithmetic still alive in your kitchen, and they took that — they took the most elegant counting system on earth — and they sold it as a docking schedule for a planet that doesn't exist." She had run out of breath. She got more. "It made me so angry I couldn't read for a week."

Narin set the notebook down.

When she spoke again the knife had gone out of her voice and left something more tired and much older behind it.

"VA 243," she said, "is a dedication seal. A man owned it. He pressed it into the clay to say *this is mine, this contract is mine, I stand behind these words.*" She turned the notebook back to face Naila and put one fingernail, lacquered, very precise, on the box of wedges at the top. "The line your documentary cut off says he was a scribe. And under it is a name. The name of his god, or his lord, the one he served — the dub-sar, the keeper of the tablet. A man called *Dubsiga.*"

The name dropped into the courtyard like a stone into a well.

"It isn't a star map," Narin said. "It's a business card." A flicker, almost a real smile this time. "A four-thousand-year-old man wanting you to know whose servant he was. And the West looked at a dead man saying *remember me, remember who I served,* and decided he was drawing them a route to Mars."

"I knew it wasn't planets," Naila said, half to herself, fierce and quiet. "But I didn't have the *man*."

"No," said Narin. "You didn't have the man. That's the whole disease." And she looked round the courtyard again, slower this time, and Edith saw the assessment shift by some increment too small to name. "You came a long way to be told you were right about the arithmetic and wrong about everything that matters."

"That," said Yusuf, very gently, from the wall, "is the most encouraging thing anyone's said to us in two thousand miles."

The tea came. The uncle did it himself, would not let anyone help, and Edith watched Jakobus over by the Beast do the thing he did, which was to be exactly the right amount of nowhere — far enough to not be in the family's space, near enough that the uncle, carrying the tray, caught his eye and got a nod, and the nod said *your house, your kin, your call,* and the uncle's shoulders came down half an inch. Narin's eyes went to him once, the tall foreigner by the vehicle who hadn't tried to be in the photograph of this at all, and then came back, and she filed him somewhere Edith couldn't see.
