It was Frik who broke first. It always was, and it was never his fault, and the crew had long ago stopped pretending the small explosions were anything but weather.

"The sar is the orbit," Frik said.

It came out of him whole and bright and wrong, in a voice that wasn't quite his, a documentary-narrator cadence, the thing he'd absorbed off some screen and that surfaced now under the pressure of a room with rules in it. His face did the thing afterward, the small collapse of *that wasn't me, that was the tic,* and his hand came up.

And Narin — Edith was watching, Edith would always be glad she was watching — Narin did not do any of the things people did. She didn't startle or smile-too-kindly or look away to spare him. She turned to Frik like he'd asked an honest question.

"No," she said. "And you know it's no, or it wouldn't have made you angry to say it."

Frik went very still. "Three thousand six hundred years," he said, in his own voice now, low. "The orbit of Nibiru. That's the line. That's the famous line."

"It's a number that means thirty-six hundred," Narin said. "It means it on a receipt for barley. It means it on a list of how many soldiers a king lied about having. It means it the way *a thousand* means a thousand to you — sometimes a count, sometimes just *a lot.* It was never a year of anything. To make it an orbit you have to take a word out of every mouth that ever used it for two thousand years and put it in the mouth of one planet that has to be invisible to work." She lifted her tea. "The tic told the truth. That *is* the famous line. The famous line is a mistranslation with a soundtrack."

Frik laughed — a real one, surprised out of him — and the laugh was permission, and the courtyard breathed.

"That's his job," Bashir said.

He'd been quiet so long Edith had half-forgotten the shape of his voice. He said it from the wall, mild, not pushing in.

"Telling the truth at the wrong moment?" Narin said.

"Telling it before anyone's decided to," Bashir said. "He says the thing the room is being careful around. We've learned to listen for it."

Narin considered him. "And you," she said. "What's yours? You've been arranging your face for ten minutes."

Edith almost flinched for him. But Bashir only inclined his head, and then he did the thing — the thing Edith had seen him do at the airport with the visa officer and once with a furious taxi driver in Erbil traffic, the small surgical kindness of meeting a person inside their own language — and he said it in Aramaic.

Edith didn't have the words. She had the music of it, and she had Narin's face, and Mira's face watching Narin's face, and that was the whole text. Whatever Bashir said was short. It was not fluent — Edith could hear even across the gap that he was reaching, that this was a borrowed tongue worn over the bones of forty others, that he was a man who belonged to no language by blood and had simply gone and *learned* the one this woman bled. He said it gently and a little wrong and entirely on purpose.

And the door that had been a finger's width open came open another inch.

Narin's whole body changed. Not dramatically. The jacket-armour stayed on. But her chin came up and her eyes brightened with what Edith, watching, identified after a second as *grief* — the specific bright grief of hearing your dying mother tongue come out of a stranger's mouth in a courtyard under a foreign occupation's ruins, badly, with love.

She answered him. Longer. Edith caught one word twice — it sounded like *lišana,* and she'd find out later it meant *tongue,* it meant *language,* it was the word at the bottom of everything they were here to do.

Then Narin turned back to the English and the room, and her voice had gone rough at one edge.

"He greets me in the language of the people who wrote the thing your documentary turned into a cartoon," she said. "It's the oldest continuously spoken language in this country. There are villages on the plain north of Mosul where they still say their prayers in it. Were. Were villages." The roughness held. "ISIS came through them in 2014. The ones who didn't die left. The language goes where the people go, and the people are in Sydney now, and Chicago, and a flat in west London where I do most of my work because it isn't safe to do it here. So." She looked at Bashir. "Thank you. For knowing it was worth learning. Most people who come for the tablets never once think the tongue is still warm."

"I think the tongue is the whole thing," Bashir said.

"It is," Narin said. "It's the only thing. Every lie you came here chasing is a translation lie. *Anunnaki* — do you know what it means? Properly?"

Naila opened her notebook. Narin held up a hand, not unkindly, *let me,* and Naila closed it.

"*Anunna,*" Narin said. "The princely ones. The princely offspring. The children of the great god, the high gods, the council of heaven. It's a *title.* It's *Your Royal Highnesses.*" She let it sit. "Over two thousand years — longer than English has existed, twice over — it drifts. By the late period it's come to mean the gods of the underworld, the judges of the dead. A word can travel that far inside one civilisation. And a man in the twentieth century picks it up at the far end of all that travel, after it's already crossed two thousand years and three meanings, and he says: *ah. Astronauts.*" Her mouth twisted. "He didn't even read it. He read *about* it, in translation, in a footnote, and built a solar system."

"Zecharia Sitchin," Yusuf said.

The name came out of him oddly. Edith looked over. Yusuf had been the easy one, the open-handed one, the one who unblocked rooms — and just now there was a thing in his face she hadn't seen, a kind of carefulness, like a man saying a name he'd once said in love.

"You've read him," Narin said. It wasn't a question. She'd heard it too.

"All of it," Yusuf said. "Years ago. The twelfth planet. The whole — " He stopped. "It got me through a bad stretch. I'm not going to pretend it didn't. The idea that there was a *reason.* That somebody made us on purpose and we mattered enough to be made." He turned his tea glass a quarter-turn on the table. "It's a kinder story than the real one. I'll defend that to anybody. It's just not true."

Narin looked at him for a long moment, and the armour came down a notch further, because she was, Edith understood, a person who had built a fortress against exactly this conversation and Yusuf had walked through the wall by being soft.

"The real one is better," Narin said quietly. "When you can bear it. But I grant you it isn't kinder. Not at the start."

"What's the real one," Naila said. Not greedy. Hungry the way Yusuf was hungry, the way they were all a little hungry, the way you get when you've come a long way for something true.

Narin shook her head once. "No," she said. "Not tonight. Not for free. You don't get the clay because you flew here and said please."

And then she turned, finally, fully, deliberately, to Edith.

Edith had known it was coming. She'd watched it not happen for forty minutes, the way you watch a wave hang.

"The camera," Narin said.

"It's off," Edith said.

"I know it's off. I can see the light isn't on. I'm not an idiot." A beat. "Do you know what's in my museum?"

"Which one," Edith said, and regretted it, and meant it.

"Good answer." Narin's voice had gone very flat and very level, the dangerous register. "Baghdad. The National Museum. April 2003. You'd have been — what. Doing your job?"

The courtyard had gone quiet in a different way now. The crew had stopped being a found family arranging furniture and become an audience for something between two women.

"I was there," Edith said.

She heard Mira go still beside her. She felt the crew not look at her, which was its own kind of looking.

"I know," Narin said. "I looked you up. The shoes thing was a joke. The cameras thing wasn't. I know exactly who you are, Edith Vos. I've seen your war." She didn't raise her voice. "There's a photograph of yours. The boy on the museum steps, the empty cases behind him through the doors, the dust. It won things. It's beautiful. It's on a wall in a building in New York where people drink wine and feel something for ninety seconds about the cradle of civilisation."

Edith said nothing. There was nothing. The photograph was real. The wine was real. The award was on a shelf she didn't dust.

"While you were making that beautiful thing," Narin said, "they were carrying the actual cradle out the back in sacks. Cylinder seals by the thousand. Tablets. The Warka Vase — they got the Warka Vase, do you understand, five thousand years old, the first picture humans ever made of a procession, of *society,* and it went out in a sack while a lens made art of the door it went out of. And the thing about your beautiful photograph," and here her voice finally cracked, just once, and re-set, "is that it taught the whole world to grieve the building and never once ask where the things *went.* It made a tragedy you could feel without doing anything. It made the looting *photogenic.* It made it a mood."

Edith found she had no hands. She had hands. They were empty and very far away.

"That's a fair charge," she said, when she could.

"I don't care if it's fair," Narin said. "I care that you're standing in my uncle's courtyard with a camera and my ancestors on the table, and I have to decide whether the next beautiful thing you make is a document or a sale."

"Then let me ask you something," Edith said, and her own voice came out steadier than she'd earned. "What's the difference. For you. What's the difference between the frame that documents and the frame that sells. Because I'll take the rule. I'd rather have your rule than my taste." She made herself say the next part. "My taste is the thing that took the museum picture. I know what my taste does. I'd like to put it down for a while and use yours."

It was the most honest thing she'd said in years and it cost her like surgery.

Narin studied her. The phone stayed face-down on the table. Up the lane a generator coughed and caught somewhere in the new city, and the citadel above them held its bronze a minute longer than seemed possible.

"The difference," Narin said slowly, "is the caption."

She picked up Naila's notebook again, the drawing of VA 243, and turned it so the seal faced Edith.

"You photograph this," she said, "and put under it *cylinder seal, Old Babylonian, dedication of a scribe, name of his lord, see catalogue, here is the museum's free page, go and read it* — that's a document. That serves the dead man. He wanted to be remembered. You remembered him correctly." Her thumb moved to the edge of the page. "You photograph the exact same object and put under it *Was This Carved by Aliens?* — and now you haven't photographed a seal, you've photographed a *price.* Because a tablet that proves spacemen is worth a fortune on the grey market and a tablet that says *Dubsiga's barley account* is worth a coffee. The lie is the markup. The lie is *why the sack is full.* You think the woo and the looting are two stories. They're the same business."

Across the courtyard, Edith heard Yusuf exhale like he'd been hit.

"Say that again," Yusuf said.

Narin looked at him.

"The lie is why the sack is full," Yusuf said. "The mystery isn't decoration. It's the *invoice.*"

"It always was," Narin said. "Make a thing too boring to steal and nobody steals it. Make it scream *secret of the gods* and it walks out the back at midnight and somebody films the door for an award." She set the notebook down a final time and looked, for the first time, weary rather than armed. "That's why I gate it. Not because you're bad people. Because the loud version of my ancestors is worth money to people I will never meet, and the quiet true version — the man saying *remember whose servant I was* — is worth nothing to them, which is exactly why it's the only version worth protecting."

Nobody said anything for a while. The brazier ticked. Mira, who had read the whole of it off mouths and shoulders, signed something small and lopsided toward Edith, and Edith caught half of it and held it, and let it land where it was meant to.

Then Narin stood, and gathered her phone, and looked round the courtyard one more time — the loud one who'd flown across the world to be told she was right and wrong; the truth-teller; the polyglot who'd learned her dying language well enough to greet her badly in it; the watcher of faces; the money man who'd needed the aliens and was letting go in real time at her uncle's table; the photographer who'd offered to put her own eye down. And the tall one by the Beast, who had gathered all of them somehow and then stood out of the picture on purpose.

"Tomorrow," Narin said. "My uncle's roof. Early, before the heat. I'll bring photographs of the real tablets — the British Museum lets you, they put it all online for free, which is the most subversive thing that institution has ever done. And I'll start at the beginning. Not the aliens. The clay. What we were actually made of, in the story they actually told." A pause. "It's worse than spacemen. It's so much braver. You won't sleep after."

"What do we have to do," Naila said.

"Be quiet," Narin said. "Don't film my uncle. And when I tell you the men I'm worried about wear very good shoes — believe me. Because someone made the loud version of my country, and someone keeps it loud, and someone makes very sure the quiet true version never trends." She was at the corner of the wall now, the city sound of her heels ready to take her back down. "I've spent twenty years watching which stories get a soundtrack and which ones disappear from the catalogue between one visit and the next. I have stopped believing it's an accident."

She let that hang in the bronze air, and did not explain it, and Edith felt the back of her neck go cold for reasons that had nothing to do with the dark coming up the lane.

"Who's keeping it loud," Yusuf said, and Edith heard the money in his voice now, the old instinct waking up, the man who could follow a sack.

Narin paused at the wall's edge, half in lamplight, half gone.

"That," she said, "is the better question than aliens. That's the one I'd film." And she went down the lane into the new city, hard heels on the wrong gravel, leaving the oldest roof on earth behind her and a courtyard full of people who had finally, all of them, started looking the right way.

Edith did not lift the camera.

It was the best shot she'd never take, and she let it go, and it felt like the first clean thing she'd done in a long time.

# Chapter 5 — Conveniently Loud

Yusuf knew the room before he knew the city.

He'd never been to Erbil and the city wasn't the room. The room was on his phone, behind a glass face smeared with the courtyard's lamp-oil and the grease of three days of bad falafel, and it was the room he'd worked out of for two years of his life he didn't talk about. Same furniture, even. There was always a long table you could project onto, a coffee machine that cost more than a forklift driver's month, and a slide deck with the word *narrative* in it.

He'd waited until the others were asleep. He sat on the low wall at the edge of the uncle's roof with a glass of tea going cold against his knee, the new-city lights laid out below like a circuit board someone had soldered over the old ground, and he typed the name of Narin's streaming-doc company into a search bar with two thumbs that had stopped being steady about an hour ago.

*Apsû Media.*

Of course. Apsû. The freshwater deep under the world in the old stories, the abyss the gods came out of. Some MBA had paid a consultant for that. They always named themselves after the thing they were strip-mining. He'd worked for a logistics firm once called *Restitution*.

He pulled the corporate site. Slick. Of course it was slick — that was the whole engine. A landing video that opened on a slow drone shot over a desert at dawn, a voice with the gravel sanded off it, *some questions are older than history,* and a montage: a cuneiform tablet rotating in a black void, lit like a watch in a watch advert, the wedges catching highlight as it turned. A man's hands — gloved, white cotton — laying it on velvet. A title card. *THE FIRST CONTACT — coming this autumn.*

Then the part that made the old part of him go very quiet and very awake at the same time.

A press page. A grants page. A *partnerships* page with logos. And buried in the footer, in the legal grey that nobody reads because nobody is supposed to, a line about *cultural-asset facilitation services* and a sister entity registered three jurisdictions away.

He knew that line. He had *written* lines like that line.

He sat back. Below him a generator coughed somewhere and steadied. The cold tea had a skin on it. He didn't drink it.

It was Naila who found him, which surprised him, because Naila didn't generally come looking. She came up the roof stair without announcing herself and stood at the lip of the light with her notebook held against her sternum the way she held it when she'd been thinking too hard to sleep.

"You're doing money," she said. Not a question. She'd clocked his posture from across a courtyard once and named the precise figure he was anxious about within fifty pounds; she read him the way she read a ledger that had been balanced too neatly.

"I'm doing money," Yusuf said.

She came and sat, not close, an arm's length down the wall, which from Naila was an embrace. She set the notebook on her knees. On the open page she'd drawn the VA 243 seal from memory, the figures, the eleven dots, and beside it a column of base-sixty arithmetic, and beside *that*, underlined twice, she'd written *NOT AN ORBIT* in block capitals with the satisfaction of a woman who had personally executed a lie.

"Narin said the men wear good shoes," she said. "I keep trying to fit it to the tablet. It doesn't fit the tablet. The tablet's solved. Dubsiga's the owner, the dots are stars but the arrangement is decorative, the whole thing's a dedication. There's no mystery in the object. So the worry can't be the object." She turned her face to him, that level grey look. "So what's the worry."

"The worry's not what it *says*," Yusuf said. "The worry's where it *goes.*"

She waited. She was very good at waiting; it was one of the few social moves she trusted, and on him, tonight, it worked like a key.

"Okay." He set the phone down between them, screen up, the rotating tablet still turning in its expensive void. "I'm going to tell you how a thing like this actually runs, because I was inside one. Not this one. One like it. You ready?"

"I prefer the real one to the polite one," she said.

"Right. Cradle of writing, yeah? Best clay in the world's been coming out of this ground for four thousand years. Most of it boring. *Gloriously* boring. Three sheep delivered to the temple of Inanna, receipt acknowledged, sealed by Lu-Ningirsu. A school exercise where some kid copied out a proverb forty times and got the third line wrong. Beer rations. So much beer." He could hear something coming alive in his own voice, the part of him that had loved this before he'd learned what he was loving it *for.* "A tablet like that — a real one, properly out of the ground — on the legal market it's worth, what. A few hundred quid? If it's clean. Most aren't clean. Most can't *be* clean, because the only way they get here from there is a chain of hands that all need deniability, and deniability has a price, and the price eats the few hundred quid."

"So a boring true tablet isn't worth moving," Naila said slowly. He watched her start to assemble it.

"A boring true tablet is a *liability,*" Yusuf said. "It's heavy, it's traceable, it's illegal, and it's worth less than the bribe you paid the man at the second border. The math is terrible. I sat in rooms and watched men look at genuine cradle-of-civilisation school texts and go *ugh, paperwork,* like it was a parking ticket." He picked the phone back up, turned it so the tablet caught the light again. "Now. Same tablet. Same beer receipt. Except this time, the man with the good shoes says — out loud, on a podcast, in a doc with a drone shot — *this object may rewrite human history.* This object has *symbols modern science can't explain.* This object proves we were *visited.*"

"It doesn't," Naila said. "It says Lu-Ningirsu got his sheep."

"It says Lu-Ningirsu got his sheep," Yusuf agreed. "But nobody who'll pay for it can read it. And here's the thing you have to actually sit in, because it's ugly: *nobody who could read it will ever be in the room.* You've made the object worth a hundred times more, and you've made it a hundred times *safer* to move, with the exact same sentence. Because now it's not a smuggled artefact. Now it's *evidence.* Now it's a *relic.* Now a rich man in a city I won't name buys it for a number that makes the border bribes look like the tea money, and he buys it *because* of the story, and the story is the thing that means he will never, ever hand it to a woman like Narin who'd take one look and say *Dubsiga, your servant,* and price-crash his miracle back down to beer."

Naila had gone very still in the way she went still when a pattern locked. She picked up her pen. She didn't write anything. She just held it over the page like she needed something in her hand to think with.

"The story is the markup," she said.

"The story is the markup." He'd never heard it put that clean. "You said it better than the slide deck did."

"And the truth—" She stopped. He could see her run it both directions, the way she ran everything both directions before she'd let herself trust it. "The truth is what makes it *cheap* again. The truth is the thing that empties the value out of the crate."

"The truth," Yusuf said, "is what turns the relic back into a parking ticket. So the truth is the enemy. Not because they want the aliens to be real—" and this was the part he'd been turning over on the wall for an hour, the part that had made his hands stop being steady, "—they don't care if the aliens are real. I never met a single one who *believed* it. That's the part nobody gets. They're not cranks. They're not even liars, technically; they never *say* it's true, they say *could it be? Some experts believe,* they keep it a *question.* They need the question loud. A loud question is a market. A loud question keeps the catalogue full of mysteries and empty of beer receipts. And a loud question keeps people like us—" he tipped his head down the roof toward the dark rooms where the others slept "—looking at the wrong tablet."

The generator below them cut out. For a second there was only the new city's hum and, further off and lower, the older sound, the one Yusuf had been half-hearing all night without naming it — water somewhere, a drip, the Beast's canvas bag weeping against the truck's flank in the lane, Jakobus's desert habit of keeping water cool by letting it cry through the cloth. A sound that belonged two thousand miles south and had come up the road anyway, the way the man had, the way the whole crew had.

"There's a worse part," Yusuf said. "You want the worse part?"

"Always," Naila said.

"The loud version's only half the cover." He swiped the doc page closed and the rotating tablet died. The roof got darker. "The other half is *you.* Or — people like you and me before we got here. The skeptic. The smart one in the comments who watches the drone shot and goes, *oh come on, it's all just myth, it's a fairy tale, who cares.* You know what that man is, to a trafficker? He's the *best customer in the world.* Because he's just told everyone it doesn't matter. He's just announced to the room that the clay is worthless mumbo-jumbo not worth protecting. The believer makes the price go up and the skeptic makes the *guarding* go down, and between the two of them they walk a four-thousand-year-old object out the back of a country that's already been bled white, and the only person in the entire transaction who actually *loses* — who loses her ancestors, her language, her dead — is the woman who can read it. Who isn't invited. Who they keep *very* sure isn't invited."

He hadn't meant to say it with that much in it. His voice had thickened on the last of it and he heard the thickening and didn't like it, because the thickening was the part that was about him, and this wasn't supposed to be about him.

Naila was looking at the dark where the city was.

"You sound like you miss it," she said. Flat. Not cruel. Just true, the way she was true, with no padding around it.

He breathed out. The tea was undrinkable. He drank it anyway, cold and skinned and bitter.

"I miss who I got to be when I believed the cover," he said. "Not the second one. The first one. The believer." He turned the empty glass in his hands. "You want to know why I came on this trip? Everyone thinks it's because I read the manifests, I follow the money, I'm useful. And I am. But I came because some part of me still — God, this is embarrassing. Some part of me still *wanted it to be true.* The big version. The visitors. The twelfth planet. I read Sitchin at nineteen on a night shift in a warehouse outside Mosul, moving boxes I wasn't supposed to ask about, and that book — it was a *door,* Naila. It was a door out of the warehouse. It said: you, specifically you, are not an accident of mud and bad luck and a war you didn't start. You were *made.* On purpose. *Wanted.* By something out among the stars that bent down and chose. That's — that's not nothing. To a kid in a warehouse, that's not nothing at all."

Naila wrote something now. One line. He didn't look.

"And then I got good at moving the boxes," he said, "and one day I understood that the men paying me to move them needed me to believe the door so I wouldn't ask what was actually in the crate. And the door I needed to walk through and the lie they needed me asleep behind were the *same door.* That's the part I can't—" He stopped. "They built my way out of the warehouse out of the same lumber they used to rob the warehouse. And I helped."

The water wept against the canvas in the lane. Somewhere a dog. Somewhere, faint, music — Jakobus, awake, almost certainly, the radio in the Beast turned low to something with a steady spine to it, the metronome he kept the dark off with.

"Narin's going to think we're them," Naila said.

"Narin already thinks we're them," Yusuf said. "She'd be an idiot not to, and she's not an idiot, she's the smartest person I've met since I stopped being one of them. She's seen this exact crew before. Western strangers, a camera, a notebook, a fixer who knows too much about logistics—" he laughed, no humour in it "—a *man no one can photograph* — and a beautiful theory about her ancestors. She's been here. The version of us she's met before walked off with the contents of her museum in 2003. In sacks. While the building burned. Edith's got pictures of it. *Edith was there.* You think Narin doesn't know that? You think she didn't clock the camera the first second?"

"Edith didn't take the museum's pictures," Naila said. "Edith took pictures *of* the museum being taken."

"Same lens, far as Narin's concerned, until proven otherwise. The witness and the thief use the same machine. That's Edith's whole problem. That's why she didn't lift the camera tonight." He set the glass down on the wall. "And it's why she was right not to."

Naila was quiet. He could feel her doing the thing, fitting it against the others, the great clicking ledger behind her eyes balancing the trip the way she'd balanced his anxiety to fifty quid. When she spoke again it was slower, and there was an edge in it he didn't expect.

"Apsû Media has a partnerships page," she said.

"You saw that."

"I saw it over your shoulder ten minutes ago." Of course she had. "Logos. One of them I know. A foundation that funds digitisation projects. *Open access. Heritage for everyone.* They put museum collections online. Tablets. Photographs. Catalogue numbers." She turned the notebook toward him in the dark, though he couldn't read it. "I use their database. Everyone does. It's how I checked Dubsiga while Narin was still on the wall — I pulled the seal's record, the publication history, the line that says it's a personal dedication. It's a good database. It's *free.*"

"So?"

"So Narin said something." Naila's voice had gone careful, the way it went when she was holding a pattern she didn't want to be true. "On the wall. She said she's watched stories disappear *from the catalogue between one visit and the next.* The quiet true ones. The ones that say *beer rations.* She said she's stopped believing it's an accident."

The roof was very dark now. Below them the new city pulsed its circuit-board pulse, and under it the old ground held its four thousand years of receipts and proverbs and the names of men who'd wanted to be remembered enough to press them into wet clay before they died, and Yusuf felt the floor of the thing he'd thought he understood tilt very slightly, just enough.

"You're saying," he said slowly, "it's not just that they make the loud ones loud."

"I'm saying I can't prove that anyone makes the quiet ones quiet." Naila said it fast, the disclaimer of a woman who had been burned for pattern-matching past the evidence and would not do it again. "I'm an arithmetic person. I count what's there. I don't count what's missing, because *missing* isn't a number, missing is a story, and stories are where the lying lives." She held the notebook tighter. "But Narin counts it. Narin's spent twenty years watching the catalogue. She says the entries thin out. She says the disproving ones, the ones that price-crash a miracle, those are the ones that go *under review* and don't come back. And she's not an arithmetic person either. She's a *language* person. She reads what got written *and* she reads what got—"

She stopped.

"What got un-written," Yusuf said.

"I don't have a word for it," Naila admitted, and from her that was nearly a confession of helplessness, this woman who had a base-sixty column for everything. "There isn't a number for a tablet that used to be in the catalogue and isn't. There's just a gap. And a gap could be a clerk. A gap could be a server migration. A gap could be nothing." She breathed. "Or a gap could be a person with very good shoes who understands that you don't have to *burn* the truth anymore. You don't need sacks and a fire. You just need to make sure the truth is never the version with the soundtrack, and you need to be patient, and you need to control which door the world walks through."

Yusuf looked at the dead black phone screen with its smear of lamp-oil and his own face faintly ghosted in it.

He thought about the rooms he'd worked in. He thought about how nobody in them had ever once said anything you could put in a court. How they'd never needed to. How the whole genius of it was that no single hand ever did a single thing you could point at — the believer believed for free, the skeptic shrugged for free, the database had a clerk who was probably just overworked, the doc company kept everything a *question,* and somewhere a long way back from all of it, where there were no fingerprints because there were no fingers, the value moved and the clay moved and the only loud thing in the whole arrangement was the one thing that wasn't true.

"I want it to be a clerk," he said.

"I know," Naila said.

"I want it to be a server migration and an overworked clerk and Narin being twenty years tired and seeing a shape in noise, because the other thing is too big and too quiet and I've watched what too-quiet looks like up close, and I would rather it be a clerk."

"I know," Naila said again. And then, after a moment, because she was Naila and she would give him the true thing over the kind thing every time and that was the kindest thing about her: "But you don't think it's a clerk."

Below, in the lane, the canvas water bag wept its slow tears against the Beast's flank, and the low radio kept its steady spine, and out past the parapet the new city's lights ran their bright loud current over the dark of the old, where the receipts slept.

"No," Yusuf said. "I think someone made the loud version of her country. And I think someone keeps it loud. And I think the thing they're keeping loud—" he heard the shape of it as he said it, the awful economy of it, the markup and the cover folded into one perfect sentence "—is a question they don't even *want* answered, because the question's the asset. The answer crashes the price. The answer's a parking ticket. The answer's *Dubsiga, your servant.*"

He thought about the boy on the night shift outside Mosul who'd needed the door so badly. He thought about how the people who'd robbed him had needed the same boy to keep loving the door. And he thought, with a kind of vertigo, that the door had never once been locked from the inside — that it had been held open *for* him, all those years, by men in good shoes who wanted exactly one thing from a kid who needed the stars to have made him on purpose, which was for him to keep his eyes up so they could empty the room.

Naila closed her notebook. She stood. She didn't put a hand on his shoulder, because she didn't do that, but she stood near him a moment longer than her arithmetic required, which was its own grammar.

"Tomorrow," she said, "Narin reads us the real one. The clay version. The one that out-wonders the fiction." She looked down at the lane, at the truck, at the slow-weeping water and the man no light ever quite held. "We should hear it before anyone makes it quiet."

"Yeah," Yusuf said.

She went down the stair. Yusuf stayed on the wall a while longer with the cold dregs and the dead phone and the city laid out below him like a board someone else had wired, and he thought: *if it wasn't aliens, then who, and why did a boy in a warehouse need it so badly that the men robbing him could build his only exit out of it.*

He didn't have the answer. That wasn't his to give. The language wasn't his and the meaning wasn't his and the grief, God knew, was not his — it belonged to the woman who'd gone down the lane in the wrong heels on the wrong gravel, who'd left the oldest roof on earth behind her and told them all, finally, to look the right way.

But the money was his. The grift was his. He'd lived in its rooms and he knew its furniture and he knew, now, sitting on a roof over a city he'd never see again, exactly what shape the hand had to be that could turn a beer receipt into a relic and a relic into a parking ticket with the same loud, patient, fingerprintless sentence.

He just didn't know whose hand it was.

He poured the cold tea over the parapet, into the dark, where it fell without a sound he could hear over the city, and went down to sleep among the others, badly, the way he slept now that he'd stopped believing the door.

# Chapter 6 — What the Word Doesn't Mean

Frik woke before the muezzin, which in Erbil meant before everything. He lay on the thin guest-room mattress and let the call come up out of the city the way it had come up every dawn since they'd landed — first one minaret, then a second leaning on it half a beat late, then the whole bowl of the old town singing slightly out of tune with itself, and he didn't have the words for any of it, but he had the shape, and the shape held him still. That was the gift nobody believed about him. The tics ran on noise and clenched on silence and went quiet, sometimes, inside a rhythm large enough to forgive a man for being one more arrhythmia in it.

Then his right shoulder snapped up to his ear, twice, hard, and the spell broke, and he was just a man on a floor in a country that hadn't asked for him.

He got up. He drank the water from the clay jug Narin's uncle kept on the sill, which tasted of the jug, and he went out into the courtyard where the light was the colour of weak tea and Jakobus was already crouched by the front wheel of the Beast with the canvas water bag weeping its slow dark stain into the dust beneath it, doing whatever he did to a vehicle that was older than most of them and ran better. The man didn't look up. He lifted two fingers off the wheel rim, which was hello, and went back to it. Somewhere in the truck a cassette was playing something with a marimba in it, very low, keeping time with nothing.

"Morning," Frik said, and then, because the morning quiet was too clean and his throat needed the load off, "*morning, morning,*" twice more, lighter, getting it out.

Jakobus tipped his head. The cayenne tin came out of his shirt pocket, was offered, was waved off, went back. None of it remarked on. That was the thing about the truck and the man at it: nothing Frik did out here was a problem to be solved. It was just weather. The crew was the first room he'd ever been in where his body wasn't the most interesting thing about him, and he'd have crawled across the bazaar on the broken glass of his own teeth before he'd say that out loud.

---

They worked in the long room off the courtyard, the one Narin's uncle used for storing the good rugs and the dates and a forty-year-run of agricultural-ministry calendars he would not throw away. Narin had cleared the long table. On it she'd laid, side by side, three things: a printout of VA 243, big, the famous one, the seal with the little gods and the little circle of dots that a man in New York had once called the solar system; a fat reference grammar with a cracked spine; and her own laptop, open to a photograph of an actual tablet, dun-coloured, fingernail-marked, with the cuneiform standing up off it in the slant light like a field of struck matches.

Naila stood at the head of the table with her arms folded close, not because she was cold but because the room had six people in it and the door was at her back and that arithmetic was always running. Yusuf had put himself by the door without being asked. He did that now. He'd done it since the roof.

"Show me the orbit," Narin said.

She said it without heat. She said it the way a teacher says a thing she has said before and will say again and would, if she were honest, like to stop having to say.

"The sar." She tapped the printout, the little dots. "This is the place people start. Three thousand six hundred. The orbit of the planet that comes back every three thousand six hundred years. So." She slid the grammar across to Bashir, who caught it without looking, the way he caught everything, and then she pulled the laptop toward Naila instead. "You read numbers. Read me that one."

Naila came off the wall. The table was a thing to do with her hands and her eyes and it pulled her in past the count of the doors. She bent over the screen.

"Sexagesimal," she said. "Base sixty." Her finger hovered, not touching. "It's not a quantity that means *years*. It's a digit in a place-value system." She looked up. "Sixty squared. Thirty-six hundred is just — it's *the round number*. It's how a kid in a scribal school writes a big tidy figure, the way we'd write a thousand or a million. It turns up everywhere. Loan periods. Grain. Gods reigning before the Flood — this king ruled a *sar*, this one ten *sars*, and they didn't mean he was on the throne for thirty-six thousand years, they meant *a very long time, a clean impressive number.*" She straightened. "You can't take a digit and bolt a telescope to it. It's a *ten*. It's a *ten* with a hat on."

Edith had her camera up — quiet, low, the lens an extension of her cheekbone, the way she held it when she'd already asked. She'd asked Narin yesterday, formally, in the courtyard, *may I photograph the work and not the room,* and Narin had looked at her for a long time and said *the work, yes. Not my uncle. Not the calendars,* and something had been agreed there that the rest of them only half saw. So now Edith framed the table and the tablet and Naila's clean unjewelled hands and she did not frame Narin's face, and the not-framing was its own kind of statement, made over and over, a hundred small refusals an hour.

"Now," Narin said. "Nibiru."

She let the word sit. It was a word the whole world thought it owned now — doom-planet, hidden planet, the planet the network's other channel ran a special on every eighteen months, *Nibiru: Is It Coming?*, with a render of a brown gas giant looming over a screaming coastline.

"It's a real word," Narin said. "That's the part people don't expect. It is *in the texts.* Right here." She pulled up a column of glyphs. "*Né-bé-ru.* From the verb *to cross.* It's the crossing-place. The ford. Where you ford a river, where the ferry runs — that's a *nibiru.*" She looked round at them. "And in the astronomical texts, when a planet reaches the point in the sky where it *crosses* — its station, the place it turns — they call that the *nibiru* of that planet. It's a *position.* Anything can have one." A small, dry pause. "The one they wrote about most, the *nibiru* that became a proper name almost, the king of the crossing-places —"

"Jupiter," Bashir said. He'd looked up from the grammar. There was a particular pleasure on his face that Frik had learned to watch for, the pleasure of a man who'd just felt a word click home in a slot it had been cut for three thousand years ago. "It's Jupiter."

"It's Jupiter." Narin almost smiled. "Marduk's planet. The biggest light that isn't the moon or the morning star. They watched it cross every year. *Every year.* You can watch it tonight, off the citadel wall, if the haze lifts. It is the least hidden object in that sky." She spread her hand flat on the table. "It was never a twelfth planet. It was never coming for us. And it was *never*—" and here she touched the printout of VA 243, the little gods, the little dots — "*never once, in any text, in any list, attached to these.*"

"The Anunnaki," Naila said.

"The Anunnaki." Narin took her hand back off the seal. "Different word. Different file. Different shelf. The man who made all this famous put the planet and the gods in the same sentence and the sentence has been travelling under its own power for fifty years. But the *clay* never put them together. He did."

---

It went very quiet in the long room. Frik felt the quiet land on him the way it always did, a pressure, a thing his nervous system would have to pay for, and he held still and breathed through it and watched the dust turn in the slant of light over the table.

Because here was the floor tilting. He could feel it tilt. He'd come in through the front door like everybody did — he'd watched the network's stuff, the gold-mining gods, the bred-from-apes, the whole gorgeous machine of it — and now a woman in a rug-room in northern Iraq had taken a screwdriver to it in its own language, gently, professionally, the way you take apart a thing you respect even when it's wrong, and the planet was just Jupiter and the orbit was just a *ten with a hat on*, and the question that came up through the hole where the aliens used to be wasn't *were they real* anymore.

It was: *then who, and why did we want it so badly.*

"VA 243," Yusuf said, from the door. His voice was careful. "The solar system seal. The dots. If it's not — what is it."

Narin pulled the printout to the centre. "It's a dedication."

She said it so plainly that Frik watched it not land, watched the whole alien architecture of the thing fail to fall just because a fact had been spoken, because facts didn't work like that, never had.

"There's an inscription on it," Narin said. "Two lines of cuneiform. People who talk about the solar system never read the two lines, because the two lines are the boring part, and the boring part is the whole point." Her finger moved along it. "*Dub-si-ga.* A name. A man's name, or a title — a high official, a scribe-prince. And then: *your servant.* Somebody had this seal cut and dedicated to a person named Dubsiga. *To Dubsiga, from your servant.* That's it. That's what it says. It's a business card. It's a man telling another man, *I'm yours, here's my mark.*" She looked up and her face had gone, for a moment, entirely undefended. "And the little dots aren't planets to scale. There's no astronomy in the proportions, there's no orbit, the spacing is wrong for the solar system and right for *decoration.* It's a star and the moon and stars, the way you and I would draw a sky. The kind of sky a child draws. The kind a god stands under." She sat back. "It's a man, kneeling, with his mark, in front of a god, under stars. It's the most ordinary devout thing in the entire collection. That's why nobody photographs the inscription."

And it was at that moment — Frik would think about this for a long time, about the timing of it, the way his body knew before he did — that the man on the floor near the door said it.

There was a person in the room nobody had named yet. Apsû had sent a producer-fixer with them, a young man called Deniz, fluent and bright and embedded, who'd been quiet and useful all morning and who was, Frik understood now, *of the network*, in the soft way the network was of everything, a clean-handed cheerful young man who believed, who actually believed, who'd grown up on the channel and the renders and the looming brown planet. And Deniz had been getting more and more wound through Narin's whole patient demolition, leaning forward, and now he couldn't hold it.

"Okay but," Deniz said, "but the sar — the thirty-six hundred — that *is* the orbit, though, right, because the King List, the reigns, they match the —"

"The sar is the *orbit*—" Frik said.

It came out of him entire and finished, in Deniz's own cadence, in Deniz's own rising hopeful pitch, the echo arriving a quarter-second behind the original and landing dead on top of it like a second foot in the same footprint — *the sar is the ORBIT* — and the whole room heard it for what it was, which was the sentence held up to the light, the sentence detached from the man and rotated once in the air so everyone could see clean through it.

The echolalia. It took whatever was loudest and gave it back. And what had been loudest, just then, was an honest man reaching for the thing he needed to be true, and Frik's nervous system had photographed the reach and printed it large.

Nobody laughed. That was the mercy of this crew, the thing Frik had crossed a continent to find — nobody laughed, and nobody hushed, and nobody looked at *him*. They looked at the sentence, hanging there, *the sar is the orbit*, with the question mark filed off it by the flatness of the repetition, so that it stopped being a hope and became an X-ray of a hope, and you could see in it exactly where the bone was broken.

Deniz heard it. You could see him hear it. His mouth was still half-open on his own version of it and there was his own want, played back to him in another man's broken voice, with all the *but* and the *though* and the *right?* burned off, just the naked claim, *the sar is the orbit*, and it sounded — out there in the air, separated from him — like exactly what it was. Like a thing somebody needed. Not a thing that was so.

"Sorry," Frik said. His shoulder jerked. "Sorry. It does that. It grabs—" his head ducked, twice — "grabs the loud one."

"No," Yusuf said quietly, from the door. "Don't be sorry."

Because Yusuf had gone very still. Frik watched him, because Frik watched the men by doors. And what was on Yusuf's face wasn't triumph and wasn't the cheap-scorn-on-the-believer thing the network would have shot in close-up — it was *recognition,* the bad soft kind, the kind a man wears when he sees his own old face on a stranger.

"It's a good thing to want," Yusuf said. He was talking to Deniz now, but he wasn't, really; the room understood that he wasn't, really. "That's the part nobody says. It's a *good* thing to want. You want it to be bigger than rations and tax-lists and a man named Dubsiga leaving his card. You want somebody to have come down. You want it to mean we were *chosen* — designed, planted, watched. You want the dust to not be the whole story." He turned the dead phone over in his hand, a habit now, a worry-stone. "I wanted it too. I wanted it like a door. You're in some warehouse job or some — some ordinary nothing life, and the alien story's the one door in the wall, and you'd be a fool not to want the door." His jaw worked. "I'm not laughing at you. I spent ten years walking through that door. It costs more than you think and it doesn't go anywhere, but God, it's the prettiest door in the building."

Deniz didn't say anything. He looked at the printout, at the little kneeling man and his little card and his little homemade sky, and something went out of his shoulders that wasn't faith exactly — Frik wouldn't have called it faith, faith wasn't being touched here, that wasn't what this room was for — but was the *flattery* going out of it. The thing Yusuf had named. The story had told Deniz he was chosen and the clay was telling him he was a man, made of clay, like the man on the seal, kneeling under a sky he'd drawn himself.

Mira had been at the far end of the table the whole time, where she could see every mouth, her chin tipped, reading. She'd missed none of it — Frik knew she'd missed none of it, she got more of a room than any of them, she got the part under the words. Now she lifted her hands and signed something to Bashir, who'd been learning, badly, eagerly. He frowned, parsing, then said it for her, low, to the table:

"She says — the want is the real subject." He watched her hands again. "She says nobody's wrong here. She says—" his eyes flicked, careful, getting it right — "the only liar in the room is the one who *sells* the want."

And that landed in Frik's chest harder than the orbit had. Because the want wasn't the lie. The want was the most human thing on the table. The lie was somebody monetising it.

---

They broke after that. Narin's uncle came through with the tea on a dented tray, the glasses too hot to hold, and outside in the courtyard Jakobus had got the second water bag rigged over the awning and the shade under it was ten degrees cooler than the lane and they drifted to it the way the crew always drifted to whatever shade the man had built without being asked. He poured nobody's tea. He'd taught them, somewhere back along the road none of them had been on with him, that you let a man pour his own. He'd taken his shades off in the shade. He said, to Narin's uncle, two careful words in Kurdish, badly pitched, and the old man laughed and corrected him and Jakobus said them again, worse, and the old man laughed harder and it was, Frik understood, a thing the man did on purpose — got the word wrong so the local could own being right. *Sawubona with the mouth,* Bashir had called it once. *He greets you by handing you the win.*

Naila stood at the edge of the awning's shade with her tea, near the open arch of the courtyard, where the doors were the whole world and not just a wall at her back, and Frik went and stood near her, not too near, and said nothing, which was the company she could take.

"It's cleaner than the lie," she said, after a while, not to him exactly. "The real one. The clay one." She was looking at nothing, at the angle of the citadel up the hill. "The lie says we're special. The clay says we're tired. Made to be tired. Made out of mud and a dead god's blood to do the gods' work so the gods could rest." She turned her glass. "That's a worse story and a better one. I keep doing the arithmetic on it and it keeps coming out *braver.*" She glanced at him, fast, sideways, the way warmth came off her, never head-on. "You did a good thing in there."

"Doesn't feel like a thing I *do,*" Frik said. "Feels like a thing that does me."

"Same," Naila said, which from her was practically an embrace, and they stood in the shade and didn't look at each other and were, both of them, fine.

---

It was Yusuf, in the end, who asked the question.

He'd helped Narin's uncle carry the empty tray back and he came out into the white lane light wiping his hands and he stopped in front of Narin where she stood under the awning with her own tea cooling, and he asked it the way you ask a thing you've earned the right to ask, which is humbly.

"So what were they," he said. "If they weren't planets and they weren't astronauts and the orbit's a ten with a hat on. The Anunnaki. What *were* they. What were they *for.*"

It was Jakobus's question, really. *What is it for.* It had been hanging over the whole table since London. But it wasn't Jakobus's to answer and the man knew it; he didn't even look up from the strap he was checking. The question went to the woman it belonged to, the way everything in this country was supposed to and so rarely did, and Frik watched the network's whole machine — the renders, the looming planet, the gold-mining gods, the fifty years of a sentence travelling under its own power — wait, in that hot lane, on what an Iraqi Assyriologist would do with her own ancestors' word.

Narin didn't answer.

She set her tea down on the low wall. She went back into the long room, and they heard her moving among her uncle's shelves, and she came back out with a different printout — not VA 243, not the famous one, nothing the network had ever sold. A column of dun clay, struck-match glyphs, older-looking, harder.

"Anunnaki," she said, "is not a what. It's a *who*, and the who *moves.* Over two thousand years the word slides under your feet like wet shale, and where it ends up is nothing like where it starts, and the slide is the whole story, and *nobody* who's selling you a planet has ever once watched it slide." She held the new sheet against her chest, not yet handing it over. "You want to know what they were for. You're asking the wrong tense. Ask me what the word *did.* Ask me what it *became.*"

She turned the sheet around.

The light came across it and the glyphs stood up off the clay, and at the top of the column, set apart, a single word waited to be read — older than the King List, older than the seal, older than the man named Dubsiga and his little homemade sky.

"Start here," Narin said. "*The princely offspring.* That's where it begins."

She held the page out flat, into the white lane light, and waited for them to come and read it with her.

# Chapter 7 — The Princely Offspring

Bashir watched her hands.

Not the page — the page he could read for himself, or read enough of, the dun column with its struck-match wedges, the single word set apart at the top like a name on a door. He watched Narin's hands hold it, because hands told him things before mouths did, and her hands were doing the thing he did at borders: holding still on purpose, the stillness costing something.

The lane behind her uncle's house ran narrow and white between two walls, and the awning over the low bench cut the heat into shade and not-shade in a hard line, and the not-shade landed on the printout and made the glyphs lift. Yusuf had pulled a plastic stool from somewhere. Naila stood where she could see the whole lane and both ends of it, which Bashir had learned not to mention. Mira had her back to the wall and her eyes on Narin's mouth. Edith's camera sat on the bench beside her with the lens cap on, which was its own kind of sentence. Frik bounced once on his heels and went still.

"The princely offspring," Narin said again. "Say it the Sumerian way and you'll forget the planet exists. *A-nun-na.* The seed of An. An is the sky — the sky-as-a-person, the oldest of the old — and the *nun* is the prince, the noble, the high-born. So: the high-born seed of the sky. The aristocracy of the gods." She tipped the page so the light ran down it. "Their children. Their grandchildren. The ones who got the good seats."

"Not a crew," Naila said. It wasn't a question. She'd been turning something over since the tea. "A *family.*"

"A dynasty." Narin let that sit. "And here's the part nobody selling you a spaceship has ever once stayed in the library long enough to find. The word doesn't *hold still.*"

Bashir felt it land in his chest before his head caught up — the small electric thing that happened when someone was about to do the only trick he truly loved, which was to take a word and walk it through time and show you it had legs.

"Go on," he said. Quietly. Just enough to half-open the door.

She glanced at him. Something in her shoulders gave a quarter-inch.

"In the oldest texts," she said, "third millennium, the Anunnaki are *everything.* They are the great gods, the deciders. There's a phrase — they fix the *me*, the destinies, the divine order of things. The flood is coming or it isn't because the Anunnaki sit in council and decide. They are the supreme court of the cosmos. The board." She turned the sheet a few degrees. "You want a word for power, that's the word. They don't dig gold. They sit. They rule. People down here in the mud are terrified of them in the good way — the way you're terrified of weather and harvest and the river."

"And later?" Yusuf had leaned forward on the stool. Bashir clocked it — Yusuf always leaned in at exactly the moment a story started doing something he hadn't paid for.

"Later." Narin's mouth did something complicated. "Centuries pass. Empires fall and other empires write over them. Akkadian comes up under Sumerian like a second language stitched into the first. And there's another bunch of gods running around the texts by now — the *Igigi.*"

"The Igigi," Bashir repeated. The word had a gear in it. *Igigu.* The plural sat in his mouth like something tired.

"The junior gods. The working gods. And in some of the texts the line between Anunnaki and Igigi gets drawn one way, and in others the other way, because — and write this down, Edith, because this is the thing —" she didn't look up to check whether Edith was writing; she just trusted it the way you trust a wall is behind you — "because it was *never one fixed system.* It was a theology. A living one. Two thousand years of priests in twenty cities arguing about who outranked whom. The names migrate. The jobs migrate. In the late texts — first millennium, the period when half the famous tablets in the British Museum were actually copied — the Anunnaki have *slid all the way down.* They're not the high court of heaven anymore."

She stopped.

Bashir watched her decide whether to give it to them. The gating again — the firewall she ran on access, on her own ancestors' word, the one Yusuf had bumped into twice and learned to wait out.

"Where do they end up?" Mira signed it and said it, voice flat and careful the way she shaped the words she couldn't hear, and the question had no patience in it, only weight.

Narin answered her face-on, which Bashir noticed, because almost nobody did.

"They become," Narin said, "the *judges of the dead.* The Anunnaki of the underworld. The grey ones who sit in the dark and weigh you when you go down. By the late period that's mostly what the word means. Not the lords of destiny up in the bright. The bailiffs of the pit." She let the page drop a little. "Two thousand years. From the board of directors of the universe to the parole panel in hell. The same word. The whole way down."

Nobody said anything for a second.

Then Naila said, "That's a *drift.*" And there was hunger in it, and Bashir knew that hunger — it was the same one he got, only she got it in shapes and he got it in sounds. "That's a — the word's not a thing, it's a *trajectory.* You'd plot it. Early, middle, late, and the meaning's a curve, it's *sliding,* and if you—" she stopped herself, the way she did, the brakes coming on visibly, and Bashir watched her choose to finish anyway, smaller — "if you only had one point on the curve you could call it anything you wanted."

"Yes," Narin said. Just that. And she looked at Naila for a beat longer than the room expected, the look of one specialist recognising another's instrument.

Frik, who'd been holding it, said it.

"One point — *one point on the curve—*" the echo running first, the way it always did, and then the blurt arriving under its own power, fast and clean and not askable-back: "*they froze it.*"

The lane went quiet around the words.

"They froze it," Frik said again, quieter, his hand opening and closing. "Whole — the whole word *moving,* two thousand years of it moving, and they just — *click.* Snapshot. Crew of twelve, gold mine, planet. Done." He blinked. "That's the cheat. That's the whole—"

"That's the whole cheat," Narin agreed, and she said it to Frik directly, no flinch, no wince, no managing him, just answering the truest thing in the lane like it was the truest thing in the lane. "You take a word that drifts through twenty centuries and you nail one frame of it to a corkboard and you sell tickets. You take a theology — a *changing,* arguing, living theology — and you call it a roster. A roster doesn't change. That's the appeal. People don't want a god whose meaning is wet shale under your feet. They want a name tag."

Bashir felt the seam open. Not loudly. Just the first half-inch.

Because this was the move, this was the actual move the book they didn't know they were in kept making: the alien version was the *easy* version, and the easy version fell apart the second you read the clay, and it fell apart not because someone shouted *fake* at it but because the real thing was *more.* The real word had been alive. The fringe had embalmed it.

"Show me how the planet dies in *this* one," Yusuf said. "VA 243 we did. The number, the Nibiru thing, fine. But this—" he gestured at the dun column — "if I'm the network, I'm telling a guy in a hangar this says *the Anunnaki came from the sky to mine gold.* What does it actually say? Make it die in the words. That's the only place I believe it."

Narin almost smiled. Bashir saw it cross and go.

"You learn fast for an honest man," she said.

"I'm not honest. I'm reformed. There's a discount."

She did smile then, briefly, and turned the page so the top word stood clear, and put one fingertip — not touching, hovering — over the wedges of *nun.*

"Here's the planet dying," she said. "*Nun* is the prince. You can *read* it. It's a sign for nobility, for the high-born, and it's all over the language — it's in titles, it's in the names of cities, *Eridu* has it, the temple of Enki at Eridu is the *E-abzu,* the house of the deep, and the deep, the *abzu,* the sweet water under the earth — that's where the wisdom lives, down, not up. The high-born gods of the *sky-and-earth* couple. An above, Ki below. Their children straddle both. They are not from *somewhere else.* They are from *here* and *up there* at once, because the whole cosmos is family, and family is how a Sumerian explained the weather."

"So when Sitchin reads *they came down,*" Bashir said, feeling the translation crack along its real fault line, "the *down*—"

"The *down* is theology, not a landing." Narin's eyes were bright now, the gating relaxed a notch further, the scholar coming up through the gatekeeper. "Gods come down to their temples. They *inhabit* the statue, the house, the city. The divine descends into the made thing — the cult image, the ziggurat that's literally built as a stairway so the god has somewhere to put his foot. *Come down* is the most ordinary verb in the religion. It's what the god does on a feast day. You translate it as a spaceship and you've — you've heard a man say *grace* and reported that he summoned food from orbit."

Naila made a sound — not a laugh, a kind of exhale through the nose, the closest she came in a crowd. "That's good," she said. "That's a clean — that's *clean.*"

"It's clean because it's *true,*" Narin said, and for the first time there was an edge under it, the old wound flexing, "and the true reading is *better,* and that's the part that makes me—" she stopped. Recalibrated. Bashir watched her put the heat down somewhere safe and pick the words up cooler. "These were my ancestors. Not in a poster way. The Nineveh plain is forty kilometres from where you're sitting. My family is from villages where they were still naming kids out of these names a hundred years ago. And a Russian-American businessman who couldn't read a line of Sumerian decided my great-great-grandparents' great-great-grandparents were too stupid to invent the wheel, the city, the *first writing on earth,* and that sky-people must have done it for them. And then a streaming company made it look beautiful." She breathed out. "So when you ask me what they were *for.* That's the tense I keep wanting to break."

The lane held it. Even Frik held it.

It was Mira who moved into the quiet — Mira, who read the thing the mouth wouldn't say, and who had been watching Narin's face the whole time and not the page at all.

She signed something small and only half for the room — to herself, working it out — and then she said it aloud, slow: "You're not angry that they got it wrong."

Narin looked at her.

"You're angry," Mira said, "that they got it wrong in a way that made your people *furniture.* In their own story."

Bashir watched Narin not answer for a second, which was its own answer, and watched her decide Mira had earned the truth and hand it over with a single nod, no words wasted, the way you pass something heavy to the one person who reached for the right handle.

"Yes," Narin said.

And that — Bashir thought, and didn't say — that was the whole inversion. Not *clever foreigners crack Sumer.* The cracking had been done by foreigners once already and they'd cracked it open and poured aliens in. The repair was hers. They were here to watch her do it, and hold the light, and write it down, and the win was not theirs to take.

Edith had not picked up the camera.

He noticed because he was watching for it now. She'd had three moments any photographer would kill for — Narin's hand over the wedge, Naila's exhale, the nod to Mira — and she'd sat there with the cap on and her hands in her lap, watching like she'd taught her hands to wait. At the nod to Mira her fingers had twitched toward the strap and stopped.

"You can shoot the page," Narin said, without turning, like she'd felt it. "Not us. The page."

"I wasn't going to shoot you," Edith said.

"I know." A beat. "That's new. Shoot the page."

Edith lifted the camera, took the cap off, and framed the dun column flat and square in the lane light — the document, not the woo, the clay and not the headline — and the shutter went once, soft. She put it down again.

Yusuf was still on the planet. Bashir recognised that too: the man circling back to the thing he most wanted to be true, picking at it, not ready to let it go clean.

"But the *Igigi,*" Yusuf said. "You said it like it mattered. Junior gods. Working gods. That's the part — if I'm selling, that's where I plant the gold mine, isn't it? *Working* gods. Labour. There's labour in it somewhere, that's real, that's actually in the clay, that's why it *grabs.*"

"There's labour in it," Narin said carefully. "That part's not invented. That's the thing I want you to understand about the good lies — they're not made of nothing. They're made of something *true* with the lights changed." She set the dun page down on the bench, flat, and put her two hands on her knees. "The Igigi. The junior gods. And in one of the oldest stories we have — older than Genesis by a long way, older than most of the Bible by a thousand years and more—"

She stopped, and Bashir saw her clock the word *Bible* land in the lane, saw her register that it had changed the air, and saw her not flinch from it and not lunge at it either. Just set it down on the bench beside the page, gently, like a thing she'd handle properly later.

"—in one of the oldest stories, the junior gods are made to *dig.*"

"There it is," Yusuf breathed.

"Not for gold." Narin's voice came down like a level. "For the *rivers.* For irrigation. The Igigi are made to dig the Tigris and the Euphrates, to haul the basket of earth, to break their backs on the canals — which is the most Mesopotamian image in the world, by the way, because that's what *living here was.* You dig the canal or you die. You haul the basket or there's no barley. The whole civilisation is a fight with two rivers and a desert. So when they imagined the gods working, they imagined them doing *the work everyone in the room had blisters from.* Digging mud. Carrying mud. Year on year on year."

"For how long?" Naila asked. Of course she did.

"Forty years, in one version. The text says it like a complaint. *They have borne the toil night and day.* Forty years of carrying the basket." Narin's hands turned over. "And then—"

She let it hang.

"And then they put down the basket," she said. "The Igigi. The junior gods. They set fire to their tools in the night and they march on the house of the chief god and they surround it and they refuse to dig one more cubit of canal. A *strike.* Four thousand years ago. The oldest labour action in literature is a *god* throwing his shovel in the fire."

Frik laughed — a sharp bark, half tic, half genuine, his hand going to his mouth and not making it. "*Sorry* — sorry, that's — the gods *unionised,*" and the echo caught it and ran it once more under his breath, *unionised, unionised,* and then he was grinning helplessly at the clay.

"They unionised," Narin agreed, and let him have it, and the lane laughed with him and not at him, which Bashir had come to understand was the entire architecture of this crew, the one load-bearing wall — that the laugh went *with.*

"So the management's got a problem," Yusuf said, leaning all the way in now, money-brain fully lit. "Workforce down tools. Production stops. Canals silt up. No barley. What's the fix? You can't make the gods go back, they'll just strike again. You need—"

"You need a *new* worker," Narin said. "Cheaper. Replaceable. One that won't burn its tools, or one that you don't mind if it does."

She picked the dun page back up. The light ran down the word at the top of it one more time — *the princely offspring,* the high-born seed of the sky, the ones who sat in council and weighed the world and would, two thousand years on, slide all the way down into the grey courts of the dead.

"And what they decide to make," she said, and her voice changed, went lower, went almost reverent, the scholar and the heir and the believer all standing in the same body in the same heat, "what the council of the great gods decides to make so that the junior gods can rest — what they reach for, to make this new worker out of—"

She didn't finish it.

She looked at them — Naila at the lane's mouth, Yusuf on the stool with his whole hunger showing, Mira against the wall reading every face including hers, Edith with her hands folded over a camera she hadn't raised, Frik bright and undefended, Bashir holding the door half-open with his whole body.

Out past the awning, in the white part of the lane, the Beast sat ticking in the heat with its water bag weeping a dark line down the dust, and a shape moved around it slow and unhurried, checking the shade, doing the thing he did that nobody could ever quite photograph; and somewhere in the citadel above them the call to something drifted thin across the rooftops, and the two rivers that had made all of this kept running south the way they'd run for six thousand years toward the cradle none of them had a permit to enter yet.

"You'll want this in the right tongue," Narin said. "Not mine paraphrasing it. The actual words. Because the next part is where they stole the most, and built the most, and where it stops being about Iraq and starts being about a book half the world prays out of — and I'm not handing *that* across on a paraphrase."

She stood. She gathered the dun page against her chest the way she had before, the gatekeeper coming back up over the scholar, but gentler now, the gate left on the latch.

"Tomorrow," she said. "My uncle's got the Atrahasis. The good edition. And we read what they made the worker out of, in the language it was written in, line by line." She looked at Bashir. "You read Sumerian?"

"Some," he said. "Enough to embarrass myself in front of you."

"Good." Almost a smile. "I'll need someone who can hear when I'm rushing it. There's a pun in the next part." She paused at the door. "There's a pun three thousand years old that the whole West missed because it can't survive the trip into Hebrew. You'll like it. It's the best joke in the Bible and nobody who quotes that verse has ever once laughed."

And she went inside, into the cool, among her uncle's shelves, and left the seven of them in the cut light of the lane with the word still hanging there unfinished — *what they reach for, to make this new worker out of* — and the answer waiting one day off, down in the older clay, in the tongue it was written in.

Bashir looked at the dust where the man and the truck had been.

The clay didn't say *spaceship.* It never had. It said *we were tired, and we made something to be tired in our place, and we made it out of—*

He didn't finish it either.

He'd wait for her to say it right.

# Chapter 8 — Made of Clay

The good edition of the Atrahasis turned out to be three books, two photocopied folders, and a paper-clipped sheaf that smelled of someone else's cigarettes, all of it spread across the uncle's table under the awning while the morning still had some cool left in it.

Bashir had not slept. He'd lain on the borrowed cot in the back room and run the line in his head — *we were tired, and we made something to be tired in our place* — except in his head it kept trying to assemble itself in Sumerian and failing, because he didn't have the lines, only the rumour of them, and a man who's spent his life inside other people's words knows the difference between a sentence he can carry and one he's only heard described. He'd lain there reaching for it and missing, the way you reach in a dark kitchen for a glass you put down somewhere else.

Now Narin set a real glass in front of him. Tea, not water. The uncle's, poured from the dented pot, two sugars dropped without asking because the uncle did not ask, he simply decided you were the kind of guest who took two and was usually right.

"Drink," Narin said. "Then we work."

The uncle had gone in out of the heat. Jakobus was somewhere past the gate with the Beast and a problem involving a radiator hose and a length of rubber that had, the last anyone heard, come off a tractor; you could hear the occasional clink of a spanner and, once, a low run of whistling that climbed a scale and then abandoned it. Edith sat at the table's far end with her camera on the bench beside her, lens-cap on, hands folded, asking nothing. Frik had his back to the wall and his knee going. Mira sat where she could see all their faces at once, which was a thing Bashir had stopped noticing she did until he'd noticed it, and now he couldn't stop seeing it. Yusuf had taken the chair closest to the books. He had a notebook open. He had not written anything in it.

"Atrahasis," Narin said. She laid her hand flat on the topmost folder. "Old Babylonian. The copy we lean on dates to the reign of Ammi-saduqa — that's a name, not a date, but call it eighteenth century before Christ. Three tablets. The scribe signs them. His name is Ipiq-Aya. A junior scribe. He tells you he's junior. He apologises, basically, in the colophon."

"The scribe apologises," Frik said.

"He says he's a junior apprentice and he writes the date and then he asks the gods to bless the man who doesn't erase his tablet." A small dryness moved across her face. "Three thousand seven hundred years and a junior scribe is still asking you not to delete his file."

Yusuf made a sound that wasn't quite a laugh.

"This is older than the Bible," Narin said, and she said it plainly, the way you'd say a river is older than a bridge — not a provocation, a fact about water and stone. "Older than the oldest layer of it. I want that sitting in the room before we start. Not to take anything from anybody. Just so nobody's surprised later about which way the borrowing ran." Her eyes touched Yusuf, then moved on, kind and quick. "Now. The opening. Bashir. Read me the first line you can read."

He pulled the photocopy toward him. The transliteration ran in its little italic forest, the Sumerograms shouting in capitals through the Akkadian, and his eye found the place she'd marked with a pencil tick.

"*Inūma ilū awīlum*," he read.

He stopped. He read it again, slower, and the stopping was not for the others.

"When the gods," he said, "were man."

The awning ticked in the heat. Out by the gate the spanner went still.

"That's the first line," Narin said. "*Inūma ilū awīlum.* When the gods were man. When the gods were the man. It's deliberately strange. It's meant to snag. The gods *were* the human — meaning, before there was a human, the gods did the human's work. They were the labour. There was no us yet, so the gods themselves had to dig."

"Dig what," Yusuf said.

"Canals." She turned a page in the folder, and there it was, the lines marching down. "This is the part everyone skips on the way to the flood. The gods are sorted into two ranks. The Anunnaki up top — the high ones, the princely ones, the bosses." She let the word sit; they'd earned it the day before, and she didn't relitigate it. "And under them, the Igigi. The working gods. The labour gods. And the Igigi carry the load. They dig the Tigris. They dig the Euphrates. By hand. For — the text gives you a span, and the span is brutal."

Bashir found it. The number stood up off the page at him.

"Forty years," he said, before he'd meant to translate it out loud. Then he checked himself, because numbers in cuneiform were exactly the trap they'd spent two days falling into. "It says — that's how I'd read it. Long stretches. Forty. Then ten. The text piles up time on them."

"Read it as 'too long,'" Narin said. "Read it as 'longer than a god should be made to dig.' That's the register. The point isn't the arithmetic." She didn't look at Naila when she said it, but Naila, who'd come up quietly to the table's edge with a heel-thump of socked feet, looked at the number and then at Narin and gave one short nod, granting it, *yes, that one's not for me, that one's a feeling dressed as a figure*, and the small permission of it passed between them without a word.

"And then," Narin said, "the Igigi have had enough."

She read it herself now, in the Akkadian, low and even, not performing it, and Bashir watched the room change shape. Mira had gone very still and was reading Narin's mouth and her throat and the small muscles beside her eyes, gathering the part of the meaning that doesn't ride on sound. Frik's knee had stopped. Even Edith's folded hands had loosened.

"They down tools," Narin said, coming back into English. "That's the only way to say it. They set fire to their digging tools. At night. They surround the house of Enlil — the chief god, the boss of bosses — they march on his door in the dark with torches, and they will not dig another canal." She tapped the page. "It's the oldest labour action in the written record. The first wildcat strike is *gods*. Striking against *god*."

"They strike," Frik said. Soft. Delighted, almost, and ashamed of being delighted, which was its own kind of true. "The workers strike."

"The workers strike," Narin said, and let him have it.

Yusuf had stopped pretending to hold the pen. "So what do the bosses do."

"They panic." Narin almost smiled. "They genuinely panic. Enlil cries.
