There's a whole scene of the high gods milling around at the door in the dark not knowing what to do, sending messengers, asking *who started this, who's the ringleader* — and the Igigi answer, beautifully, that *all of us* started it, every one of us, we declared war together. No ringleader. You can't behead a no." She turned the page. "And the mother. Now we get the mother."

Bashir felt the chapter of it turn under his hand. The forest of italics rearranged itself around a name.

"There's a goddess," Narin said. "Names shift on her across the versions — Nintu, Mami, Bēlet-ilī, Lady of the gods. She's the womb-goddess. The birth-goddess. The midwife of heaven. And one of the gods — the clever one, Enki, Ea, the water-and-wisdom one — stands up in the council and proposes the solution. The Igigi won't dig. Fine. *Make a substitute.* Make a thing that will carry the load instead. He says it to her directly." Her finger came down on the line and stayed there. "He says: *you are the womb-goddess, the one who can create. Create a* lullû. *Create the human. Let him bear the yoke.*"

She looked up.

"*Lullû awīlum.* The savage human. The primitive man. The clay-thing. *Let him bear the yoke that was the gods' yoke. Let him bear the load.* And then there's the line. Bashir."

He'd already found it. He'd found it before she said his name and he'd been sitting on it because saying it felt like a step you couldn't take back.

"*I have imposed your toil on man*," he read.

He let it land in his own chest first. It went in clean.

"*I have imposed your toil on man,*" Narin said after him, in English, and then in the Akkadian, so the room had it both ways, so it had the dressed version and the naked one. "The gods are freed. The Igigi never have to dig again. That's what we're for. That's the *job description.* In the oldest version of the human story this civilisation wrote down, the reason we exist is that the gods got tired of working, and they made a tool, and the tool was us."

Nobody said anything.

Out at the gate a hose-clamp went *tk-tk-tk* against the radiator, three small turns, and stopped.

"Now," Narin said. "What do they make us *out* of."

She didn't rush it. She let them want it. The gatekeeper and the teacher had become, in two days, hard to tell apart; the gate was open but she still chose the order you went through.

"Two ingredients," she said. "First. Clay. Earth. The mud of the abzu, the deep waters under the ground." She paused. "Hold the clay. We come back to the clay. Second—"

She turned to the other folder, the Enuma Elish, the *good* edition with the better photographs, and slid out a glossy plate, a museum photograph of a tablet, the wedges crowding the broken edge.

"—because the recipe gets two writers, and they don't agree on the cooking, only the ingredients. Atrahasis says: they slaughter a god. A god named We-ila — a god who *had* something, the texts argue about what, intelligence, breath, a heartbeat, a *planning-spirit* — and they kill him in the assembly, and they mix his flesh and his blood into the clay. And the womb-goddess spits on the clay. And the great gods spit on it. And it's worked together — *and a ghost comes into being from the god's flesh.* The text says that. A spirit, an *eṭemmu*, a ghost, comes into the clay from the slain god and it stays. It's the part of us that doesn't go quiet. The drumbeat that goes on beating after the body's mixed. They give us a leftover piece of a dead god so the clay will get up and walk."

Bashir's hands were flat on the table. He found he'd flattened them to stop them moving.

"And Enuma Elish," Narin said, sliding the plate an inch toward the middle of the table so they could all see the photograph, "the Babylonian one, the great one, says it slightly differently and worse. There the slain god is Kingu — the rebel general, the consort of the chaos-mother Tiamat, the one who carried the rebellion's authority. The victorious god, Marduk, has won the war in heaven. And the council needs a labour force, same as ever — *to set the gods free, to release them from service.* And the question goes around: who's to blame for the war? Who do we kill for this? And Kingu's given up. They arrest him. They cut his arteries. And from *his* blood — the rebel's, the traitor's, the guilty party's — Ea fashions humankind."

She set her hand beside the photograph but did not touch it.

"So in the version half of you might've grown up adjacent to, in the great state liturgy of Babylon," she said, "the substance of the human being is the blood of a condemned rebel, mixed into mud, *so that the gods can take a day off.* We are made of the guilty. We are made of the loser. We are made to clock in."

It was Yusuf who broke.

He didn't make a scene of it. That was almost the worst part, Bashir thought afterward — that Yusuf, who could fill a room when he wanted to, who'd talked their way past three checkpoints and a man with a clipboard who'd hated all of them on sight, just sat there with his closed notebook and his idle pen and went somewhere behind his own eyes, and his jaw worked once, and he said, to the photograph, not to any of them:

"That's it?"

"Yusuf," Narin said.

"No — I'm asking. That's the real one. That's the original. The thing under the thing." His voice stayed level and that was how Bashir knew it wasn't level at all. "We dug it up, we deciphered it, we did all the work, and *that's* what it says. We were made because somebody upstairs didn't want to dig their own ditch."

"Yes," Narin said. She didn't soften it. She'd watched a lot of people meet this text and she knew the only insult was a lie. "That's what it says."

Yusuf turned the pen over in his fingers. Once. Twice.

And Bashir watched the door close on him, and understood — fully, with his whole chest, for the first time since a man with sunglasses and a clean hanky had found him in a Marseille hostel and asked him in four languages whether he'd ever wondered about a certain seal-impression — understood what the man had actually gathered them around. Not a mystery. A grief, with a mystery painted over it.

Because Bashir had read the other version too. He'd read it in English, years back, in a paperback with a foil cover, on a long train with nowhere to be. *They came for gold.* The sky-people, the long-lived ones, the home planet with the failing atmosphere; they came down to the marsh-lands to mine, and the work was hard, and the working ones rebelled — *they* down their tools, in that telling, same as the Igigi — and so the chief scientist and the birth-goddess took the clay of this planet and the *essence* of a sky-person and they made a worker. A primitive worker. Made in their image, with a piece of them inside it, to carry the load.

The skeleton was the same. He could see it now, laid bare on the table beside the real one: rebellion-of-the-workers, decision-to-make-a-substitute, clay-of-the-earth plus a-divine-contribution, made-to-bear-the-load. Bone for bone. Joint for joint. Whoever had written the foil paperback had not invented a thing. He'd kept the whole skeleton and changed the meat. He'd swapped the gods for astronauts and the canals for gold and the ghost-in-the-clay for *a splice of DNA*, and—

And he'd done one more thing, and that was the thing that had Yusuf turning his pen over, and Bashir said it before he could stop himself, the way Narin had read the line about the toil before she meant to.

"He made it kinder."

They all looked at him. Mira's eyes came to his mouth.

"The — the other version. The famous one." He kept his eyes on the photograph because it was easier than faces. "It says the same skeleton. But it tells it kinder. In that one we're *designed.* We're a *project.* Someone clever stood over the clay and *wanted* a thing and made it on purpose and put a piece of themselves in it because they needed it to be smart enough to use a *pickaxe.* You're — in that story you matter. You're the solution. You're wanted." He swallowed. "In *this* one—" he put two fingers on the museum plate, near the broken edge, not quite touching the glossy wedges "—in this one we're a way the bosses got out of digging. We're made of mud and a corpse so the management can knock off early. There's no part of it that needs us to be clever. There's no part of it that *wants* us. It just needs the ditch dug by something that isn't a god."

He stopped.

"That's the trade," he said. "That's the whole trade. The alien story keeps every bone and gives you back the one thing this story takes away. It tells you you were *meant.*"

The awning ticked. Somewhere a generator two streets over coughed and caught.

Naila had been standing at the corner of the table with her arms wrapped around herself, swaying a little, the table-edge under one hand where she could feel the grain of it. She'd been quiet through all of it, doing her own arithmetic, and now she said, to the wood, in the flat clear way she said the true things:

"It's the same shape. Drawn around a nicer middle." She tapped twice on the table, lining something up nobody else could see. "If you want the shape to be true you have to take the middle that comes with it. They cut the middle out and glued in a better one and kept the shape, and people felt the shape was *real* — because it is, it's three thousand seven hundred years real — and they thought the feeling of the shape was a feeling about the middle." Another tap. "It isn't. The shape's old. The middle's new. The new middle is the part that makes you feel chosen." She looked up, found Yusuf, held it for the half-second she could. "It's a comfort wearing a fact's coat."

Yusuf put the pen down. Flat. Deliberate. Like a man laying down a tool.

"I read all of it," he said. Quietly. To Naila, because she'd given him the room to. "All twelve books. I read them on a phone, in a Land Cruiser, between two consignments I'm not proud of, while a man across the dashboard counted money that was the wrong colour. And the whole time I'm reading—" He stopped. Started again. "You want to know the worst of it? It wasn't even about the aliens for me. It was that it made the world have a *reason.* It made all of it — the consignments, the money, the man, *me* — it made it a chapter in something. Like there was a plan and I'd just lost the page. And I needed there to be a plan so badly that I never once asked the page what it actually said." He looked at the photograph. At the wedges. "And this is what it actually said. We were made to dig. And then to die. There's a flood coming in tablet three to thin us out because we get too loud. We breed and we make noise and the boss can't sleep, so he drowns us. That's the *plan*, Naila. That's what was on the page I was so desperate to find."

"Yes," said Narin, again, and this time she did soften it, but not the fact — only the silence around it. She reached and turned the Atrahasis folder so the photograph faced her, and she touched the broken edge of the worker-line, the *I have imposed your toil on man*, with a fingertip, the way you'd touch a scar that wasn't yours.

"But listen to me, because I'm the one who has to live next door to this text," she said. "It is the bleaker story. It's mud and a dead man's blood and forty years of digging and a flood for the crime of being loud. That's all true. And it is also the *braver* one. Because these people—" her hand opened over the books, the photocopies, the cigarette-smelling sheaf, her uncle's whole crowded shelf "—these people sat in the heat between two rivers four thousand years ago and looked their actual situation in the face. The canals really did have to be dug. By hand. By people. Forever. The river really did flood and really did drown the loud and the quiet alike with no regard for anybody's plan. And instead of telling themselves a story where the sky loved them and built them on purpose, they told the truth: *we are clay and a borrowed spark, and the work is endless, and the water does not care.* And then—"

She stopped. The teacher caught herself one syllable before a sermon, and the gate came down to exactly the height she wanted it.

"And then they kept going anyway," she said. "They wrote it down. They signed it. Ipiq-Aya the junior scribe wrote *please don't erase me* at the bottom of the bleakest story a person ever told about themselves, and three thousand seven hundred years later we did not erase him. He's right here. We're saying his name." She looked around the table. "That's not nothing. That's the opposite of nothing. They didn't need to be chosen to be worth the clay. That's braver than any spaceship."

It was very quiet for a moment.

Then Frik, who had not blurted once in twenty minutes, who'd held it the way you hold a breath, let it out, and it came out true the way it always came out true, aimed at the foil paperback that wasn't even on the table:

"He gave you the gold to look at so you'd never read the *ditch.*"

And the awful elegant rightness of it sat there, because that was the seam, exactly — the gold was the bright thing, the gold pulled the eye, *they came for gold* was a treasure-hunt and a treasure-hunt is a kind of hope; and behind the gold the original word stood plain and unhopeful: *toil.* Imposed. On man.

Out at the gate the spanner clinked down onto stone. There was the sound of a hand testing a fresh clamp, the squeak of rubber seating home, and then the low whistle again, climbing its scale, and this time finishing it — a small, complete, unhurried tune, the sound of a man who had fixed a thing and was content to let the fixing be the whole of it, and asked nothing of the table about what it had found.

Narin let it breathe. Then she pulled the third folder over — thinner, older photocopies, a different hand of marginalia in it, in a script Bashir didn't know.

"Tomorrow we'll do the pun," she said. "The Lady of the Rib. I promised you the best joke in the Bible and I keep my promises." A flicker. "But you're not ready for the joke until you believe the rest. Until you believe it isn't a fairy tale somebody made up at a campfire. Until you believe people sat in a real city and read this off a real tablet and *checked each other's work.*"

She set her hand on the thin folder.

"Because that's the part nobody tells you," she said. "Everyone wants to argue about what the clay *says.* Almost nobody asks the question that should come first." She looked at them, the gatekeeper gone all the way down now, the scholar offering it up. "How do we even know we can *read* it? A dead language nobody'd spoken in two thousand years, in a script with six hundred signs, dug out of a hill in pieces? How does anyone *prove* they cracked that — and didn't just make up a story about a story?"

She turned the folder so its faded first page faced the room.

"Tomorrow," Narin said, "I'm going to tell you about the time four men sealed their answers in four envelopes."

Yusuf picked his pen back up.

This time, he wrote.

# Chapter 9 — The Real Evidence

Naila woke before the heat did, which was the only honest hour in Erbil. She had learned this in two days. The light came up grey-gold over the citadel and the air still held the night's coolness like a held breath, and for maybe forty minutes a person could think without their own sweat arguing with them. She took the thin folder out to the awning table while the others slept, because she wanted to read the sealed-envelope story alone before Narin narrated it. She wanted to find the shape of it herself. She did not want to be *shown* the pattern. She wanted to *catch* it.

The marginalia in this folder was in the script Bashir didn't know — Syriac, Narin had said, her grandmother's hand, a woman who had copied scholarship by lamp the way other women copied recipes. Naila couldn't read the Syriac either. But she could read the photocopied English under it, and she could read the structure, and the structure was what made her sit very still with her tea going cold.

It went like this.

A man named Henry Rawlinson, an army officer, had spent years hanging off a cliff in Persia. The cliff was called Behistun, and three hundred feet up it, a Persian king named Darius had carved a brag the size of a billboard: I am Darius, I crushed the liars, the god Ahuramazda gave me the kingdom. He'd carved it three times, in three languages — Old Persian, Elamite, Babylonian — because he wanted everyone in his empire to read it, and the three languages were three doors into the same room.

Naila's chest did the thing it did when a system clicked. Three statements. Same content. Different code. If you could read one, you had a key to the others. It was *parallel text.* It was the most beautiful kind of proof there was, because you didn't have to trust the man who carved it; you only had to trust that he'd said the same thing three times, and that arithmetic — the brute, unbendable count of which sign sat where — would hold him to it.

She heard the awning rustle. Mira, barefoot on the packed earth, hair flat on one side from sleep, reading Naila's face before Naila said a word. Mira tapped two fingers near her own eye — *you're seeing something* — and sat, and Naila turned the page so they could both look, and that was a whole conversation.

The rest of the house woke in pieces. Yusuf with coffee he'd made wrong and drunk anyway. Bashir with a notebook already open, as if he slept holding it. Frik in last night's shirt, hands going, jaw working on a word that hadn't decided to come out yet. Edith last, camera bag over one shoulder by reflex, the strap-mark already worn pale into the canvas. Out past the gate the Beast sat with its hood down now, and a low African vowel-music drifted off it — Jakobus, working a wet rag over the windscreen against the dust, asking the morning for nothing.

Then Narin came out with the real coffee and the rest of the folder and the morning began.

---

"He nearly died for it," Narin said, before she sat. "Rawlinson. Behistun. People skip that part. He's swinging off a rope on a cliff three hundred feet up, copying a Persian king's victory speech sign by sign, and the only ladder to the Babylonian section is gone, so he hires a Kurdish boy — a local boy, mind you —" a small, dry stress on *local* that landed on every foreigner at the table at once "— who free-climbs across the rock face with a paper squeeze and copies what no adult could reach. So when people tell you cuneiform was *cracked*, remember a Kurdish kid with no rope is in that sentence, and nobody knows his name."

Edith's hand moved toward the camera bag. Stopped. Did not.

Naila watched her stop. So did Mira.

"Old Persian came first," Narin went on. "Forty-odd signs, alphabetic-ish, the easy door. They guessed at it from the proper names — you find *Darius, Xerxes, Hystaspes* in the king-lists, you find the same shapes on the rock in the right places, you start matching sounds. Slow. Years. Arguments. But the Persian gave them the key, and the key turned the Babylonian, because Darius had said the same thing three times." She set a hand flat on the table. "And *that's* where it gets ugly. Because Babylonian — Akkadian cuneiform — isn't forty signs. It's six hundred. And the same sign can be a syllable, or a whole word, or a silent marker telling you what *kind* of word is coming. One sign reads four different ways depending on its mood."

"Polyvalence," Bashir said, soft, like a name he was glad to hear out loud. "It's not a code. It's a *language wearing* a code."

"It's a nightmare," Narin said. "And here's the part the believers should sit with. By the 1850s you had four men in Europe all claiming they could read it. Edward Hincks, an Irish parson stuck in a country rectory — possibly the best of them, never got the credit. Henry Rawlinson, the cliff man, all bluster. Jules Oppert, German-French, brilliant and prickly. And William Henry Fox Talbot — yes, *that* Talbot, the photography one—"

Edith's head came up.

"—he invented the negative and then got bored and learned dead languages," Narin said, and let the small smile happen. "And the field had a problem. Because four men reading the same broken tablet and getting four different stories — that's not a science. That's a séance. That's everybody seeing the face they brought with them."

Frik's hands went still. His mouth opened. "*That's the fringe,*" he said — flat, blurted, not to anyone. "Four men. Four stories. Pick one." His face coloured at having said it. Nobody moved to make it a thing.

"Yes," Narin said simply, like he'd answered a question correctly. "That's exactly the fringe. So in 1857 they did something the fringe has never once done in its life."

She slid a single page to the centre of the table. A photocopy of a printed report, Royal Asiatic Society, the long S's of old type.

"Somebody had dug up a clay prism — a barrel of baked clay, eight sides, covered in the annals of an Assyrian king, Tiglath-Pileser the First. Eight hundred lines. A king bragging about his wars and his hunts and the temples he fixed, which is what kings did, it was the only thing they ever did. And the Society had a brilliant, nasty idea." Narin's eyes had the brightness now that Naila had learned meant the gate was open all the way. "They printed four copies of the prism. They sent one to each man. Hincks, Rawlinson, Oppert, Talbot. *Translate this. Independently. Tell no one. Seal your answer in an envelope and send it back to us unopened.*"

The table was very quiet. Out at the gate the rag stopped on the glass, then started again.

Naila felt the shape lock. This was the thing she'd caught at dawn and hadn't had the word for. "Blind," she said. "It was blind. None of them could bend toward the others. None of them could see the answer and steer the glyphs to meet it."

"It was a *trial,*" Narin said. "It was the cradle of the whole discipline putting its own neck on the block. Because if the four envelopes came back four different stories, that was the end. Then cuneiform was a séance, and everyone reading it was a fraud or a fool, and you could fold the whole field up and go home. They *risked it.* That's the part. They were willing to be proven liars in public."

"And?" Yusuf said. He'd stopped writing. He had a habit, Naila had noticed, of going still when money or proof was on the table — the part of him that used to evaluate whether a thing was real for a buyer.

"The twenty-ninth of May, 1857," Narin said. "They opened the four envelopes in front of a committee. Four men. Four sealed translations of eight hundred lines of an arrogant dead king. And they *matched.*" She tapped the page. "Not perfectly. There were disagreements — a number here, an unclear sign there. They were honest about those; it's *in the report.* But the substance? The king, the campaigns, the gods, the structure, the sense of it? The same. Four men in four rooms had read the same dead tongue the same way and not known they agreed until the envelopes opened."

She let it sit.

"That," Narin said, "is the moment cuneiform stopped being a guess and became a *reading.* That is the receipt. When somebody tells you the scholars just *invented* a way to read the clay — that they're all in on a story — you tell them about the envelopes. Because the men who made this field earned it by agreeing to be caught if they were wrong. They submitted to the seal."

---

Naila was already running it. Couldn't not. The arithmetic of it, the cleanness.

"It's reproducible," she said. "That's what it is. It's the whole thing science is. You hide the answer from the people generating it, you let them work alone, and if they land in the same place by separate roads, the place is real — not the road, the *place.*" She heard her own voice going faster, the flat fast cadence the others had stopped flinching at. "It's a control. They built a control. They didn't trust *themselves,* that's the genius, they didn't trust their own desire to be right—"

"Naila." Bashir, gently, two fingers tapped on the wood — their version of *land it.*

She landed it. Breathed. "It's a good system," she said, quieter. "It's a really good system."

"It's the *opposite* system," Bashir said, and now he leaned in, and the polyglot's loneliness went out of his face the way it always did when language stopped being a border and started being a key. "Think about what the other method is. The fringe method. You decide first — the gods were astronauts, say. You *know* the answer before you open the envelope. Then you go to the clay and you find the signs that fit and you blur the signs that don't. You bend the glyph to the story." He turned his hands over, empty. "These four men did the inverse. They made it *impossible* to bend. They sealed the story away from the glyph. They made themselves blind on purpose so the clay could speak."

"Say the other one," Naila said. "Say nibiru."

Bashir almost laughed. "*Nibiru.* Yes. The famous twelfth planet. It's a real Akkadian word — *nēberu.* You know what it means? It means a crossing. A ferry-point. The place you cross a river. The Babylonians used it as the name for a celestial *position* — the point a planet crosses at its high station." He spread his hands. "Most often: Jupiter. You can watch it. It's up there every year. *Nēberu* is the crossing-point of *Jupiter,* a thing a Babylonian could see with his own eyes from a rooftop." A beat. "It is never — not once, not anywhere — a death-planet on a thirty-six-hundred-year orbit full of gold-miners. That sum was done by reading the word as a story you brought with you, and never opening the envelope."

"And the *sar,*" Frik said. He'd been holding it, Naila could tell — the over-reach he'd punctured at the museum, days ago now, that had cost him a flinch then too. He got it out clean. "The three-thousand-six-hundred. They call it the *orbit.*" His hands flared and settled. "It's a *number.* Base sixty. Sixty times sixty. It's how they *counted.* It's not a clock for a planet, it's — it's *math homework.*"

"It's their sixty," Narin said. "We still have it. Sixty seconds, sixty minutes, three hundred and sixty degrees. We are standing in their arithmetic right now and we don't even feel it. *Sar* is a *big round number* the way we say *a million* — and someone read it as a doomsday calendar." Her mouth twisted, but not cruelly. "The men who guessed read forward from a story. The four men in 1857 read backward from the clay. One of those methods built a discipline that can read a dead king's grocery list. The other one sold a lot of paperbacks."

The cruelty wasn't in it. Naila noticed that, the way she noticed everything. Narin wasn't sneering at the paperbacks. She'd said it almost tired.

Yusuf had gone quiet in the particular way he went quiet, and Naila watched Mira watch him, because Mira had clocked it first. He turned the RAS page around with one finger, slow.

"It would've been easier if they hadn't matched," Yusuf said, to the page. "For them. Four different answers, you can argue forever. Argument is a market. As long as nobody's *sure,* everybody's selling." He didn't look up. "These four men closed the market. They made it boring. They made it *settled.* And settled doesn't sell."

Nobody asked him what he meant. Naila understood it sideways anyway — that he was talking about the paperbacks, and not only the paperbacks. That he'd been the kind of man who profited from things staying loud. That the man across the table from himself five years ago would've hated this room.

---

The heat had its hand on the day now. The awning's shade had pulled in tight. Out at the gate Jakobus had gone from the windscreen to the radiator, and the Beast made its small sounds, and twice his shadow crossed the gravel and didn't come closer.

Narin folded the RAS page back into its sleeve, and the gate in her face changed key. The teaching gate closed. A harder one came down.

"Now," she said. "The next part isn't a story. It's logistics. And it's where I find out whether you're tourists."

Edith's camera bag was on the table. Narin looked at it, then at Edith, until Edith moved it onto the bench beside her, off the wood. It was not subtle and was not meant to be.

"You want to go south," Narin said. "All of you, even if you haven't said it. You've got the cradle in your eyes. Uruk. Ur. Eridu. The first city, the first writing, the first wheel — it's all *down there,* in the alluvium, where the two rivers used to meet the sea before the silt moved the coast. That's the real thing. That's where the clay we've been reading came *out of the ground.*" She let it ache for half a second, because it was aching in her too. "And we are *not going there.* Not on this trip. Maybe not on any trip you're part of."

"Why?" Bashir asked. No challenge in it. He of all of them knew what a border cost.

"Because south is Baghdad's writ, federal Iraq, and that's a different set of permits, a different security picture, a different *decade* of trust I'd have to spend to take seven foreigners with cameras down there safely, and I won't spend it on a road trip." She didn't raise her voice. That was the thing — she never raised it. "Up here is the Kurdistan Region. The KRG runs its own antiquities, its own roads, its own answer to the question *who is this person and why are they near the tablets.* It's the safe north. It's the part the West is allowed to visit. And even *here* I had to vouch for you. With my name. To people who remember every foreigner who ever came to look at the clay and decide what it meant *for* us."

The diesel-and-dust grain of it sat in the room now, the way it had sat under the whole trip — that they were not on a set, that there were forms with real stamps, that a man named Yusuf had spent three weeks of phone calls and one favour he didn't explain to get them an awning to sit under at all.

"There's a tablet moving," Narin said. Flat. "You should know that's why I let you this close. Not the lecture. The lecture's just so you don't embarrass me. There's a tablet that came out of the south in '03 — out of the museum, or out of a dig nobody licensed, I don't know which yet — and it surfaced two months ago in a sale catalogue. A streaming outfit's involved. The kind that makes the pretty documentaries. And the catalogue copy calls it—" her jaw set "—it calls it *possible evidence of pre-Sumerian astronomical knowledge.* Which is the new way of saying aliens without saying aliens, so the lawyers stay calm."

"What's it actually say," Naila asked. She had to know. The arithmetic of the thing was already itching.

"I've seen one photograph. Bad light." Narin's mouth did something complicated. "It's a school text. A *practice* tablet. A scribe-student copying out grain measures and a list of beer rations for temple workers. *Dubsiga* — a name, a foreman maybe, *your servant so-and-so.* It's a kid's homework, three and a half thousand years old, and somebody is going to sell it for the price of a flat by calling its number-columns a star map." She looked around the table. "And here's the obscene part, the part that should make you angry. The *louder* the lie, the *safer* the theft. A tablet called *proof of ancient astronauts* is worth more on the grey market — and *nobody who could read it ever gets near it,* because the only people in the room are buyers who want the story. If they called it what it is — a beer-ration list — some Assyriologist would walk past the case, read it in four seconds, and ask where it came from." She let that land hard. "The myth is the markup. And the myth is also the *camouflage.* It's the best cover a smuggler ever had. Make it about aliens, and you've made it *un-investigable,* because everyone serious looks away and everyone who looks is in on the wanting."

Edith had gone very still. The 2003 in her was on her face — Naila couldn't read it the way Mira could, but she could see the cost of it, the held breath. Edith had photographs of the museum that year. The sacks going out. She'd never said it but it was in the room every time the looting came up, and Narin knew it was in the room, and that was half of why the camera bag had had to go on the bench.

"And the other lie's just as useful to them," Narin said, softer now, and this was the part Naila hadn't seen coming. "Not just the believers. The *shruggers.* The clever ones who say *oh it's all just myth, who cares, it's all made-up stories anyway.*" She looked, briefly, at no one and everyone. "That's the best cover of all. Because the believer at least *wants* the tablet to be real. The shrugger doesn't care if it's real or stolen or sold or smashed, it's all *just old superstition* to him. You want to move a crate of looted clay through customs? Don't hire a smuggler. Hire a cynic. Hire a man who thinks none of it matters." Her voice didn't rise. It got quieter, which was worse. "Both of them — the one who thinks it's spaceships and the one who thinks it's nothing — both of them help the crate move. The only person who stops the crate is the one who thinks it's *exactly* what it is. A boy's homework. Sacred and ordinary and *somebody's.*"

The Beast's engine turned over out at the gate, caught, settled into an idle. Jakobus had it breathing again. The water bag swayed on the wing where it had been hung to weep and cool, and the music had stopped because the engine was the music now. He didn't come in. He never came in for this part. He was outside making the thing that would carry them, and he left the inside of it — the language, the meaning, the grief, the *who decides* — to the people whose it was.

"So that's the gate," Narin said. "It doesn't open south. It opens *here,* it opens *slow,* and it opens only as far as I say, and I can close it whenever you make me regret it." She looked around once more, and something underneath the hardness was almost — not soft, but *level.* Equal. "You read pattern. You read tongues. You read faces and money and the thing a man blurts when he can't help it. Fine. Good. I needed people who read. But the place is not a puzzle for you to solve and leave. There are people *living* on the cradle right now, in the alluvium, in the dust, going to school, getting married, burying their dead, over the top of the first city anybody ever built. They are not scenery. The West made my ancestors into scenery once. Into a B-movie. I will not hand them to you to do it again."

Nobody spoke. Frik's hands were still. Even Naila's were.

Then Mira moved.

She'd been watching the whole time — not the page, not the tablet talk, *Narin* — and now she lifted one hand and signed something slow and careful and entirely wrong, the crew's terrible handmade dialect, and Bashir started to translate it and stopped, because Narin's face had changed before the words arrived. Whatever Mira had read in her — the thing under the hardness, the thing the lecture was built to cover — Mira had seen it, and let Narin see that she'd seen it.

Narin didn't answer. She looked at Mira for a long moment, and the room understood, in the way a room does, that they had walked up to an edge and that the edge had a name and that Narin was not going to say it.

Naila looked at the third folder, the one with the grandmother's Syriac in the margins. The pun was in there. *Tomorrow,* Narin had promised. The Lady of the Rib.

But the pun lived in the same book as the Flood and the clay and the genealogy, and Naila understood, watching Mira watch Narin, that the joke was going to cost the woman who told it more than any of them had thought a joke could cost.

Mira lowered her hand. And kept her eyes on Narin's face, reading the thing the mouth wouldn't say.

# Chapter 10 — What She Won't Say

The bazaar came at Mira through her feet first.

Erbil's old market wrapped the base of the citadel like a thing that had grown there, and Mira walked it the way she walked everywhere — through the soles of her boots, through her hips, through the rail of warm air that pressed off a baker's oven and broke against her cheek. She felt the generator before she saw it: a low industrial shudder coming up the cobbles, the kind of drone that lived under hearing aids and made them useless, so she'd switched them off an hour ago and let the world go to its truer self. Vibration. Light. Faces.

The faces were the loud part.

Two men by a copper stall, arguing about a price with their whole bodies, hands open, shoulders rolling, neither of them angry — she could tell from the eyes, which stayed soft even as the hands shouted. A grandmother in black, sorting dried limes, her mouth moving in the patient nothing-shapes of a song. A boy running a tray of tea glasses overhead, threading the crowd, his attention a wire pulled taut from the tray to the gap to the next gap. Mira read it all without trying. She'd never had a choice. When the ears go, the eyes do the work the ears refuse, and after thirty-some years the eyes don't stop, can't be told to stand down, will sit in a meeting and tell you who's lying before the lie finishes its first syllable.

Narin walked two steps ahead, and Narin was the only person in the bazaar Mira couldn't fully read.

That was the interesting thing. That was the thing that had kept Mira up the night before, after the session under the awning, after she'd reached out one bad clumsy hand and shown Narin that she'd seen the edge of it — the thing under the lecture, the thing the lecture was a wall against. Narin had built a beautiful wall. Mira admired walls. She'd built a few. But she'd spent a lifetime watching the seams where a wall meets the face that's holding it up, and Narin's seam was *there*, just under the left eye, in the small muscle that tightened every time the talk got near the third folder. The one with the grandmother's handwriting in the margins.

Narin turned and said something. Mira caught *uncle*, caught *tea*, caught the lift of her brows that meant a question, and nodded, and they climbed.

---

The uncle's place was up a stair off the bazaar, under an awning of corrugated tin and threaded vine, on a flat roof that looked over the whole churning market toward the walls of the oldest continuously inhabited place on the planet. Mira knew that fact the way you know a thing from a placard. Standing in it was different. The citadel sat up there in the late gold like a loaf of risen earth, and somewhere under it, layer on layer on layer, were the bones of every Erbil that had ever been, going down past memory into the dark where the first writing had been pressed into the first clay by people who never imagined a woman would stand here who couldn't hear a word of their language and would understand them anyway.

The uncle was small and broad and had hands like the limes downstairs, brown and creased and capable. He saw Mira's hearing aids — she watched him see them, watched the half-second recalibration that most people botched and he didn't — and he didn't shout, didn't slow his lips into a mush, didn't grab her elbow. He simply turned his body square to her, full face, good light behind her so his own face was lit, and welcomed her with his whole front. Some people knew. You couldn't predict who. A man who'd spent eighty years on a roof above a market had learned to be read, and so he made himself readable, and Mira loved him instantly and without complication.

He poured tea. There was no negotiating it. The glass came small and waisted and scalding, amber going to copper, a sugar cube parked on the saucer, and he set it in front of her and watched until she lifted it, and only then did his shoulders come down. *You cannot refuse this,* his whole posture said, and Mira had no intention of refusing anything on this roof.

The rest of the crew came up the stair in ones and twos. Bashir, who folded onto a cushion and started talking to the uncle before he'd finished sitting, the two of them lobbing words back and forth — Arabic, then something older, the uncle's eyebrows going up in delight at whatever Bashir had reached for. Naila, who took the corner seat with the wall at her back and the stair in her sightline, and to whom the uncle handed tea without making it a thing, because Bashir must have said a quiet word at some point about exits. Frik, whose head ticked twice toward the citadel and who said, very clearly, "*Big,*" and nothing else, and the uncle laughed, a real laugh, and Frik's whole face opened. Edith last, with the camera at her hip, not raised, the strap doubled around her wrist.

And outside the awning, down the stair and across the little square where the Beast was parked under a plane tree, Jakobus. Mira could see him through the gap in the vine: a shape at the rear of the vehicle, doing something to a canvas bag that hung off the back and wept a slow dark line of water down the dusty paint. Cooling the water by letting it cool itself, some desert magic. He didn't come up. He never came up to these. He stood by the made thing he understood and let the people who understood the other things do their understanding. The uncle, though — the uncle had gone to the edge of the roof when the Beast first pulled in, and called something down, and Jakobus had answered him, three words, and the uncle had come back to his stove looking like a man who'd been handed a small good gift. Mira had no idea what was said. She only knew that Jakobus, who could not be read, who stood at the wrong angle to every light, had said something to an old Kurdish man on a roof that made the old man glad.

Then Narin sat, and the tea went around, and the third folder came out of her bag and lay on the low table, closed.

---

Edith lifted the camera.

She did it slowly, the way she'd been doing everything slowly since Baghdad came up — and it had come up, the museum, the sacks, the year that was in Edith's camera roll and was the reason Narin had looked at the lens for the first three days like it was a weapon, because for Narin it had been. Mira watched the lift. She watched Narin's eyes go to it. She watched Edith *stop*, the camera at half-height, and turn it toward Narin instead of toward the table, and ask — actually ask, not with a raised brow and a hopeful angle but with her free hand making the small flat circle the crew used for *okay?*, then a point at the folder, then a question-shrug.

Narin looked at the folder. Looked at the camera. Said something. Mira caught the shape: *the folder, no. My uncle, no. Me, you can.*

Edith lowered the camera all the way down. Set it in her lap. Put her open hand over the lens like a lid.

Something in Narin's seam eased. Not the deep one. A shallow one, the one that had been about the lens. There were two walls, Mira had understood that under the awning — one against the camera, which Edith was learning to disarm, and one further in, against the folder, which no one could disarm because Narin was holding it up herself with her own face.

The uncle refilled glasses no one had emptied.

---

Bashir began to translate the session as it ran, but Mira didn't need the words and the words weren't where it lived anyway, so she let his hands do their job at the edge of her sight and gave her eyes to Narin.

The folder opened. The grandmother's Syriac was there, looping up the margins of a typed Sumerian transcription, and beneath each line in a third hand the English. Narin's finger moved along a line. Her mouth made a word Mira had seen her make before, two days ago, under the awning, when the whole conversation had bent around a single broken bone of a joke. *Ninti.* The Lady of the Rib. Bashir's hands flowered when he came to it — Mira saw him reach for the air the way he did when a thing only lived in one language, his fingers opening on *rib*, then opening again on *life*, the same sign twice because Sumerian had hung two meanings on one peg and the joke was the peg. *Ti* is rib. *Ti* is life. So Ninti, the goddess made to heal the water-god's aching side, is both Lady of the Rib and Lady Who Makes Live, and the pun is three thousand years old and it is still funny, and it is hiding under a woman built from a sleeping man's rib in a book that came a thousand years later, the joke worn smooth by the crossing, the peg pulled out and only the rib left.

Mira watched Frik take that in. Watched his hands go still on his knees the way they had at the very end of the last session — the stillness that wasn't a tic at all, that was the opposite of a tic, a man so caught by a true thing that the noise in him went quiet. Naila had gone still the same way. They'd all gone still. A joke that survived three thousand years and one whole change of God.

But Mira wasn't watching the joke.

She was watching Narin, because the joke was the easy part, the part Narin had promised under the awning — *tomorrow, the Lady of the Rib* — and the easy part was over now, and Narin's finger had stopped moving on the page. It rested at the foot of the column. Below the joke. Where the typed Sumerian ran out and there was only the grandmother's Syriac and then the English under it, and Narin's mouth was not making the English.

Bashir's hands paused, waiting for her.

Naila leaned in toward the page, and her voice came — Mira couldn't hear it, but she saw it land on the others, saw Bashir's hands pick it up: *the same flood, the same clay, the same council of gods deciding to make a worker out of mud and a dead god's blood.* The pattern. Naila's gift. The same shape across cultures, the older draft showing through the newer one like a wall showing through plaster. Naila said it cleanly, kindly, the way she said the true things, with no idea — Mira could see she had no idea — what it was doing to the woman across the table.

Because Narin had not moved.

---

Here is what being talked-over feels like, from the inside.

It is not loud. That's the thing people get wrong, the hearing people, who think the harm is volume. It's the opposite of volume. You are in the room. You have a thing to say, and the thing is true and you can see that it's true and you have seen it before anyone, you saw it three sentences ago and you have been carrying it, waiting for the gap — and the gap doesn't come, because the gap is sound and you don't make sound the way they do, and the talk runs over the place where your turn would be like water running over a stone. You are not contradicted. You are not even disagreed with. You are simply not there in the part of the room where the deciding happens, and you watch them decide without the thing you carried, and the thing was the thing they needed, and you watch them go without it.

Mira had lived in that room her whole life. She knew it from the floor up. And so she knew, watching Narin's finger rest at the foot of the column, watching Narin not make the English, that Narin was in a version of the same room. Not Mira's room. Narin could hear. Narin could speak; Narin spoke five languages and out-read every person on this roof on the clay and the meaning, and it was *her* meaning, her language, her dead and her grief and her God. But she had walked up to a thing she carried, the thing under all the other things, and she had reached the gap where her turn was — and her own mouth had run over it like water over a stone. She was talking-over herself. She had a wall and the wall was made of her own voice, the beautiful confident teacher's voice, and the voice was running so the silence couldn't, because in the silence was the thing she would not say.

Naila and Bashir kept working the pattern between them, gentle, brilliant, building the bridge from the clay to the page, the older draft to the newer, and Mira understood with her whole body that they were building it *toward* Narin and that Narin could not cross it, and that no one but Mira could see she couldn't.

So Mira reached across the low table and put two fingers, very lightly, on the back of Narin's resting hand.

The talk stopped. Bashir's hands froze mid-sign. The whole roof felt it — the way a room feels it, the way the cobbles had felt the generator. Narin looked up.

Mira signed.

She signed badly. The crew's handmade dialect, half-invented at a thousand campfires, was a pidgin even by the generous standards of pidgins, and Mira's own native sign was a different country anyway, and Narin knew no sign at all. So Mira did the only true thing, which was to make the signs slowly enough that the *shape* of them carried even when the words didn't. She touched her own chest. She touched the folder. She made the sign the crew used for *holy*, which was wrong, was somebody's guess from years ago, two hands lifting like a roof — and Narin, who couldn't read the sign, read the roof, read the lift, read Mira's face under it, which was not asking Narin to say the thing.

That was the whole message. Mira had it down to nothing. *I see that you won't say it. You don't have to say it. I am not going to make you say it, the way they're about to make you say it without knowing they're making you.*

Bashir started to translate the sign and Mira shook her head at him, once, and he stopped, and got it, and his hands came down, and the roof understood that for once the words were not the point and the man who lived inside all the world's words was the one who saw it first.

---

Narin's seam went. The deep one. The one under the left eye that the whole lecture had been built to hold.

She didn't cry. It wasn't that. Mira would have hated it if it had been that — the easy release, the witnessed weep, the camera-shaped grief. It was smaller and worse and truer. The muscle let go, and for one second Narin's face was not a teacher's face and not a gatekeeper's face and not the face of the woman who had told the West *I will not hand them to you*. It was the face of a person who had found out, somewhere back along the line of her training, in a library or over her grandmother's shoulder, that the book her people had carried into exile and out of it, the book they'd kept their soul inside while empire after empire tried to grind them into scenery — that this book had a genealogy. That the Flood in it had an older draft. That the clay-and-blood, the council, the worker made of mud, were *there before*, in Babylon, in the very tongue of the people who carried her ancestors off in chains.

And that this was not a wound to the book.

That was the thing under the thing. Mira read it whole, in the second the seam gave, before Narin's voice came back to cover it. The terror in the wall was that the genealogy would be a verdict — that a hundred grinning men with green-lit logos would say *see, it's all just stolen myth, the Bible's a fraud, the woo and the shrug are the same coin and we'll spend it both ways* — and that her grandmother's loops in the margin would become a punchline in someone's streaming doc. And under *that* terror, the deeper thing, the thing she had walked up to and would not say:

That she had read the older draft, and the newer one, and she knew what the newer one had *done* with the older furniture. The Babylonian gods make the man out of mud to do the gods' toil, to break their backs digging the rivers so the gods can rest — the man is a slave, made because somebody had to dig and the gods were sick of digging. And the book her people carried took that same furniture — the clay, the breath, the council, the *let us make* — and turned it inside out. Made the man in the image. Not for toil. Not slave-clay. The same mud, and a god who didn't fight a monster to build the world but *spoke* it, and a man not bred to dig but made to be like the one who made him. The newer draft is an argument *against* the older one, written in the older one's own words, by a conquered people sitting in the conqueror's city refusing the conqueror's story of what a human being is for.

Narin knew this. It was the most beautiful thing she knew. And she could not say it on a roof to six foreigners with a camera, because to say it she would have to lay the genealogy on the table first, naked, the sources, the dates, the older clay — and trust that none of them would pick it up and run to the market and sell it as *the Bible is a Babylonian knockoff* before she got to the part where it stops being a theft and starts being the bravest re-authoring a people ever performed under occupation. She would have to hand them the explosive to get to the song. And the West had already shown her, once, in 2003, in sacks, what it did with the explosives.

Mira knew all of it because Narin's face said all of it in the second the seam gave, and faces don't lie, not at that depth, not for that fraction of time.

---

Mira didn't say any of it.

That was the whole gift, and she knew it, and she gave it. She had read the thing the mouth wouldn't say, and the reading was complete, and the completion did not require speech. A book about everything that ever got written down — the clay, the sealed envelopes, the four scholars who proved the language was real, the grandmother's loops in the margin, the joke that crossed three thousand years — a whole book of the written, and it was finished here, on a roof, by the one who read what stayed unsaid, and who understood that the kindest thing she could do with what she'd read was to keep it unsaid too.

She just held Narin's gaze. And nodded. Once. *I have it. It's safe. You don't have to.*

Narin's hand turned over under Mira's two fingers, slowly, until it was palm up, and her fingers closed around Mira's, brief and hard, a grip and a release, the most articulate sentence spoken on that roof and the only one with no language in it at all.

Then Narin's voice came back — Mira saw it return, saw the teacher reassemble — but it came back changed, lower, and she did not pick the folder up. She left it open on the table, the grandmother's loops face-up in the gold light, and she said something short to all of them, and Bashir's hands made it:

*Tomorrow. The whole of it. Not today.*

And Naila, who missed faces the way Mira missed sound, looked up from the pattern she'd been building and read, finally, from Mira's stillness rather than from any face, that the bridge had been built toward something that wasn't ready, and let it lie. She closed her own folder. She put her tea to her lips. The crowd-shudder of the bazaar came up through the roof, and Mira watched Naila's jaw, and reached over without a word and tapped the table twice in front of her — *exit, the stair, two steps* — and Naila's shoulder came down a centimetre, and that was a whole conversation too.

The uncle, who had read the entire wordless thing from his stove, came and refilled the glasses one more time, and set the pot down with finality, and said something to the roof in general that made Bashir laugh out loud and translate it as: *Drink, argue tomorrow, the dead have waited five thousand years, they will wait one more night.*

---

Down in the square, under the plane tree, the Beast's water bag had stopped weeping. The light was nearly gone off the citadel. Jakobus was at the front of the vehicle now, hood up, a torch in his teeth, and Edith — who had not raised her camera once on the roof, who had kept her hand over the lens like a lid through the whole of it — leaned at the parapet and watched him work and did not photograph that either.

He shut the hood. Wiped his hands on a hanky that came out of his pocket impossibly clean and went back impossibly clean. Looked up at the roof, at the line of them along the parapet with their copper glasses, and lifted two fingers off the brim of nothing in a small salute, and said three words up the dark — Mira couldn't hear them, couldn't read his mouth at the angle, would never know them — and the uncle, beside her, raised his glass down into the square in answer.

Then Jakobus crouched by the rear wheel with the torch and unfolded a map across his knee, and traced a line on it with one finger, north and west, toward the edge of the gold country where the safe north thinned out into the disputed ground, and held the finger there, on a place, in the dark, and did not look up again.

# Chapter 11 — The Beast on the Plain

The Beast did not have headlights so much as a strong opinion about the dark.

Edith learned this somewhere past the last village with a name she could pronounce, when Jakobus killed the mains at a wave from the old man on the motorbike ahead — a cousin of a cousin of Narin's uncle, she'd been told, which here meant the same as a passport — and the world went to moon and starlight and the small amber pilot of the dash, and the truck kept moving anyway, downhill into a basin of grass and stone, on a track Edith could not see and Jakobus apparently could.

She had her camera in her lap. She had not lifted it.

This was the discipline now. She felt it in her hands like a held breath: the want of the body to bring the eye up and frame the thing, the click that resolved the world into something she owned, and underneath it the new muscle she was building, slow and against grain, that let the want pass through her and out the other side without her hands moving.

Beside her, in the middle of the bench, Naila had her knees up and a folded printout against them and a penlight in her teeth, reading. She read in moving vehicles the way other people sat still in chairs. The light made a coin of orange on the paper and threw the shapes of cuneiform up onto the underside of her chin.

"Stop here," Narin said, from the back. Quiet. Not to Jakobus — past him. The cousin had stopped his bike at the lip of the basin and was off it, holding something to his ear, a phone with no signal that he was using as a watch. "We walk the last part."

"How far," Yusuf said. He had the look he got near money. Not greed. The opposite of greed — the face of a man reading a room for the exits, who had once been very good at being the danger and was now only good at smelling it.

"Eight hundred metres. Maybe less." Narin opened the door before the Beast had fully stopped. "The site is over the rise. They work it at night because there are no inspectors at night. There are no inspectors at night because the inspectors are in Baghdad arguing about whose budget pays for the road, and the road washed out in March."

"And we're here," Bashir said, "because—"

"Because I want to see it with my own eyes before I file a thing nobody reads." She was out, on the grass, a dark shape against darker land. "And because Hozan says they brought a lorry tonight. Lorry means they are loading. Loading means it is leaving. Once it leaves, it is gone. There is no after. There is only the photograph of the hole where it was."

Edith heard that and felt it land on her like a hand.

She got out.

---

The grass was waist-high and dry and full of the small constant noise of insects, and it smelled of dust and something green underneath, crushed where they walked. Jakobus came last from the Beast, and Edith watched him do the thing he did — the thing she had stopped trying to photograph because it never came out, because there was never enough of him in the frame to say it was him. He took a tin from his breast pocket. Cayenne, she knew now; he'd dusted the truck's air intake with it at the last fuel stop, against something, she hadn't followed the logic and hadn't asked. He pressed the lid back down. He set off behind them, not leading, a half-shape that the starlight refused to commit to, his shape forever about to become a bush or a fence-post or nothing at all.

The cousin Hozan led. Then Narin. Then the rest of them strung out in the grass like a bad idea.

"Down," Hozan said — Bashir relayed it, low — and they went down into the grass, and over the rise the night had a colour to it, a sick electric orange, and a sound: a generator's flat industrial drone, and under it the chunk and scrape of metal in earth.

Edith came up over the lip on her elbows beside Naila and looked.

It was not a dig.

She had photographed digs. She knew what a dig looked like: the string grids, the trowels, the little flags, the unbearable patience of a person on their knees with a brush, the whole grammar of *we will be here for years and we will write down everything.* This had the grammar of a quarry. Floodlights on poles run off the generator, throwing everything into hard orange and black. Three trenches torn straight down through stratigraphy that someone had spent five thousand years laying — she could see it in the lit walls of the cuts, the bands, the dark line of an old floor, the paler line of an old flood, all of it sheared through like a cake by people who only wanted the cherry. Men in the trenches. Men on the spoil heaps with sieves. A lorry, canvas-sided, backed up to the largest cut.

And the sacks.

That was the part. That was the part she would carry. Not the violence of it — she had a high tolerance now for the violence of it, that was the wound, that was the thing the doctor in London had a word for. It was the sacks. Rice sacks, the woven plastic kind, white going grey, the kind you saw stacked outside every shop in the bazaar with flour and bulgur and dried limes in them, and men were carrying them two-handed up out of the trench and swinging them up into the lorry, and the sacks were full of the cradle of writing.

"They're packing tablets in grain sacks," Naila said. Flat. The penlight was off. "Like potatoes."

"Don't," Bashir said, very gently, automatically, because the word had landed wrong in the dark, and then he heard himself and almost laughed and didn't.

Naila didn't laugh. She had gone still in the particular way Edith had learned to read — not the still of calm, the still of a system loading too fast. Her hands were flat on the dirt. The orange light, the generator's drone that never resolved into rhythm, the figures moving without pattern, the smell of diesel: Edith watched the whole night arrive on her at once and saw her decide, by some private arithmetic, to stay.

"Count them," Edith said, low, into the side of Naila's head. Giving her a fence to hold. "The sacks. I can't from here."

A breath. Then: "Nineteen in the lorry. Four at the lip. Two men filling. They've been at it—" the smallest pause, the eyes flicking, doing the thing she did that was not magic, that was just counting faster than the rest of them could "—the spoil heap is wrong for one night. They've been here a week. Minimum. Look at the lorry's tracks, there's a rut, they've parked it there before, the rut has dried."

"A week," Yusuf said, and the word had a whole career in it. He'd come up on Edith's other side. "A week, this close to the line, undisturbed. Somebody's paying for that. The quiet isn't free out here. Somebody bought the quiet."

"Who," said Bashir.

"That's the wrong question." Yusuf's voice had gone very even. "*Who* you can't find. *How* leaves a trail. How does a sack of school-texts about beer rations turn into money? It can't, that's the thing, nobody loots Sumer for the beer rations." A breath. "So you make it about aliens."

Down in the orange light a man straightened from the trench holding something up to the floodlight, turning it, and even at this distance Edith could see it was a cone — a clay cone, a foundation deposit, a thing a king had buried in a wall four thousand years ago so the gods would know who built it — and the man wasn't looking at the writing on it. He was looking at the shape. Whether it would *photograph*.

"There it is," Naila said. "That's the markup."

"What is?" said Edith.

"He's not selling a text. He doesn't read it, he can't read it, the buyer can't read it. He's selling a *story.*" Her voice had found its rail and was running on it now, fast and certain and almost happy, the way she went when the pattern came clear, the cost forgotten in the click of it. "A clay cone in a museum case with a card that says *Foundation deposit of Ur-Nammu, c. 2100 BCE, recording the building of a temple* is worth — what, Yusuf?"

"On a clean market with provenance, a few thousand. Without provenance, on the grey, nothing, because no auction house will touch it."

"Right. But the *same cone*—" and she had it now, the whole shape of it, and Edith felt the grief of the thing pour out of her sideways as delight, because that was how Naila grieved, by understanding "—the same cone, in a streaming documentary, lit blue, with a synth chord, and a man in a leather jacket saying *but mainstream archaeologists CAN'T EXPLAIN how a Bronze Age culture* — what's underneath, the lighting, do it — *and what if this symbol here is in fact a star map* — that cone is worth a fortune. Because now it's not a building record. Now it's a clue. Now it's *contraband from the gods.* And the beautiful part—"

"The ugly part," Edith said.

"The ugly part," Naila agreed, "is that the story makes it *more* sellable and *less* likely to be read by anyone who'd notice it just says a man's name and that he built a wall. The lie is the lock. You can't read a thing that everyone's been told is unreadable."

Frik had been silent the whole walk, which Edith had learned to clock the way you clock a held breath. He was on his stomach, fists working in the grass, jaw tight with the effort of holding, and Edith watched him lose it the way it always went — not a choice, a sneeze, the body overriding the man.

"*Beer rations,*" he said. Too loud. Then his own hand to his own mouth, eyes shutting, the small flinch of a man who'd spent forty years apologising for his own throat. And then, lower, helpless, the truth coming out the way it came out of him, sideways and exact: "Some scribe — Dubsiga's cousin — counting out the *beer*. For the wall crew." A breath. "And these *cunts* are selling him as a spaceman."

It rang in the grass. The generator ate it. Nobody flinched but Frik, who flinched at himself.

"Yeah," Yusuf said, simply, and put a hand flat on Frik's back, between the shoulder blades, and left it there. "Yeah. That's it. That's exactly it."

And Frik went still under the hand, and the night held.

---

Narin had not said anything for a while.

Edith found her a little apart, lower on the slope, on her knees in the grass with her hands open on her thighs, watching, and the watching was a different kind than the rest of theirs. The crew watched the way you watch a thing you've come to understand. Narin watched the way you watch a house burn that you grew up in.

Edith went to her on her knees, slow, telegraphing it, the way you approached a person who'd asked you to ask first. She did not bring the camera up. She left it on its strap, dead weight against her sternum, and she felt the want of it like a tooth.

"They're not even good trenches," Narin said. She didn't look round. "Look. There. The wall — see the line, the pale line, mud-brick — they've cut straight through a wall to get to the floor below, because the floor below is older and older sells. They've destroyed the building to get the bricks out of the way of the floor. The building was the context. Without the building, the floor is just—" her hand turned over, empty "—objects. Loose objects. A man finds a tablet in a floor and you can read the room. You know it was an archive, you know what was kept here, you know who they were. A man finds the same tablet in a sack and you know nothing. You have a *thing.* They are making things out of a city."

"I know," Edith said. Then, because it was true and she had decided to stop hiding behind being a professional: "I was here in 2003."

Narin turned her head at that. Just her head.

"Not *here* here. Baghdad. The museum." Edith kept her eyes on the orange trenches. "I photographed the looting. Day three. They were carrying things out in — the same. Sacks. Cushions. A man with the Warka mask wrapped in a — I never found out if it was a curtain or a flag. I photographed all of it. It ran on the front of three papers. It won a prize." She made herself say the next part. "I have never once stopped that man. In any of the pictures. I have a lot of pictures of him leaving and not one of him not leaving, and the pictures are very good, and I have thought, for twenty years, that taking them well was a kind of — that the beauty was a kind of—"

"Absolution," Narin said.

"Yeah."

Narin looked back at the trenches.

"It's why I didn't want your lens near my grandmother's hand," she said. "You understand. Your prize. Their grief, your prize. The most honest thing the West did with my country was photograph it leaving."

There was nothing to say to that that wouldn't be a defence, so Edith said nothing, which was the new muscle, and it hurt, and she let it.

Below them, the floodlights threw two long shadows up the slope, because a man with a clipboard — a *clipboard*, out here, the banality of it — had walked into the light, and he was not a labourer. Clean shirt. He held up a tablet, an actual tablet, oblong, dark, and turned it under the floodlight, and he had a phone up, and he was photographing it where it was, on site, the trench wall behind it, for someone, somewhere, who wanted to see it *in the ground* before they paid, because *in the ground* was the story, *in the ground* was the markup, *just discovered, never before seen, what the experts don't want you to know.*

Edith's hands came up.

She did not decide it. The camera was up, the eye was to it, the floodlight had given her everything — the trench, the clean shirt, the dark tablet held to the light like an offering, the lorry's canvas catching orange behind, the *whole sentence* of it composed in the frame the way it only ever composed itself maybe twice in a career, the picture that explains the thing, the picture that runs everywhere, the picture with her name under it.

She had the focus. She had the man's face turning a half-degree more into the light. She had the tablet's cuneiform catching as a row of small hard shadows. Three stops down, the floodlight wouldn't blow. One frame. Quiet shutter. Nobody would hear it over the generator.

*The picture that makes my name,* she thought, with the cold clarity of the body that has done this a thousand times, *and the dig coordinates are in it. The wall behind him is in it. Hozan walks these hills. Narin's uncle pours tea three valleys from here. This frame names this place, and the network has a media arm, and a media arm reads the news, and the news would run this, and they would read where it ran, and they would come for the people who could only have stood here.*

The frame was perfect. She held it.

She felt Narin not moving beside her. Not reaching for the camera. Not telling her. Trusting her, or testing her, the two being the same thing here.

She lowered the camera.

She let the man's face turn the rest of the way into the light unphotographed. She let the perfect frame dissolve back into ordinary dark. Her hands were shaking, which she noted and did not stop. She put the lens cap on. The small click of it was the loudest thing she had done all night.

"I'll get the lorry," she said, low, "when it's on the road. No site. No faces. Plate, if there is one. Make of truck. Direction. That's the trail, that's what Yusuf can run. The picture that catches *them* and not you."

Narin looked at her for a long moment in the dark.

"Yes," she said. "That one."

It was not absolution. Edith knew it was not. But it was the first frame she had ever *not* taken because of what the taking would cost someone else, and the not-taking sat in her chest like a coal, hot and clean, and she understood — no, she did not understand, that was the crutch, she would not do that — she *felt*, in the meat of her, that the wound and the cure were the same shape. Beauty as absolution had killed something in her. Maybe witness without consuming would grow it back.

---

The generator changed pitch.

Everyone on the slope felt it before they understood it — Naila's hand was on Edith's wrist, hard, before Edith had registered the change — and then the floodlights died, all of them, and the basin went to moon and the lorry's reverse-lights, red, and there was shouting, fast Arabic, fast Kurdish, a man's voice over it that had authority and no patience.

"They're spooked," Yusuf breathed. "Something spooked them. They're cutting it short. Leaving."

"Did they see us?" Bashir.

"No." Naila, certain. "They saw their own perimeter. Somebody's man up on the far rise stopped checking in. Their pattern broke. They don't know why their pattern broke, they only know it broke, so they run."

And up on the far rise, where the network's perimeter had stopped checking in, there was — Edith brought her old long lens up, the night-glass, slow, no shutter, only to *see* — there was a shape on the skyline that the starlight would not commit to. A shape that was about to become a fence-post, or a juniper, or nothing. It moved along the ridge against the grain of the wind, unhurried, and where it passed, a man with a phone to his ear lowered the phone and looked at it and looked at it and could not, by his whole posture, by the cant of his shoulders that Mira three feet away was already reading and would tell them later, *resolve* it into a man. The network had cameras on that ridge. Edith would learn that, after, from Yusuf, who would learn it from the trail. The network had three trail-cameras on that ridge and a man with a phone, and not one of them, that night, got a clean frame of whatever walked their line and made their watcher come down off the ridge to start his own engine.

She did not photograph it either. She would not have got it. Nobody ever got it.

"They left a man," Mira signed, fast, her hands catching the moonlight, and Bashir voiced it under his breath for Narin, *they left a man, the one with the phone, look how he keeps looking back, he didn't see a person, he saw a* — and Mira's hands stopped, and started, finding it — *a wrong. He saw a wrong and he can't say what.*

The lorry's engine caught. The canvas sides shuddered. Nineteen sacks plus four, Naila had said, twenty-three sacks of the first writing on earth, of grain-counts and god-lists and a scribe's complaint about his fee and somewhere in there, statistically, a school-text where a boy had copied out the flood, the older one, the one with the different mountain and the different birds, the one that was a thousand years old before anyone in Judah was carried off to Babylon to hear it — twenty-three sacks of it grinding away north and west across the dark plain toward the seam in the country where the safe north thinned out and a sack of cradle became, on the far side, *contraband from the gods.*

Edith got the lorry on the road, on the rise, against the last grey of the west. No site. No faces. The plate was mudded over, which told its own story. The make of the truck. Two reflective triangles on the tailgate, one cracked. The direction. The trail, handed to Yusuf, who took the camera screen and looked at it and said, quietly, "Okay. Okay. That I can follow. That I know how to follow," and there was, under the professional flatness, the smallest live thing — a man who had once moved crates like this for the wrong side, who had needed, more than any of them, for the lights in the sky to be true so that his old work might have been about something bigger than money, and who was here instead, in the grass, on his stomach, finding that the real work — *follow the cracked triangle, follow the mudded plate, follow the road that washed out in March* — was the bigger door after all.

---

They walked back to the Beast in the new dark, the generator silent behind them, the basin returned to its insects and its five thousand years of patience.

Naila walked beside Edith and was quiet, which after a thing like that was its own weather, the system unloading, and Edith did not fill the quiet. Then, near the truck, Naila said, to no one, to the grass:

"It's not even the worst of it. The selling. That's just money, money's stupid, money I almost understand." She stopped. Her hands found each other. "The worst of it is — they took those people's name off their own work. A man wrote *I, Ur-Nammu, built this wall,* he carved his *name* into it so the gods would know him in five thousand years, and the gods waited, and we dug him up, and instead of saying his name we said — *aliens did it.* We looked at the most patient act of authorship in human history and we gave the credit to the sky."

Nobody answered her. The Beast's water bag, hung again under the wheel-arch, had begun, very faintly, to weep.

Jakobus was already at the front of it, hood up, doing the thing with the torch in his teeth, separate, a half-shape, the man no camera could hold. Narin went to him — to the truck, to the open hood, the only one of them who could — and stood a moment, and said something low, three valleys from her uncle's awning, in the old tongue, the courtesy handful, and he answered with the same handful, and shut the hood, and wiped his hands on a hanky that came out clean and went back clean, and they got in.

"North and west," Yusuf said, from the back, watching the cracked triangle on his little screen go dark as the lorry crested out of range. "Toward the line."

"Toward the line," Narin said. And then, after a while, with her grandmother's notebook somewhere in her bag and her whole burning country in the back of her throat, quiet enough that only Mira, watching her mouth in the dash-light, was sure of every word: "We have to stop thinking of it as *stealing.* That's not what it is. That's not big enough for what it is."

Bashir half-turned. "Then what is it?"

Narin looked out at the dark plain going by, the grass, the old floors under the grass, the names carved into walls so the gods would remember.

She didn't answer yet.

The Beast carried them on into the night, headlights off, toward the seam.

# Chapter 12 — The Seam

The safehouse was a half-finished concrete shell on the edge of a town Yusuf had stopped trying to name, two floors up a stairwell with no rail, and the room they'd taken had a window with no glass and a view of a sodium light and, under the sodium light, a goat. Yusuf had been watching the goat for an hour. The goat had been watching him back. Of the two of them, the goat seemed more at peace.

Below, the Beast sat in the dark with its hood down and its water bag rehung, drinking nothing, weeping nothing, finally. Jakobus was somewhere out by it, a shape that resolved into a man only when the sodium light caught the rim of his glasses and then unresolved again, the way he did, going quiet and grey at the edge of any frame. Edith had her camera in her lap with the cap on. She'd had the cap on for two days. Yusuf had clocked it without saying anything. They all had.

Naila sat on the floor with her back to the good wall — she always found the good wall, the one with two exits in her sightline — and she had Narin's grandmother's notebook open beside her own, not touching it, just open, two pages of two different hands. She'd been doing the thing she did, the soft thing under her breath, numbers, the base-sixty going round and round. *Sixty, six hundred, three thousand six hundred.* The sar. She'd killed the sar a week and a country ago — *it's a numeral, it's a big round Mesopotamian ten, it's not an orbit* — but she still ran it sometimes, the way you press a bruise.

"I keep coming back to the gold," Yusuf said. To the room. To the goat.

Nobody answered for a moment. Bashir was lying flat on the bare floor with his eyes closed, which was how he listened. Mira sat by the window where the light was, because the light was how she heard, and she was watching Yusuf's mouth.
