"The gold," Yusuf said again, slower, for Mira, turning so she had his face. "Sitchin's whole thing. The Anunnaki come down, they need gold — for their atmosphere, for their dying planet, whatever — and they're too grand to mine it themselves, so they make us. Workers. Out of an ape, with a bit of their own genes spliced in. Made-to-dig." He laughed, once, with no air behind it. "That's the version I bought. That's the version that got me through about four years of nightshifts at a freight desk."

"It's a good story," Bashir said, eyes still shut.

"It's a *great* story." Yusuf heard his own voice go up and brought it down. "It's got everything. We're special. We're engineered. We matter so much the gods crossed the dark to build us. You ever notice that? The whole thing's a love letter to *us.* We're not nothing. We're a *project.*"

Naila stopped the number under her breath. She looked up.

"That's the cheat," she said.

Yusuf waited. With Naila you waited; the thing came when it was built.

"That's the whole cheat in one sentence." She had the two notebooks open and she put a finger on neither of them, hovering, the way she held a pattern before she'd say it. "Sitchin's story makes us *want it.* It flatters. Designed. Wanted. Important enough to build a star-bridge for." She tipped her head at the dark, at the plain out there under the goat-light, the old floors under the new grass. "But that's not what the clay *says.* I read it again on the truck. Atrahasis. Tablet one." She found it in her own book and turned it to him though he couldn't read a word of it, because that wasn't the point; the point was she was holding the real thing. "The little gods — the Igigi — they're the ones digging. Forty years they dig the Tigris and the Euphrates out by hand, by *order,* and they crack. They burn their tools. They march on the big house at night with torches. A *strike.*" She almost smiled. "Forty centuries before anyone had a word for it. And so the great gods — to break the strike — kill one of their own, a god named We-ila, and they mix his blood and his *spirit* into clay, and the womb-goddess pinches off fourteen lumps, and that's us. That's what we're for." She closed the book. "Toil. We're a strike-breaker. We're made of clay and a murdered god so the gods don't have to dig their own canals."

The room held still. The sodium light buzzed.

"That's *worse,*" Yusuf said. It came out small.

"Yes," Naila said. Not unkindly. "It's much worse."

"I mean—" He scrubbed a hand down his face. "I mean it's *bleaker.* Nobody crossed the dark for us. We weren't a project. We were a *labour solution.* Clay and blood, get to work, you'll die, the gods will sleep better." He surprised himself with the shape of it in his mouth. "That's not a love letter. That's a—"

"A foreman's memo," Bashir said, from the floor, and opened one eye, and there was no cruelty in it.

"A foreman's memo," Yusuf agreed, and something in his chest did a thing he didn't have a clean name for, half a laugh and half the other.

Mira had been reading Yusuf's face the whole way down, and now she signed something to him — slow, because his sign was bad and she was patient — and when he didn't catch it she spelled the last word into the air with her hand, *G-R-I-E-V-I-N-G,* and pointed at him, eyebrows up. A question, not a verdict.

Yusuf looked at the goat.

"Yeah," he said. To Mira, to her face, so she had it. "Yeah. I think I am."

---

It would have been easier, Naila thought, watching him, if the real one were boring. That was the lie the genre lived on — that the choice was *aliens* or *nothing,* magic or homework, the wonder or the dust. As if when you took the spaceship away you were left holding a school-text about beer rations and a hangover. Bashir kept saying it on the truck, the loneliest true thing she knew: *every alien decode is a bad translation.* And the corollary, which was the whole reason she could stand any of this — that a *good* translation didn't leave you with less. It left you with a four-thousand-year-old labour dispute. It left you with a strike.

She did the arithmetic of it almost without meaning to, the way other people hummed. Atrahasis: older than Genesis by — she ran it — over a thousand years on the conservative copies, more on the oldest. Enuma Elish, the Babylon one, where the warrior-god Marduk splits the salt-sea goddess Tiamat like a fish for drying and builds the sky from one half and the ground from the other and then makes mankind from the blood of a *second* murdered god, the rebel Kingu — that one a little later, but the bones of it old, old, the bones of it everywhere. The clay-and-blood. The slain god. The toil. It wasn't one text. It was a *tradition.* You could lay the copies out across centuries and watch the same skeleton get re-dressed for each new empire, and the skeleton was always the same:

*The gods were tired. So they made us to be tired in their place.*

That was the thing the West had taken. Not a tablet. Not even a story, exactly. The *authorship.*

"It's not stealing," she said, out loud, picking up the thread Narin had dropped in the truck two nights ago and left hanging in the dark. "Yusuf, you said it. Narin said it. *Stealing's not big enough.*"

"Then what is it." Bashir sat up. He'd been waiting on this; she could see he had the same itch she did, the one where a word was *almost* there and your whole skin wanted it.

Naila put her two notebooks side by side, the dead language and her grandmother's-equivalent, her own hand.

"There's a man," she said. "Swiss. Von Däniken. He's the one who really lit the fuse, before Sitchin, the sixties — *Chariots of the Gods.* And his actual argument, when you strip the airport bookshop off it, is one sentence. He looks at the Nazca lines, the pyramids, the Sumerian king lists, the canals — all of it, every wonder built by brown and Black and ancient hands — and he says: *they couldn't have done this.*"

The room had gone very quiet now, a different quiet.

"That's it," Naila said. "That's the engine. *These people were too primitive.* The lines are too straight, the stones too big, the maths too good — for *them.* So someone came down and did it. Someone from outside. Someone—" she didn't say it, let it sit, "—from *up.*"

"Whiter," Bashir said softly. Stateless, switched-tongues-at-the-border Bashir, who had crossed more lines than the rest of them put together. "Up is whiter. Up is always whiter."

"It's the oldest move there is," Naila said. "It's the colonial move with the serial numbers filed off. You arrive, you see the city, the city is too good, so you decide the people in it didn't build it — a lost race did, a vanished superior, the *real* architects, who happen to look more like the man holding the notebook. And then the people who actually quarried the stone and cut the canals and *invented writing* —" her voice didn't rise; it went flatter, which was worse, which was the thing she did when it was real, "— they become the squatters. The tenants. The ones who found the keys to a house they could never have made."

She looked at the window, at the plain.

"That's what was done here. Sumer invents the *written word.* The first poem. The first law. The first joke we can still read. And we looked at it and decided they were too primitive to have done it, so we gave it to the sky." She closed both books. "It's not theft. Theft's a thing you can give back. This took the *making* away from the makers. It un-authored a civilisation."

Frik had been quiet in the corner the whole time — Frik went quiet when it mattered, that was the secret nobody knew about Frik, that the blurts came when it *didn't* — and now his head jerked once and his shoulder rose and he said, fast, helpless, in the voice that wasn't choosing it:

"They wrote it *down.*"

It hung there.

"They wrote it down," Frik said again, slower, and this time it was him, riding the back of the tic, claiming it. "That's the — that's the thing nobody — you can't *un-write* it. It's in the clay. It's their *hand* in the clay. You can lie about it but you can't—" his neck pulled, his eyes squeezed, "—you can't get their *hand* off it."

"No," Naila said. "You can't."

And Mira, watching all their faces from the window, in the light, saw Yusuf's do the thing again, the half-and-half, and she signed nothing this time, just held his eye and put her flat hand to her own chest and pressed it there. *I see it.* Yusuf nodded. The goat, outside, ate something off the ground and did not care.

---

Narin came up the stairwell with no rail like she'd been born to it, ducked the lintel, and stood in the doorway with two glasses of tea she'd got from God-knew-where in a half-built town at midnight, the way she always could, the local always could, and she handed one to Naila and one — after a beat that was a decision — to Edith, whose camera-cap had been on for two days.

Edith took the tea like it weighed something.

Narin had heard the last of it from the stairs; you could tell. She didn't perform having heard it. She just leaned in the doorway and let the steam off her glass go up into the sodium light.

"You're close," she said. To all of them, in English, her London English with the Nineveh under it. "You read the pattern. The same flood, the same clay, the same council of gods, the same strike. You read it across the cultures and you saw the skeleton and you named what the aliens did to it." She drank. "That's good. That's real work. Most people who come to my office with the word *Anunnaki* in their mouth can't do half of it."

"But," Bashir said. Quietly. The way you say *but* to someone who's earned it.

"But you're reading it like a crime," Narin said. "Sitchin took it. Von Däniken un-authored it. The looters down there pack it into grain sacks. The doc people up in their offices sell it back to the world with a spaceship glued on. All theft. All loss. The clay goes out the door and the hand goes off it." She set her glass on the windowsill, careful, near Mira so Mira had her mouth. "And that's all true. I've spent twenty years being angry about exactly that. I watched my own museum go out the door in sacks in 2003." She didn't look at Edith when she said it; she very precisely didn't, which was its own kind of looking. "So believe me, I know the inventory of what's been lost."

She picked the glass back up.

"But you've stopped one country too early."

Naila's head came up.

"You all keep saying it went *out,*" Narin said. "Out the door. Into the market. Into the lie. And it did. But that's not the only direction this story ever travelled." She turned the glass in her hand. "You're sitting in the room thinking about where the tablets *go.* I'm asking where the *story* went. Not the looted object. The thing Frik says you can't get the hand off. The flood. The clay. The murdered god. The seven days of toil before the gods rest." A small pause on that last; Naila clocked it, didn't know yet why. "Where did *that* go, after Babylon?"

"It—" Yusuf started, and stopped, because he didn't actually know, and the not-knowing was a clean, cold, exciting thing in him, the first clean thing in days.

"You know what happened to Babylon," Narin said. "Six hundred years before anyone's Christ. The empire comes — and it takes a people, a small people, a people with a god and a temple and not much else, and it marches them off into exile. Out of their hills. Into the great brick city. Into the libraries. Into the *liturgy.* Into the place where every educated man and woman knew Enuma Elish the way you all know — what, song lyrics. By heart. Recited at the new year. *Marduk splits the sea-goddess, builds the sky from her ribs, makes the slaves from a dead god's blood.*" She let it sit. "And the exiles sat in that city for two generations. Hearing it. Knowing it. *Made of clay to bear the gods' toil.*"

The sodium light buzzed. The goat had lain down.

"And then," Narin said, "some of them wrote something."

Naila felt it land before she had the words — felt the pattern *click,* the way it did, two pages of two hands sliding into register. She put her finger down at last, on the dead language and on her grandmother's hand, both, at once.

"Oh," she said. Just that.

"You said it un-authored a civilisation," Narin said to her, gentle, the local out-reading them all and choosing to do it kindly. "I want to show you a civilisation that got *re-authored.* By the people the empire tried to delete. With the empire's own furniture." She finished her tea. "There's a flood story you all know. Older than you think — *no.* Not older. The *Mesopotamian* one is older, by a thousand years and more, that part you've got right, don't let anyone tell you the other way round. But there's a *second* flood, written down by a conquered people with the first one ringing in their ears, and it is *not the same,* and the places it's not the same are the whole point." A flicker of the scholar's fierce joy, the thing the West had aliened out of her ancestors and she was about to hand back. "Different mountain. Different birds. Different boat. Different god — and the *difference of the god* is the argument. They're not copying. They're *answering.*"

"Genesis," Bashir said. The word came out of him soft and enormous.

"Genesis," Narin said. And then, before anyone could fill the room with the wrong thing, before Yusuf's face could fall the wrong way or Edith's hand could go to a camera that would steal it, she put up one finger and held them there. "And I want to be very careful with you now, because the cheap version of where I'm taking you is just as much a theft as Sitchin's. The man who says *aha, the Bible's plagiarism, it's all just stolen myth* — he's doing the exact same move von Däniken did. He's un-authoring it. He's getting the hand off the clay." She looked, for a second, almost tired. "Both of those men think they're the clever one in the room. They're the same man."

"Then what is it," Naila said. The question of the whole book, in three words, in her flat clear voice.

Narin smiled, and it was the smile of someone who had waited a long time for the right people to ask.

"That," she said, "is the question." She stood off the doorframe. "It's not a copy and it's not a coincidence. It's a *conversation.* A conquered people sat in the heart of the empire that ate them, and they took the empire's biggest, oldest, most sacred story — *the gods made you out of mud to be their slaves* — and they wrote it back, line for line, furniture and all, and they changed the verb. Not *made to toil.* Made in the—" she stopped herself, deliberate, a teacher refusing to give the answer the night before the lesson. "No. You'll read it. In the morning. In the light. I'm not handing it to you in a concrete box at midnight like it's contraband. It deserves better than the way the *other* tablets travel."

She looked at the window, at the dark plain, the old floors under the new grass, the same long look she'd given the night the Beast carried them out.

"You wanted to know what was bigger than *stealing,*" she said. "Here it is. Somebody downstairs is packing the flood into a grain sack to sell with a spaceship glued on. And three thousand years ago, somebody who'd lost everything took the same flood and used it to keep their soul. Same story. Two directions." She turned for the stairwell. "One of those is the saddest thing I know. The other one is why I do this."

At the lintel she paused.

"Mira," she said, and faced her full-on, mouth clear in the light, and made the sign — clumsy, learned-for-this, the way they all signed for each other, badly and on purpose — for *thank you.* Because Mira had been watching her face the whole time, reading the thing under the lecture, the grief and the joy braided so tight you couldn't pull one out without the other, and Narin had felt herself being read, and had let it happen.

Mira signed it back. Cleaner. *Thank you.*

Then Narin ducked the lintel and went down the stairwell with no rail, and her footsteps faded, and outside, far down, you could just hear — for a moment, no longer — three notes of something hummed low and steady by a grey shape near the Beast, a metronome for a man no light could hold, and then that faded too.

---

Yusuf sat with the goat-light on him and the cold tea in his hands and the foreman's memo in his chest, and waited for the grief to be the only thing in there.

It wasn't.

That was the part he hadn't seen coming, on all those nightshifts, with the spaceship and the splice and the *we're a project.* He'd thought the rabbit-hole was the door out of an ordinary life, and he'd thought that letting go of the aliens meant the door swung shut and you were back at the freight desk with nothing but dust. He'd grieved it on the truck. He was grieving it now, for real, the way Mira had named.

But there was a draught coming under the other door. He could feel it. *Same story, two directions.* A conquered people who'd taken the empire's own myth and turned it into a way to survive the empire. He didn't have it yet — Narin had pulled her hand back, *in the morning, in the light,* and she was right to, he'd have grabbed at it and broken it — but he could feel the size of the room on the far side. It was bigger than the spaceship. The spaceship had only ever flattered him. This thing didn't flatter anybody. It had been written by people in chains and it was *braver* than anything he'd believed for four years on the nightshift.

Naila had her two notebooks closed in her lap, and her eyes shut, and she was running it under her breath again, but it wasn't the sar this time. It was something new, a count he couldn't follow, the rhythm of a thing that hadn't been read yet and was about to be.

"Bashir," she said, eyes still shut.

"Mm."

"The flood story. The Genesis one. You've read it in—"

"All of them," Bashir said, simply. The polyglot's loneliness and the polyglot's gift, one sentence. "Hebrew. Greek. Syriac. Aramaic. The Sumerian's the only place I have to crawl."

"Tomorrow," Naila said. "In the light. The two of them. Side by side. The mountain and the birds and the boat. Where they're the same and where they—"

"Answer," Bashir said.

"Answer," she agreed.

Frik, in the corner, jerked once and said, very quietly, to no one, riding the tic and then choosing the last of it himself the way he'd learned to in this room and nowhere else on earth:

"Same flood."

Nobody corrected him, because he wasn't wrong, and nobody finished it for him, because he didn't need them to.

Outside, the sodium light buzzed, and the goat slept, and the plain stretched off to a horizon they couldn't see, full of old floors and old names cut into walls so the gods would remember — and three thousand years of a story that had gone two directions out of this same mud, one toward a grain sack, one toward the morning.

In the morning, in the light, they would read the other flood.

# Chapter 13 — The Same Flood

The light came up grey and then gold, and Bashir was awake before any of them, which was usual, because the first hour of a day in a new place was the hour nobody talked, and the hour nobody talked was the only hour he ever truly rested. He sat on the cool of the unfinished step with a tin mug of tea that Yusuf had made too sweet, and he watched the plain take its colour back from the dark, and he ran the two stories through his head the way you'd run two threads through your fingers to feel where they were spun the same and where they parted.

He had told Naila the truth last night. He had read the flood in all of them. Hebrew with the *vav* doing its quiet work, the *and-and-and* that made the prose move like water itself. Greek with the Septuagint's careful sandals on. Syriac, his grandmother's church-tongue, the one that still wept the old words out on Good Friday. He could read the flood in his sleep. The Sumerian was the only place he had to crawl, and crawling was where the love was.

Inside, the others were beginning. He heard Mira's bare feet, which made no sound he could name but which moved the air, and then her hand on his shoulder, light, a question. He turned so she could see his mouth.

"Tea's poison," he said. "Yusuf made it."

She read his lips and her face did the thing it did, the small flat amusement that lived mostly in the eyes, and she signed something fast and sloppy that he didn't catch, and he gave her the open palm, *again, slower*, and she did it again, and it was *coward*, and she took the mug out of his hand and drank the poison herself, watching him over the rim, daring him.

Narin came out last. She had a thermos of her own — she'd brought it from London, real tea, leaves, the kind you let breathe — and a laptop under one arm and, under the other, a thing wrapped in a tea-towel that turned out to be a stack of printouts, the British Museum's own photographs, the tablets shot from above under raking light so every wedge stood up in its own little shadow.

She did not say good morning. She set the printouts down on the upturned crate they'd been using as a table and looked at the seven of them — six and herself — gathering in the cool concrete shade with their mugs, and Bashir watched her decide, again, whether to do this. She decided it fresh every morning. He respected that. A door you open carelessly is a door you've stopped guarding.

"I'm going to say something first," Narin said, "and then we read."

They waited.

"There is a stupid version of what we're about to do," she said. "The stupid version is a man on the internet with a graphic. Left side, Genesis. Right side, Gilgamesh. Big arrow. *PLAGIARISM.* Caption: *they don't want you to know.*" She let that sit. "I've spent twenty years cleaning that man's fingerprints off my field. He is, in his way, the same animal as the alien man. He needs the two texts to be *identical*, because identical is loud, and loud is what gets clicks. And identical is a lie." She tapped the printout. "They are not identical. They are *related*. Those are completely different sentences and the difference is the whole entire point. If anybody in this room says *the Bible just copied it* I will pack the truck myself. Are we clear."

"Clear," said Yusuf, quietly, and Bashir saw that it cost Yusuf to say it, because Yusuf had been, for four years, exactly the man who needed it loud, and was learning, slowly, painfully, in front of all of them, to need it true instead.

"Good." Narin pulled the first sheet to the top. "George Smith. 1872. Tell them, Bashir, you'll do it better than me."

He hadn't expected the handoff. She did that — gave you the thing you were best at and then watched to see if you'd take it greedily or hold it open. He held it open.

"George Smith was a banknote engraver," Bashir said. "Working class. London. No university. He used to spend his lunch breaks at the British Museum looking at the tablets behind the glass, the ones nobody could read yet, and he taught himself cuneiform off them. Off the *glass*. The way you'd learn a language by reading the menu in a restaurant you can't afford to eat in." He felt his own grandmother in his mouth when he said it, the autodidact ache, the man who belonged everywhere they'd let him stand. "And one day in 1872 he's working through a broken tablet from Nineveh, from Ashurbanipal's library, and he's reading along — and the line says a ship has come to rest on a mountain, and a man sends out a dove, and the dove finds no resting place and comes back."

He stopped, because the next part deserved the stop.

"And Smith — the story goes — Smith stood up. In the reading room. And he started taking his clothes off."

Frik made a sound, a short bark of a laugh that was half tic and half delight, and nobody minded it because it was exactly the right size for the thing.

"He was overcome," Bashir said. "He'd found a flood. Older than the one in his Bible. With a dove in it. He ran around the room. They thought he'd lost his mind. He hadn't. He'd just touched the oldest written wonder anybody had touched in a very long time, and the body does what the body does." He looked at Frik when he said it, on purpose, gently, and Frik looked back, and something passed between them that did not need a word. *The body does what the body does.* "He gave a lecture about it that November. The Prime Minister came. Gladstone. To hear a banknote engraver read a poem older than Moses."

"And then," Narin said, picking it up, dry as the plain, "the newspaper that funded his dig back to Nineveh wanted a headline, so the find got sold as *proof of the Bible*. Which it wasn't. And the man who actually read it spent his short life" — she didn't soften this — "trying to say the more interesting thing over the noise of the simpler one. He died at thirty-six. Dysentery. On the way home from the field. The loud version outlived him by a hundred and fifty years." She squared the printouts. "Now. The poem."

She read it to them. Not from the laptop — from the printout, with her finger moving under the wedges, translating live, and Bashir watched her do it and felt the specific envy of a man watching someone do, in a language he had to crawl in, the thing he could only fly in elsewhere. She read Utnapishtim telling Gilgamesh how the gods resolved to drown the world. How the god Ea, who could not break the oath of silence outright, whispered the warning *to a wall* — to a reed wall — so that the man on the other side of the wall would overhear it and could not later be said to have been *told*.

"That's a lawyer's god," Yusuf said, and laughed, a real one, surprised out of him. "He found the loophole."

"He found the loophole," Narin agreed. "Tear down the house, the wall says. Build a boat. Abandon wealth, seek life. Take the seed of all living things aboard." She moved her finger. "And the man builds it. Seven decks. A perfect cube, near enough — the dimensions are a riddle, very deliberate, the boat is *too* square to be a real boat, which is one of the ways you know you're inside a poem and not a logbook." She glanced up. "Hold that. The square boat. We'll come back to it."

Mira was watching Narin's hands and Narin's mouth both, and when Narin paused, Mira's own hands moved — to Bashir, fast, *the boat shape*, two flat palms making a box, a question in her eyebrows.

"Different boat," Bashir signed back, badly, and said it aloud for the room. "Mira wants the boats compared. They're different. The ark in Genesis is a long boat, a ratio, three hundred by fifty by thirty cubits — that's a *ship* shape, that floats. Utnapishtim's is a cube. Atrahasis, the older one again, the one we read last night — his might be round. A coracle. There's a tablet, found this century, that gives the recipe for a *round* ark, coiled rope and bitumen, like the boats they actually still made on these rivers into living memory." He felt the wonder of it move up his arms. "Three floods. Three different boats. That's not one blueprint photocopied three times. That's three poets, a thousand years apart, telling the *same true frightening thing* — the water came, a man was warned, the seed survived — and each one building the boat his own way."

"Which is exactly what I needed nobody to forget," Narin said, and there was approval in it, brief, real, gone. "Keep going. The water comes."

She read the water. The storm-gods harnessed. The dykes broken. The land shattered like a pot. And then the part Bashir had been waiting for, because it was the part that did the work no graphic could do — the part where the gods themselves, the very gods who had sent the flood, cowered.

*The gods were frightened by the deluge,* Narin read, slowing. *They drew back, they went up to the heaven of Anu. The gods crouched like dogs, crouched against the outer wall.*

The room was quiet.

"They made the storm," Yusuf said, "and then they were scared of it."

"They're not in control," Narin said. "That's the theology of it. The Mesopotamian gods are *inside* the weather, not above it. They're hungry. They're loud. They squabble and they regret and they cower. The goddess Belet-ili — the mother goddess, the one who made humankind — she cries out that she let it happen, she wails that her people have filled the sea like fish. The gods didn't drown the world because the world was wicked. They drowned it because" — she checked the line, because she was Narin and she would not round it off — "in the older version, the real reason, the *human noise.* People multiplied and made too much noise and the head god couldn't sleep."

Frik, who had been still, said: "*Couldn't sleep.*" Echolalia, riding it out, and then the choosing-the-end-of-it that he did only here: "That's the *reason*?"

"That's the reason," Narin said, and she did not laugh at it and she did not let them laugh at it either, and Bashir saw why. Because the next thing she said put a floor under the whole morning.

"Now hold that god — the god who drowns the world to get some sleep, the gods who cower like dogs against the wall — and hold him there, and we'll go to the other flood." She set the Gilgamesh printout aside, gently, the way you'd set down something that had earned its keep, and she opened a worn paperback with a black cover, and Bashir saw it was a Bible, an old one, the Syriac-and-English she'd carried out of London under the printouts, and he understood that she was about to read her own scripture in a room of strangers and that this was the part she decided fresh every morning.

She read Genesis. The flood. And she read it *as a believer reads it*, with the weight in the right places, and she read it *as a scholar reads it*, with her finger under the seams — and Bashir, who could read it in five tongues, sat very still, because watching someone do both of those at once, in one breath, without either one eating the other, was the rarest reading he knew, and he had been to a great many readings.

"Different reason," she said, when she came to it. "Read it. The reason here is not noise. The reason here is *moral.* The earth was filled with violence. And it grieved God in his heart that he had made man." She looked up. "Do you hear it? In the poem, the gods are annoyed. Here, God is *grieved.* The same flood — and they've put a soul in the engine. That is not a copy. That is an *argument.*"

She turned the page.

"And here," she said. "Watch the boat get a number that the other boats don't have. Genesis tells you to bring the animals in twos. And then — this is the part the loud man with the graphic always skips — it *also* says bring seven of some, the clean ones, seven pairs. Two different counts, side by side, in the same chapter." She let that hang for the scholars in the room, which was all of them now. "There are two flood accounts here, stitched together. You can see the stitch. A reader from the south, a reader from the temple, woven into one. The tradition was already arguing with *itself* before it ever got written down. That's not weakness in the text. That's the text being *old* and *honest* about being old."

"Two scribes," Naila said. It was the first thing she'd said all morning. She'd been sitting against the cool wall with her knees up and her two notebooks shut on her lap and her eyes half-closed, doing the count under her breath, and now she opened her eyes. "Two scribes and you can see the join. Like a tablet copied by two hands and the second hand's wedges sit deeper." She wasn't asking. She was placing it, snapping it into the lattice where it went, the pattern under the pattern. "It's the same as the math. The seam shows. The seam's the most honest part."

"The seam's the most honest part," Narin repeated, and looked at Naila a beat longer than she'd looked at any of them, the look of one careful reader recognising another across a table, and then she moved on before it could become a thing, because she was kind. "Now. The birds."

This was the part Bashir had wanted since the step at dawn. He leaned in.

Narin laid the two printouts side by side, the wedges and the verses, and put a finger on each.

"Utnapishtim," she said, "sends out a dove. The dove flies, finds no resting place, comes back. Then he sends a swallow. The swallow finds no resting place, comes back. Then he sends a raven. And the raven" — she paused — "does not come back. Because the raven has found the water gone down. So the man knows it's safe."

She moved her other finger.

"Noah sends out a raven first. The raven goes to and fro until the waters dry. Then he sends a dove. The dove finds no rest, comes back. He waits seven days. Sends the dove again. It comes back with an olive leaf in its beak. He waits seven more days. Sends it a third time. It does not return."

She looked up at the room.

"Same idea," she said. "Send a bird to read the world for you, because the bird can land where the man can't. *Exactly* the same idea, a thousand years apart, on two sides of the same river-world. And" — the scalpel — "*different birds, in a different order, doing different things.* Dove, swallow, raven. Raven, dove, dove, dove. One of them carries an olive leaf — that's Genesis only, that's a new thing, that's *hope on the wing*, and it's not in the poem at all. The loud man would put both lists in a box and draw an equals sign. There is no equals sign. There's a family resemblance. There's a grandmother's nose on a grandchild's face." She straightened. "If you want to feel how close two stories can be while being two stories — read the birds."

Bashir found his throat had gone tight and he didn't entirely know why, except that it was the leaf. The whole horror of the drowned world and the cowering gods and the human noise, and a poet a thousand years downstream of all of it had reached into the wreckage and given the bird a green leaf to carry back, a thing that grows, and Bashir thought that whoever did that had done it on *purpose*, had taken the old terrible flood and bent it, just slightly, toward the morning — and he could not have explained it to anyone who hadn't read both, and that was the loneliness, and that was also the gift.

"And then," Narin said, "the part that ends the morning."

She read the end of the poem. The flood subsides. The boat grounds on the mountain — *Nimush*, she said, *a real peak, you can argue about which one, somewhere up toward the Zagros, not Ararat, a different mountain entirely, hold that too* — and Utnapishtim opens a window and lets out the birds and then he comes down and he makes a sacrifice. He pours out a libation. He sets up offering vessels and burns reed and cedar and myrtle.

And the gods, she read, smelled the savour. The gods smelled the sweet savour.

*And the gods,* she read, *gathered like flies over the offering.*

She set the printout down.

"Like flies," she said.

Nobody moved.

"They've been crouched against the wall like dogs," she said, "scared of the storm they made, *starving*, because nobody's been feeding them, because all the people who fed them are drowned. And the one man left alive lights a fire and the smell of meat goes up and the gods come down on it" — her hand made the smallest gesture, a descent, a swarming — "like flies on a carcass. That's the line. A thousand years before Genesis. The gods are *hungry,* and they need us, and when they smell the smoke they come down like flies."

She let it land all the way down.

"Now hear what Genesis does with the same picture," she said, and turned the page, and read it. Noah builds an altar. He takes of every clean beast and offers burnt offerings. *And the LORD smelled a sweet savour.* The same image — the smoke goes up, the smell reaches heaven. "Same furniture," Narin said softly. "The savour. The offering. The smell rising. The single survivor at the altar. And then watch the camera move. There are no flies. There is no swarm. There is no hungry god. There is one God who smells the offering and says in his heart that he will not curse the ground again for man's sake. The picture is inherited" — she touched the wedges, touched the verses — "and the meaning is *overturned.* They took the hungry gods and they refused them. They kept the smoke and they threw away the flies."

Bashir realised he was leaning so far forward his tea had gone cold in his hand.

"That," Narin said, "is what it means to say Genesis has a genealogy. Not theft. Not a copy. A people in exile — *here*, by these rivers, in Babylon, six hundred years before Christ, sat in the captial of the empire that had burned their temple and dragged them east — and they sat in the empire's own library, with the empire's own flood story open in front of them, the gods like flies and the noise and the cube boat — and they *answered it.* Line by line. Picture by picture. They took the most powerful story their conquerors had, the story Babylon told to say *this is how the world is*, and they rebuilt it to say *no. Not flies. Not noise. Not a god who can't sleep. A God who grieves, and a covenant, and a leaf.*" She closed the black book. "They kept their soul by re-authoring the empire's own myth. That's the most extraordinary thing I know how to put in front of you. And it's a thousand times braver than aliens."

Frik said it then. He'd been holding it, Bashir could see, jaw working, the tic riding up under it, and when it came it came clean, the involuntary truth that he could not paint over and so never did:

"*They wrote back.*"

"They wrote back," Narin said, and for the first time that morning she smiled, properly, at Frik, who had said in two words the thing she'd spent twenty years and a whole dawn trying to say.

Mira had her hands over her own heart, flat, and then she signed something to the room, slow enough that even Bashir caught all of it, and he said it aloud because that was the deal, that was always the deal, nobody got talked over here: "Mira says — what did they not say." He frowned. "She means — in the answer. What did the exiles leave *out*, on purpose, that we can only see if we already know what they were answering."

The room turned to her. Narin turned to her, and Bashir watched the Assyriologist actually stop, the way she had not stopped for the loud man or the alien man or any of them, and look at the deaf woman who read the unsaid, and consider the question as a real question, the best one of the morning.

"That," Narin said slowly, "is the right question. And it's the next one." She gathered the printouts, the wedges and the verses both, squared them under the tea-towel. "Because the flood is where they *kept* the picture and bent it. There's another place — the very beginning, the creation, the waters and the deep — where they kept a single *word* and refused everything around it. Where Babylon's god makes the world by *butchering* a goddess and splitting her body — and Genesis makes it by *speaking.*" She tucked the bundle under her arm. "Combat against word. That one's not a family resemblance. That one's a fight. We'll do it tonight, in the cool, when the light's gone flat and nobody's eyes hurt."

Outside, near the Beast, Bashir could see Jakobus through the doorway, doing some slow patient thing to the water bag that hung off the truck's flank and wept its single bright drop into the dust, keeping itself cold the way it had kept itself cold across a different and farther desert. He had his back to them. He had been, Bashir was fairly sure, within earshot of the whole reading and had not come in, because the morning belonged to Narin and to the dead poets and to the people who lived here now, and he knew it, and so he stayed outside with the truck and the slow drip and let it.

Naila had her notebooks open again. She wasn't running the sar. She was making two columns, the way she did, and at the top of one she'd written, in her square small hand, *flies,* and at the top of the other she'd written nothing yet, because the other column was the silence — the thing they'd left out — and you don't fill a column like that until you know what it was answering.

"Tonight," she said, to no one, to the count, to the lattice closing its next gap. "The water that gets divided."

"Tonight," Bashir agreed, and went to make new tea, the right way, with leaves, because somebody had to, and because the morning had earned it.

# Chapter 14 — By Word, Not Combat

The light went flat the way Narin had promised it would.

Naila felt it before she saw it — the heat coming off its high white edge and slumping into something the skin could stand, the concrete giving its hoarded sun back to the room a degree at a time. Outside, the call to prayer wound up from somewhere across the rooftops, threaded the unfinished window-holes, and the diesel generator two doors down kept its low animal idle going underneath, and the two sounds did not agree and did not need to. She liked the hour. It had no glare in it. Glare was a kind of noise.

They'd dragged the plastic chairs into a loose ring around the camp lantern. Yusuf had found a second lantern somewhere — Yusuf found things — and set it on the floor, so the light came up from below and made everybody's faces strange and tall-shadowed, like figures pressed into a seal and rolled out across a tablet.

Narin had her laptop open on her knees, the British Museum photographs queued, but she hadn't turned it toward them yet. She was waiting. She had a teacher's instinct for the held breath.

"Tonight," she said, "I'm going to break a rule I made you keep this morning."

Bashir, cross-legged on a folded blanket, looked up. "You said *identical equals lie.*"

"I did. And I meant it." She set the laptop aside on the floor, screen down, so the only thing on offer for the moment was her own face. "This one isn't a resemblance. This one's a fight. And I want you to see the fight before I let you anywhere near the things people get wrong about it."

Naila uncapped her pen. New page. She drew the line down the middle without thinking about it, the way her hand drew lines down the middle of things, and at the top of the left column she wrote *combat.* At the top of the right column she wrote nothing.

The right column was for what the fight was *for.* You don't fill that one early.

---

"Enuma Elish," Bashir said, and the words came out of him the way they always did when a language opened its own door — easier than English, smoother, his mouth glad of the work. "*When on high.* That's the title, which isn't a title, it's just the first two words, because that's how they named things. *Enūma eliš lā nabû šamāmū* — when the heavens above were not yet named."

"Not yet *named,*" Narin repeated, and let it hang. "Hold that. We'll need it later."

Naila wrote *named* in the right column, faint, in pencil, because she wasn't sure yet what it was answering.

"It's Babylon's creation poem," Narin went on. "Seven tablets. Recited at the New Year festival, every spring, out loud, in the Esagila — Marduk's temple, in the city of Babylon. Imagine that. Not a book on a shelf. A thing the king himself processes through the streets to hear, while the river comes up and the fields drink. It is *liturgy.* It is the city telling itself why the city gets to exist."

"And why does the city get to exist?" Yusuf asked. He had his elbows on his knees. He'd gone quiet and forward in the lantern light, the way he did when a story had a shape he hadn't expected, and Naila clocked it — Yusuf leaning in was its own data point, Yusuf who'd come into all this needing the bigness, the spacefarers, the secret kings. He leaned into the dust-version now the same way. She didn't think he'd noticed yet that he was doing it.

"Because Marduk won a war," Narin said.

She picked the laptop back up. Turned it. The first plate came up — not the poem itself, a relief, a thing of muscle and wing, a god striding with the lightning gripped in his fist like a sheaf of reeds, and a great coiling horror underfoot, all jaw and serpent-loop.

"At the start there's only water," Narin said. "Two waters. Apsu — the fresh water, the sweet water, the deep that wells up under the ground. And Tiamat — the salt water, the sea. They mingle. From the mingling come the gods, generation on generation, and the young gods are *loud.* They dance, they make noise, they disturb the old quiet of the deep. Apsu wants to kill them all so he can sleep. The young gods kill him first. And Tiamat — the sea, the mother — rises to avenge her husband."

The relief held the room. Frik had stopped tapping his knee, which for Frik was a kind of held breath of its own.

"She makes an army." Narin's voice didn't perform it; she let the thing be enormous on its own. "Eleven monsters. Serpent-dragons, the scorpion-man, the bull-man, the great lion, demons with venom for blood. She puts a consort over them, a god named Kingu, and she gives Kingu the Tablet of Destinies — fastens it to his chest — and that's the rule of the world made portable. And the young gods are terrified. The high gods, the wise ones, they go out one by one to face her and they come back white. Nobody can stand against the sea."

"And Marduk says he'll do it," Bashir said quietly, because he knew the shape of it.

"Marduk says he'll do it. For a price." Narin's mouth tightened, almost a smile, almost not. "He's young. He's the storm-god, the city-god of Babylon, which a few centuries before this was nobody's idea of a capital. And he says: *I'll fight your monster. But if I win, I'm in charge. Forever. My word fixes fate. You bow.* And they're so frightened they agree. They throw a feast, they get drunk on the strong beer, they test his power by telling him to destroy a constellation and recreate it with a word — and he does — and they hand him everything."

Naila wrote, in the left column, *power = won, not given.* Then she underlined *won* twice, because that was the whole hinge and she could already feel where the right column was going to swing against it.

"So he goes out to the sea," Narin said. "And this is the part. This is the engine of the whole thing." She didn't raise her voice. She lowered it. "He takes the four winds. He makes a net to catch her. He fills his body with flame. He rides the storm-chariot, and he rolls out the lightning, and when Tiamat opens her mouth to swallow him — he drives the evil wind in so she *can't close it.* Her belly distends. And he shoots the arrow in through the open mouth and splits her heart."

The generator coughed, two doors down, and steadied.

"Then he stands on her corpse," Narin said, "and he splits her. Like a fish split for drying. He takes the top half and raises it up and makes the sky, and he sets a bar across it and posts a guard, and he tells the guard: *do not let her waters out.* He takes the bottom half and makes the earth. Her eyes become the sources of the two rivers — the Tigris, the Euphrates. Her breasts become the mountains. He bends her tail up into the sky to make the Milky Way. The whole world, the dome over your head and the ground under your feet, is the carved-open carcass of a murdered goddess, held apart by force, because if the force ever lets go the waters come back together and drown everything."

Nobody spoke. Edith, on the far chair, had her camera in her lap, both hands folded over the body of it, not lifting it, and Naila saw her decide not to lift it, which was a thing Edith was learning to do in front of them like a man learning to set down a drink.

"Creation," Narin said, "is what's left over after a fight. The sky is a wound that won't reopen because somebody's standing on it."

---

"Now." She put the laptop down again. Her voice changed register — not less reverent, *more,* the way a hand gets more careful near the thing it loves. "I'm going to read you something. Bashir, you'll know it before I'm three words in."

"I already do," Bashir said, and there was a thickness in it, because he heard the trap she was springing and it was a beautiful one.

"*In the beginning,*" Narin said, "*God created the heavens and the earth. And the earth was without form, and void; and darkness was upon the face of the deep. And the Spirit of God moved upon the face of the waters. And God said, Let there be light.*"

She let *Let there be* sit in the lantern-light.

"There's the deep," she said. "There's the dark water at the start, no shape to it, waiting. Same furniture. Same problem the Babylonians were chewing on — what do you do about the formless water that was there before everything. And on the second day —" she found it from memory, no screen — "*And God said, Let there be a firmament in the midst of the waters, and let it divide the waters from the waters. And God made the firmament, and divided the waters which were under the firmament from the waters which were above the firmament.*"

She looked up.

"The dome," she said. "Holding back the waters above from the waters below. The exact same cosmic plumbing. The waters of chaos, divided, held apart, a barrier set between them. It is, I promise you, the *same room.* Anyone who tells you the Hebrews didn't know this poem, didn't know this whole way of seeing the sky — they're not being careful."

Yusuf was nodding, slow, and Naila could see the old door in his face, the one he'd come in through — the *aha, it's all connected, it was all one secret thing.* He was a half-second from saying it.

"So they copied it," Yusuf said. Not triumphant. Almost mournful. "It's just Babylon with the serial numbers filed off."

And Narin smiled, and it was the warmest Naila had seen her, because Yusuf had walked exactly into the spot she'd dug.

"No," she said. "Look at *how* the firmament gets made."

---

She stood up. She couldn't help it; she was a teacher and the thing wanted standing for. The lantern threw her shadow up the unfinished wall, huge and rough on the grey block.

"Marduk *butchers a god,*" she said. "He shoots the arrow into Tiamat's open mouth and splits her body and shoves the halves apart with his hands and the dome is her carcass. Combat. Murder. Dismemberment. The sky is held up by an act of war and it stays up because a soldier's posted to keep it up."

She turned, found the page in the air.

"Genesis. *And God said, Let there be a firmament.* And there was. He *talks.* There's no monster. There's no sea-mother. There's no rival to fight, because there's no second power in the room to fight *with.* The deep isn't a goddess. It's just water. It's just dark water with no will of its own, waiting to be told what to do — and it gets *told,* it doesn't get *fought,* and it obeys, because the one doing the telling is the only one there. The waters get divided by a *sentence.*"

Naila's pen had already moved. In the left column: *split by force.* In the right, where she'd been waiting all night to write something true: *divided by word.*

There it was. The lattice clicked its gap shut.

"That's not filing off the serial numbers," Narin said, and now her voice had grief in it as well as fire, and the grief was the part Mira was watching — Naila could see Mira's eyes on Narin's hands, on the set of her jaw, reading the thing Narin wasn't saying yet. "That's taking the conqueror's own poem, the one his soldiers recited over your burning city, and bending every joint of it. They kept the furniture. The deep, the dome, the waters above and below — they kept all of it, because that's the furniture *everyone* in that world had, that's just what the sky was *made of* if you were a human being between the two rivers. But they changed the *verb.* Combat became speech. Theomachy became a single voice in the dark. War became a word."

She sat back down. The shadow came down off the wall.

"And the people," she said, quieter. "Tell them, Bashir. What are people for, in Marduk's version."

---

Bashir had been waiting too. He'd half-said this before, days ago, but he said it whole now, and Naila watched the language carry him.

"After Marduk wins," he said, "the gods are tired. They've been doing the digging — the canals, the irrigation, the whole back-breaking apparatus of keeping Mesopotamia alive, because Mesopotamia does not feed you unless you *break your back* moving water around. And Marduk says, I'll make a thing to take that off you. And they take Kingu — Tiamat's general, the one who held the Tablet of Destinies, the loser, the traitor — and they cut his throat. And from his blood they make people."

"To do the work," Yusuf said. Very low.

"To do the work," Bashir said. "So the gods don't have to dig. *Lullû* — a savage, a labourer. Made from the blood of a defeated rebel god, mixed with — in the older poem, Atrahasis, it's clay — mixed with clay, so that the rebel's blood carries the spark and the clay carries the body. You are dirt and a dead traitor's blood and your job is the irrigation roster. That's what a human being is. That's the *good* news in the poem — congratulations, the gods don't have to dig anymore, because now there's *you.*"

He stopped. Frik had gone very still again and then he wasn't still — his shoulder jerked, his head went sideways and back, and the words came out of him before he could weigh them, clean and flat the way his true things came:

"*Slaves.* Made to be slaves."

He blinked. Nobody laughed. Nobody ever laughed; the crew had unlearned that early. The blurt sat in the lantern-light, exactly right, the thing the poem said and would not soften.

"Slaves," Narin agreed, gently, like she was thanking him. "Made of clay to bear the gods' toil. That's the Mesopotamian answer to *why are we here.* You're here because somebody with more power than you got tired of digging."

She let it lie a moment.

"Now Genesis," she said. "*And God said, Let us make man in our image, after our likeness.* In the *image.* Not as a labour-saving device. Not from a traitor's blood to spare the gods a sore back. In the image — the word they used for the *statue of the king* you'd set up in a province so everyone knew who ruled there. Every single human being, the king's standing-stone. Put down here to *represent.* Same dust — God forms him from the dust of the ground, the clay's still there, they kept the clay — but the clay gets the breath of the only god there is, and gets told to *rule,* not to dig."

She spread her hands.

"That's the argument. That's the whole, furious, brilliant argument a defeated people made with their captor's own toys. *You said we were made from a dead god's blood to spare the masters their labour. We say we were made in the image of the only Master, to stand for him over the whole earth.* You can't make that argument unless you know Babylon's version cold. You have to *love* the enemy's poem to fight it that precisely."

Mira lifted her hands and signed something, slow and careful, and when nobody caught it she said it, in her own flat clear voice that the crew had stopped hearing as flat months ago: "They didn't run from the big story. They climbed inside it and turned it around."

"Yes," Narin said, and looked straight at her, full at her, the way people so rarely did. "Exactly that."

---

And then she did the thing Naila had been waiting for, the thing that was Narin's whole gift, the reason the win in this room was hers and would never be theirs to take: she turned the scalpel on her own side.

"And now," she said, "I'm going to disappoint the people who want this to be neater than it is. Because there's a version of what I just told you that's *too* clean, and the internet loves it, and it's wrong, and if you go out and repeat the wrong version you'll deserve every Assyriologist who corrects you."

She held up one finger.

"You'll hear that *tehom* — the Hebrew word for the deep, 'darkness on the face of the deep' — is just Tiamat with the vowels changed. That Genesis literally *borrowed the goddess's name.* It's all over the place. It sounds amazing. It's the kind of thing that makes you feel like you've cracked something." A breath. "It's not true. *Tehom* and *Tiamat* both come down from a common Semitic root — *t-h-m* — that just means the deep, the abyss, the primeval ocean. They're cousins. They share a great-grandmother. *Tehom* is not a loan of the *name* Tiamat; if the Hebrews had borrowed her name directly the word would have a different shape and it doesn't, it behaves like an ordinary inherited noun, no goddess attached. It's a cognate, not a theft. And the difference *matters,* because the whole point I just made you — that they stripped the personhood off the deep, that they turned a goddess into plain water — *only works* if *tehom* is a word for water and not a smuggled-in deity. The reverent reading and the careful reading are the same reading. The cheap version breaks both."

Second finger.

"And you'll hear: *seven tablets, seven days.* The poem's in seven tablets, creation's in seven days, ooh." She made the *ooh* gently, no cruelty in it. "It's a coincidence people fall in love with. The seventh tablet of Enuma Elish isn't a day of rest — it's fifty hymns of praise piled on Marduk, his fifty names, the council exalting him. It's nothing like a Sabbath. The poem isn't even organised by days. You can want the parallel as hard as you like; it isn't there. Hold the parts that are real *because* they're real, and let the pretty fake ones go, or you hand the whole case to the first person who knows the texts."

Naila wrote, in small letters in the margin between her two columns: *cousins, not theft. 7≠7.* She felt the same plain satisfaction she got from a balanced sum. The over-reach died the same way the aliens died — in the actual words, with the actual root, *t-h-m,* a thing you could go and check.

"So what's left," Yusuf said. He'd stopped leaning forward. He was sitting up now, and there was a kind of vertigo in his face, the man who'd come for the secret-spacefarers finding the floor of the real thing was further down and held more weight. "Strip the cheap stuff out. What's left."

---

Narin closed the laptop. The screen-glow went, and there was only the two lanterns, one high, one low.

"What's left is bulletproof and it's the only thing worth saying," she said. "The Hebrew scriptures — the creation, the flood, Eden, the tower, the law-codes — are in deliberate conversation with older Mesopotamian literature. Older by a thousand years and more. And the channel's not a mystery. It's the Exile. Six hundred years before your calendar starts, Babylon takes Jerusalem apart and marches the educated class — the priests, the scribes, the people who *make texts* — to Babylon and keeps them there for two generations. They live in the city of Marduk. They hear Enuma Elish recited at the New Year while the river comes up. They go home, eventually, and they write — and what they write is shot through, on every page, with the poems of the people who conquered them, *answered.* Not copied. Answered. That's not a fringe theory. That's the first week of any honest seminary. It has a genealogy, and the genealogy runs straight through the worst thing that ever happened to them."

She stopped. Let it be as big and as quiet as it was.

"That's the real thing," she said. "And it's harder to live with than either lie. It's harder than *the Bible fell out of the sky with no fingerprints on it.* And it's a thousand times harder than *aliens did it and it's all just myth anyway, who cares.* Because it asks you to hold two things at once: that this is a made thing, made by human beings at a specific desk in a specific catastrophe — *and* that what they made out of their captivity was a vision of the dignity of every human being that the people who wrote the alien comics couldn't dream up on their best day. Made of clay, yes. But made to *stand,* not to dig."

Outside, the call to prayer had ended; the generator alone now, and far off a dog, and once, the soft particular sound of Jakobus moving the Beast's water bag a hand's width along the rail so it could drip on dry ground instead of mud. He hadn't come in. The room was Narin's and he'd left it hers. Naila found she'd stopped resenting how the man arranged himself around the edges of rooms; he arranged himself the way she arranged the margins of a page, leaving the centre for the count.

She looked down at her two columns. *Combat* and *word.* *Force* and *speech.* *Made to dig* and *made to stand.* The left column full, the right column full now too, and across the bottom of the right one, the pencil thing she'd written hours ago, when Bashir first said it — *named* — *when the heavens were not yet named.*

Marduk *split* the world.

But before any of it — before sky, before earth — the very first verb in the Babylonian poem was the world being *named.* And Genesis ran the same verb the whole way through: God said, and called the light Day, and called the dark Night, *named* and *named* and *named.* Two civilisations, both reaching for the same intuition that to make a thing real you first had to *say* it.

The number sat clean. She liked it. She didn't say it; it wasn't hers to say.

Bashir had gone quiet on his blanket, and Naila recognised the quiet — the polyglot's quiet, the one that meant a language was holding a thing he couldn't put in English without breaking it. He had his lips moving, soundless, working at a word.

"Bashir," Narin said. She'd seen it too. "Where've you gone."

He looked up, and there was a grin starting in him that had nothing of mockery and everything of love.

"There's a better one," he said. "There's a *joke.* Older than all of this. In a poem about a paradise-garden and a god who eats the wrong plants, there's a pun — and it's hiding under one of the most famous verses in your Bible, Narin, and it only works in *one* dead language, and the moment you translate it into Hebrew it dies, and nobody who reads it in English has ever heard it laugh." He shook his head, slow, the way you do at something that's lasted three thousand years and is still, somehow, funny. "The rib. The woman from the rib. Wait till you hear what *rib* used to mean."

Narin's eyebrows went up.

"Tomorrow," she said. "In the cool. When the light's gone flat."

"Tomorrow," Bashir agreed.

And Naila turned the page, and at the top of the fresh one, square and small, she wrote a single Sumerian sign she didn't yet understand, and left the column beside it empty, the way you do when you don't yet know what it's answering.

# Chapter 15 — The Joke in the Dead Language

The cool came late.

All day the heat had sat on the safehouse like a hand pressing a lid, and Bashir had felt the word he wanted to say going soft in his mouth, the way a thing does when you carry it too long without setting it down. He'd carried it through the morning's tea, through Yusuf's phone calls to a man in Sulaymaniyah about a man in Dohuk, through the long flat hours when nobody moved and the lizard on the wall by the window did not move either. He'd carried it past noon, when Naila had her notebook open and was checking the same column of base-sixty numerals for the fourth time, not because she doubted them but because the checking was its own kind of rest. He'd held it the way you hold a coal you mean to plant in a fire later.

Now the light was going. It came in long and yellow under the awning Jakobus had rigged from the Beast's tarp, and the dust in it turned to gold, and somewhere below the citadel a man was calling people to prayer, and the call rose and the heat broke, and Bashir set the word down.

"Ninti," he said.

Narin looked up from the low table. She had a glass of black tea going cold beside her and a photocopy of a tablet under her hand, the lines of it furred and broken where the clay had chipped three thousand years before anyone thought to photograph it. She had spent the day gating them. *Not that text. You're not ready for that text. You'd read it like Sitchin read it.* She gated by instinct now, the way you'd flinch a child's hand off a stove. But she didn't gate this. She watched him say the name and something in her face came halfway open, the same half-open Mira clocked from across the room and didn't mention.

"Go on then," Narin said.

—

He'd reached her in Aramaic, four days ago, at the museum gate. That was the thing nobody else on the crew quite understood, and he'd never tried to explain it because the explaining made it small. He'd had thirty languages by then and none of them a country. You learned to read a border guard's first word and answer in his mother tongue before he'd decided what you were, and the door that had been shut swung to a crack, and you went through the crack, and that was a life. Belong everywhere. Native nowhere. He'd said three words of her grandmother's language and watched a closed Iraqi Assyrian face decide he was not, after all, only one more foreigner come to dig her dead out of the ground for the camera.

It was the loneliest gift he owned. He could open any door and stand in no room.

But he could feel a joke that had been waiting three thousand years for someone to laugh, and tonight he was going to laugh at it with the right people, and that was a thing too.

—

"There's a poem," Bashir said. "Sumerian. *Enki and Ninhursag.* It's old — old the way the others are old, the way that stops meaning anything when you say the number. There's a paradise in it. A clean place. No sickness, the raven doesn't croak, the lion doesn't kill, the wolf doesn't snatch the lamb. A garden where nothing has gone wrong yet."

"Eden," Edith said, low. She had her camera in her lap and her hand on it, flat, the way you'd hold a dog by the collar. She did not raise it. Bashir clocked that she did not raise it and was grateful in a way he didn't have a word for in any of his languages, which was rare enough that he noticed.

"It's a paradise," Bashir said. "I won't say it's Eden. Narin will skin me." Narin's mouth moved. "But it's the same *furniture.* A garden, a god, water that makes the place live, and then — a god who can't leave the plants alone."

"Enki," Narin said. "Eats them. Eats the eight plants of the garden. He wants to *know* them, what they are, so he eats them, one after another, and Ninhursag — she's the great mother goddess, the lady of the mountain — she curses him for it. She says she won't look on him with the eye of life until he dies. And he starts to die. Eight plants, eight sicknesses. He's dying organ by organ."

"And the foxes broker a deal," Bashir said, grinning now, "because in these poems there is always, *always,* a clever animal who fixes things —"

"There's a fox," Frik said. It came out of him sideways, fast, the involuntary echo, and then a small tic of the shoulder, and then his own voice, dry: "Sorry. There's a fox."

"There's a fox," Bashir agreed, easy, no weight on it, and Frik's shoulders came down a half-inch.

"She comes back," Narin said. "Ninhursag. She relents. She sits him down — and this is the part — she takes him in and she sets a healing goddess at each sick part of him. One goddess per pain. And she asks him, each time: *brother, what hurts you?* And he tells her. *My jaw hurts. My rib hurts. My mouth hurts.* And for each one she births a deity whose name is a pun on the part."

"This," Bashir said, and he could feel it coming up his throat now, the coal he'd planted, going to flame, "is where it lives. He says — Enki says — *my rib hurts me.* My rib. The Sumerian is *ti.*" He said it carefully. "*Ti.* It means rib." He let it sit one beat. "And it means *life.* To live. To make live. *Ti* — same sound, same sign, both."

Naila's pencil had stopped. Bashir watched it stop.

"So the goddess she births to heal his rib," Bashir said, "the lady of the rib — her name is *Nin-ti.* Lady of the rib." He spread his hands. "And lady who makes live. *Nin* is lady. *Ti* is rib *and* life. The name is a joke. It's a *pun.* You can't choose between the two readings because the poet didn't choose — that's the whole point, that's why it's funny, that's why somebody three thousand years ago wrote it down and was pleased with himself. The lady of the rib is the lady of life. The same word. You hear them both at once or you don't hear it at all."

The lizard moved on the wall. Nobody else did.

"And now," Bashir said, quieter, "say it in Hebrew."

—

He turned to Narin when he said it. Because it wasn't his to land. It sat under the most famous garden in her book, and she was the one who'd grown up with that garden, and he wasn't going to be a clever foreigner who cracked her scripture open like a nut on the table for the band's benefit. He'd watched her gate them all day. He gated himself now. He held it out to her on his open hand.

She didn't take it for a moment. She looked at the photocopy under her palm and then at the dark coming on under the awning and then at him.

"You want me to say it," she said.

"It's yours," Bashir said. "I only heard it. You *carry* it."

Narin let out a breath. It wasn't quite a laugh.

"The woman," she said. "In Genesis. God puts the man to sleep and takes a *tsela'* — a rib, a side — and from it he builds the woman. And the man names her *Chavvah.* Eve." She turned her cold glass a quarter-turn on the table. "And the text *tells* you why it named her that. It glosses its own joke, in case you missed it. *Because she was the mother of all living.*" She looked up. "Mother of all *living.* From a *rib.*"

Mira, who had been reading the whole room — Bashir's hands, Narin's quarter-turned glass, Edith's flat palm on the camera — signed something to Yusuf, two slow shapes, badly, and Yusuf, who signed worse, mouthed it back to be sure, and then said it aloud so Mira could watch his lips do it: "Rib. Living."

"Yeah," Narin said, to Mira, looking at Mira when she said it, which most people forgot to do. "Rib and living. In Hebrew those are two completely different words. *Tsela'* and *chay.* They don't sound alike, they don't share a root, the pun is *dead.* It's gone. So the Hebrew has to do it the hard way — it has to take the rib and the living and *put them next to each other* and say, look, look at this, the rib became the mother of all living, here is a connection." She spread her own hands now, the way Bashir had. "It's reaching for a joke it can't make anymore. The joke's in the other language. It got left behind in the other language, on the way over, and what came across into Hebrew is the *shape* of the joke with the laugh taken out."

For a while nobody said anything. The prayer-call below had stopped. The dust had stopped being gold and started being grey.

"That's the most beautiful thing I've ever heard," Yusuf said. His voice did a thing on it. He covered the thing by reaching for the teapot. "That's — sorry. Carry on."

—

Bashir had spent a long time learning when to let a silence work and when a silence was about to curdle. This one was working. He let it. Across from him Naila had bent back over her notebook, and she wasn't writing words, she was making the sign — the one she'd put at the top of a fresh page last night and left a blank column beside, not knowing what it answered. *Ti.* She was making it again and again, small, getting the wedges in the right order, the way she'd worried at the base-sixty numerals all day. Pattern. Not magic. Just a woman who found the truth of a thing by drawing it until her hand believed it.

"Okay," Edith said. Carefully. Hand still on the camera, still flat. "Help me, because I'm the one who's going to frame this and I don't want to frame it wrong." She looked at Narin. "Tell me what I'm *not* saying. Because I can already hear how the other lot would cut this." She did a voice. Not cruel. A trailer voice, slick, the streaming-doc voice they'd all started hearing in their sleep. "*The shocking pun hidden in Genesis. The ancient code the translators didn't want you to see. What were they covering up?*" She let it go. "Right? That's the cut. That's the version that gets eleven million views and a sequel."

"That's the version that's a *lie,*" Narin said, and there was heat in it, and Bashir understood the heat. It wasn't the comparison that made her angry. It was the markup. "Nobody covered anything up. There's no code. There's no secret. The Sumerian poem is on a tablet in a museum, the Hebrew is in every house in the world, the dictionary entry for *ti* with both meanings is in a book a first-year student buys for forty pounds. It's the opposite of hidden. It's the most *read* book and one of the most studied poems on earth, and the connection isn't whispered — it's *taught,* it's in the footnotes, it's in the introductions. The scholars who worked this out a hundred and fifty years ago didn't hide it. They *published* it. They wanted you to see it." She tapped the photocopy. "The only people who need it to be a secret are the people selling you something."

"What is it for, then," Edith said. "If it's not a smoking gun. What is it for."

It was Jakobus's question. Edith said it the way you handed back a tool. None of them had decided he led; he never led; but the question had got into all of them the same way his cayenne had got into the rice, a few grains, changing everything. *Not who. Not is-it-real. What is it for.*

Narin was quiet a moment.

"For me?" she said. "Or for them?"

"Both," said Mira, watching her mouth.

—

"For them," Narin said. "For the people who made the Sumerian poem. It's *play.* That's what kills me about the alien version, more than anything. They look at this — at people who invented writing, who ran the first cities, who did astronomy with their naked eyes and got it *right,* who balanced grain accounts in base sixty and counted to numbers that would take you all night — and the aliens-people look at that and they go: *no. Couldn't be them. Must've been visitors.* As if the men who could make a pun like *Ninti* couldn't possibly have made a city."

She said it level. She'd promised herself, Bashir could tell, not to let it become a speech. She kept it level.

"And the joke proves them wrong better than any argument," she said. "Because a pun is the most *human* thing there is. It's not engineering. You don't import it from the stars. A pun is somebody being *clever for the love of it,* somebody who knows their own language so well they can make it mean two things at once and trust you to catch both. That's not a slave-race waiting for instructions. That's a poet showing off. You don't get a better proof of a civilisation than a joke it left for you, and the joke's right there, and the alien version had to *not read it* to tell its story. That's the tell. Every single time. They had to leave the clay unread."

"Say more about that," Yusuf said, and he wasn't reaching for tea now. He'd gone still in the particular way he went still when the thing being said was the thing he'd needed, once, to be the other way round. He had needed the aliens, hard, in the years he didn't talk about. The rabbit-hole had been a door out of a life he couldn't stand. Bashir watched him hold the door open now and look at the bigger room behind it and not flinch.

"They had to leave it unread to make it shine," Yusuf said, slowly, working it out as a man works out a sum he doesn't like. "If you actually read the tablet it stops being a relic and starts being a *text.* Some bloke's joke. And a joke isn't worth much on a grey market. But *PROOF OF ALIENS,* a tablet nobody's translated, a tablet that *can't* be translated, that's worth —" He stopped. He'd come at the network sideways, by accident, by following the money the way he always did, and now he'd arrived at it. "That's the markup. The mystery is the markup. The minute someone reads it and it says *the lady of the rib is the lady of life,* the price collapses. So you keep it unread. You keep the question loud and the answer buried and the crate keeps moving."

Nobody said anything. Below the citadel a generator coughed and caught.

"Right," Yusuf said, to himself.

—

Frik had been bouncing his knee for a while, a small fast bounce, and his hand had come up to his collarbone twice and gone down. The room had gone serious and the serious was loud in him. Bashir saw it building, the way you see weather. They all saw it. Nobody moved to manage him, which was the kindness, the actual one — not pretending it wasn't happening, not making him a problem to be solved, just leaving him room in the circle.

"They keep it unread," Frik said, fast, an echo of Yusuf, and then his voice tried to climb on top of itself, "keep it unread, keep it — *the sar is the orbit,*" and the wrongness of it, the over-reach he'd punctured a dozen times before, came out of him whole and helpless, and his face did a thing, the way it did, because he hadn't meant to throw the lie up, the lie had thrown itself, and the involuntary part was the worst part to live in — and then, lower, scraped out of him, the spike, the rare one, the one that only came when the truth was too big to come politely: "It's a *number.* It's just a — it's a fucking *number.*"

The word landed in the quiet. Nobody laughed. That was the rule and the rule held because it was true: the swear wasn't a gag, it was the truth coming out the only door it could find, and the truth was that the 3,600 — the *sar,* the great number the aliens had made an orbit, a doom-planet, a clock counting down to the return of the gods — was a base-sixty numeral, sixty sixties, a sign a child of Ur could read, and the colossal effort it had taken to turn a number into a spaceship was the whole crime in one syllable, and Frik had said it, involuntary, exact.

"It's just a number," Narin agreed, gently, and Frik's knee stopped, and his hand came down off his collarbone and stayed down. "You're right. That's all it ever was."

"Sorry," Frik said.

"Don't be," said Mira, who'd read the whole thing off his body before the words came and had her hand half-raised in something none of them had taught her, an apology she was offering *to* him for the room going so quiet around him.

—

It was full dark now. Someone had lit the lantern. Out past the awning, near the truck, Jakobus stood in the way he stood, shades up on his head though there was no sun, his clean hanky going over the wing mirror in slow passes, and the Beast's canvas water bag sweating its slow cold beside him, and he was not in the conversation and never came into it, but his presence sat at the edge of the circle like the desert sits at the edge of a town, and once, when Frik's voice had spiked, the slow passes of the hanky had not paused, which was its own kind of answer. *Carry on. The man's fine. The man's one of mine.* He looked at none of them. He hummed something, low, a tune none of them knew, keeping a time none of them could hear.

Bashir watched him for a moment through the dark, the man no camera could hold, and felt the old loneliness move in him, and then felt it settle, because the man out there belonged nowhere either and had made this — this circle of the written-off under a tarp on the wrong side of the world — out of exactly that not-belonging. You could make a home out of having no country. Bashir was learning that. It was taking him a long time.

"Bashir." Narin's voice brought him back. She was looking at him oddly, kindly. "Where've *you* gone now."

"Nowhere," he said. Then, because it was true and the room had earned true things tonight: "Everywhere. Same problem."

She nodded like she understood it, and maybe she did. She had two languages and one people and no safe country either. They were not so different, the two of them, the man with no nation and the woman whose nation kept being marched out of its own ground.

—
