"There are more," Bashir said, because he wanted to give them one more and then let the night close. "Eden threads. Not one-to-one — Narin will skin me again — not a *blueprint.* But the furniture keeps showing up in the rooms. Adapa." He looked at Narin to take it.

"A man," she said. "Offered the bread of life and the water of life by the gods, and he refuses it — because his own god warned him it was the bread of death, a misunderstanding, a kindness that betrays him — and so he loses immortality by a *mistake.* By getting it wrong. Not by sin. By misreading the situation." Her mouth tilted. "Which, given what we've been saying about translation all night, I'll let you enjoy."

"And the plant," Bashir said. "In Gilgamesh."

"He finds it," Narin said. "The plant that makes the old man young. At the bottom of the sea, he ties stones to his feet and goes down and brings it up, the one thing in all his grief that could undo death — and he sets it down on the bank to bathe, and a serpent comes up out of the water and eats it, and sheds its skin, and slips away renewed, and the man sits down and weeps. The snake gets the immortality. The snake. In a garden, by the water." She held up a flat hand before anyone could leap. "It's *not* the Genesis serpent. Don't. It's the same furniture in a different room. A motif. A snake, a plant, a lost forever — these are the pieces the whole world built its gardens out of, everyone working with the same handful of clay."

"Same handful of clay," Naila said, from her notebook, not looking up. It was the first thing she'd said in an hour. She'd made the sign for *ti* maybe forty times by now and her hand believed it. "That's the actual finding. That's the pattern. Not *the same story.* The same *pieces.* Everyone in the room got the same Lego and built a different house, and you can tell whose house is whose by what they did with the pieces." She looked up at last, found Narin. "And the question that's left over — the real one, the one nobody's answered — isn't *which house is true.* It's *who handed Genesis the Lego.* Where the pieces came from. How they got from a tablet in a Babylonian library into the hands of the people who wrote the garden in Hebrew."

The lantern hissed. Narin had gone still, the particular stillness of a person who has been waiting all night for the others to walk themselves to the right door so she wouldn't have to drag them there.

"Yes," she said. "That."

"You know," Naila said. Not a question.

"I know where the older drafts were kept," Narin said. "And I know who got marched there, in chains, and stood in front of them, and read them." She drew the cold glass toward her and didn't drink. Outside, Jakobus's tune went on, low, marking a time none of them could hear. "There was a library," Narin said. "It belonged to the people who'd just burned your city and taken your god's house apart stone by stone and walked you a thousand miles to live in their capital. And in that library, in the language of the people who'd destroyed you, was the older version of the story of how the world began."

She looked round the circle, at the written-off, at the man no camera could hold standing out in the dark.

"Tomorrow," she said. "I'll tell you what they did with it."

# Chapter 16 — The Enemy's Library

Morning in Erbil came in flat and white, the heat already a held breath by the time Mira came out under the awning. The others were arranging themselves on cushions and crates in the half-shade. She took the place she always took now, back to the wall, angle on every face. Naila sat to her left where she could see the hands; that had become a quiet agreement between them, never spoken, the best kind.

Narin came last, with a tray. Tea in the small waisted glasses, and beside it a slab of bread torn from a sheet the size of a tablecloth. She set the tray down and lowered herself onto a cushion and did not look at any of them for a moment, looking instead at the steam coming off the glass nearest her hand.

Through the gap in the awning Mira could see the truck, and the long shape of Jakobus beside it, doing the thing he did with the water bag — the canvas one that wept itself cool, sweating in the heat the way nothing here did, a desert trick on a desert he hadn't been born to. He wasn't watching them. He never watched them in a way you could catch. He was reading the lane.

"Last night," Narin said, and her hands found the glass and turned it, "Naila asked the right question. She said: who handed Genesis the Lego." A small movement at the mouth that wasn't quite a smile. "I have hated that metaphor all night and I can't replace it."

Naila signed something at Mira, fast and badly — *I'm sorry* and *good* tangled into one shape. Mira shook her head: *no, it's good.* Across the circle Bashir caught the exchange and translated it for himself silently, his lips moving, the way he couldn't help.

"So I'll answer it," Narin said. "And the answer is a city. And a march. And a library."

---

She started with the march, because that was where the people started.

She told it plainly. In the year the West likes to round to 586 before its own era, the armies of Babylon came up the Levantine coast and did to Jerusalem what Babylon did to cities that didn't pay. Mira watched the words on Narin's mouth and the words under them. *Came up.* *Did.* Narin was not performing grief. She was holding it at a distance you could only manage with a thing you'd lived around your whole life, the way you carry a body you have washed yourself.

The temple, Narin said, was taken apart. Not burned only — *taken apart.* The bronze pillars cut up for the metal. The vessels carried off as inventory. And the people — not all of them; the ones who mattered, the ones with words in their heads — the priests, the scribes, the skilled, the literate, the dangerous-because-they-remembered — were chained and walked east.

"A thousand kilometres," Narin said. "Give or take. To the heart of the empire. To the capital of the people who had just unmade your god's house with their hands."

Yusuf had gone very still. Mira knew the stillness. It was the stillness of a man recognising a road from the wrong side of it.

"And here is the thing nobody draws in the children's Bible," Narin said. "They didn't go to a camp. They weren't dumped in a desert to die. Babylon was *cosmopolitan.* The cleverest city on earth, then. They settled the exiles by a canal — there's a man, Ezekiel, who tells you the name, the Kebar, the *nār Kabari,* it's in the cuneiform too, it's a real waterway — and the skilled among them found their level. And a scribe is a scribe. A scribe with letters in two languages is a scribe anywhere."

She let that sit.

"They could go into the city," she said. "And the city had a library."

---

This was where Bashir came forward, because libraries were his. He didn't take it from her — Mira watched the handover, the small tilt of Narin's head that *gave* it to him, *go on, you know this part* — and he took it gently, the way you take a sleeping child off someone's lap.

"You have to understand what a Babylonian library was," Bashir said. "Not a building of scrolls that rot. Clay. Baked clay. Tens of thousands of tablets, shelved, *catalogued* — they had catalogues, they had the equivalent of a card index, they had a tablet that told you which tablets they had. A scribe's whole training was copying. You copied to learn the signs, you copied to keep the texts alive, you copied because that is what a library is — a machine for not forgetting."

"And the Hebrew scribes could read it," Naila said. Not a question. She had a stylus going on her knee, the cut reed, marking the clay tablet she'd been allowed, the soft grey one. Mira couldn't see what she was writing. It didn't matter. The writing was how Naila held still.

"Akkadian was the diplomatic language of the whole ancient Near East for centuries," Bashir said. "It was the French of its day, the English of its day. A trained Judean scribe, a man whose job was words — there is no reason on earth he couldn't read the tablets. Every reason he would *want* to. You are a person of the book in a city that invented the book. You are a writer in the largest library that has ever existed. Of course you went in."

He spread his hands.

"And there, in the language of the people who marched you here in chains, was the older draft of your own beginning."

Mira watched Narin while Bashir said it. Narin's face did a thing then — not a flinch, the opposite of a flinch, a settling, like a stone finding the bottom of a well.

---

"Tell them what was on the shelf," Narin said.

Bashir told them.

He told them about the flood — not the way the streaming-doc would tell it, with a dramatic chord and a dark water effect, but the way the clay told it. A council of gods. A man warned. A boat built, sealed with bitumen, loaded with kin and craft and beasts. The rain. The waters. The boat grounding on a mountain. The birds sent out to test for land. The sacrifice when the man comes ashore, and the gods — Bashir's voice did something here, dropped — the gods crowding to the smell of the offering "like flies," because they'd flooded the world and forgotten they liked to be fed.

"A thousand years older than Genesis," Bashir said. "At least. Sitting on a shelf the exiles could walk up to."

"So they copied it," Edith said. She had her camera in her lap. The lens cap was on. Mira had clocked that the moment she sat down — the cap on, the strap looped twice round Edith's wrist so the thing couldn't lift itself to her eye by old habit. Edith was keeping her own hands honest.

"That," Narin said, "is exactly the wrong word. And it's the word people reach for, and it's why this whole conversation goes to ruin in the comments section." Her mouth quirked. She'd clearly read the comments section. "*Copied* is what the man who needs scripture to be a fraud says. *Copied* is the looting word. You took it, it was always theirs, you're a thief." She turned the glass a half-turn. "And it's wrong. Naila, tell them why it's wrong. You found it yourself, you don't need me."

Naila looked up from the clay. The whole circle waited, and didn't crowd, because they'd learned not to crowd.

"Because the houses are different," she said. "Same Lego." The faintest of grins. "Sorry. Same pieces, different houses. The flood story isn't *copied,* because if you copied it you'd get the *same one,* and you don't. The mountains are different — theirs is a mountain called Nimush, the Hebrew one is the mountains of Ararat, that's not a transcription error, that's a different mountain. The birds go out in a different order. The boat's a different shape. The number of days is different. The reason for the flood is *completely* different, and that's the whole — that's the whole tell."

"Say the reason," Narin said.

"In the old one," Naila said, "the gods flood the world because people are too *loud.* They're breeding, they're noisy, the gods can't sleep. It's a noise complaint. It's the upstairs neighbours calling the council on the human race." A few breaths around the circle, the laugh that isn't quite a laugh. "And in Genesis the reason is *moral.* The world is full of violence, of *hamas,* and that's why. Somebody took the same flood and changed *why God does it.* You don't change the why by accident. You change the why because you have a different idea of what God is *for.*"

She looked down at her clay again, finished.

"That's not copying," she said, to the reed. "That's arguing."

---

Frik had been quiet the whole morning. He sat a little apart, as he always did when he was working hard at holding, his shoulder giving the soft repeated rise it gave. Now his head ticked once, twice, and the word came out of him sideways, not aimed at anyone:

"*Better.*"

Then, the involuntary completion, riding the same breath, surprising him as much as them: "*The arguing's the good bit.*"

Narin looked at him. Something passed over her that Mira read clearly and that the others probably missed, because they were watching Frik to see if he was all right, which was the kind thing and the wrong thing. The thing that passed over Narin was *relief.* Frik had said the load-bearing sentence and he'd had no choice about it, which meant it was true the way only the things a person can't stop are true.

"Yes," Narin said quietly. "The arguing is the good bit. Hold that."

---

She poured more tea. The morning had thickened; you could hear the bazaar starting up below the citadel, the metal-on-metal, a generator, a long way off a call.

"Now I'm going to do the thing I trust the fewest people on earth to do," Narin said. "I'm going to take you to the second shelf. And I want you to watch how careful I am, because there's a version of this conversation that turns my faith into a museum case, and I won't have it, not in my own family's house. So watch my hands."

She put both hands flat on her knees, like a witness swearing in.

"The tower," she said. "Babel."

Mira felt the room change shape. Even Yusuf leaned in.

"Genesis eleven," Narin said. "The whole world had one language. They came to a plain in the land of Shinar — and Shinar *is* Sumer, that's not in dispute, that's just the Hebrew name for the place — and they said, come, let us build a city and a tower whose top is in the heavens, and let us make a name for ourselves, lest we be scattered. And God comes down to see it, and confounds the languages, and scatters them. And the place is called Babel, *because there the LORD confused* — *balal* — *the languages.*" She let the breath out. "And every Sunday-school child in the world is taught that the tower fell down. It doesn't. Read it. The tower doesn't fall. God just stops the building by breaking the words."

She looked at Bashir.

"Tell them what the writer was looking at."

Bashir's whole body had come awake. This was his country, the country of words.

"He was looking at a ziggurat," Bashir said. "A real one. Babylon had a great temple-tower, a stepped mountain of brick. It had a name — Etemenanki. It means, roughly, *the house of the foundation of heaven and earth.* House. Of the foundation. Of heaven and earth. A tower whose top is meant to reach the heavens. It is *literally named* the thing the Genesis tower is *for.*" He laughed, short. "And the name of the city, Babylon — in the Babylonians' own language it's *Bāb-ilim, bāb-ili,* the gate of the gods. A holy name. A proud name. *The gate where heaven touches earth.*"

"And the Hebrew writer," Narin said, "takes *the gate of the gods* — that proud, sacred name — and says no. He hears *bāb-ili* and he answers with *balal.* Confusion. Babble." Her mouth was a thin line and there was a fierce light behind it. "He puns. He takes the conquering city's holiest word for itself and turns it into the sound a baby makes. The gate of the gods is just *noise.* That's not a man who got the story wrong. That's a man who got the story exactly right and *answered it.*"

Bashir picked it up like a relay baton. "And it's in the building too. Genesis: *let us make bricks, and burn them thoroughly, and they had brick for stone, and bitumen for mortar.*" He looked round at them. "Stop. Listen to that. Burnt brick. Bitumen for mortar. That is not how you build in Canaan. In the Levant you build with *stone.* You quarry it, you stack it, you mortar it with lime. Mud-brick maybe, sun-dried, but you don't bake your bricks in kilns and you don't seal a tower with tar — *because you don't have the tar.*" He brought a hand down soft on his own knee. "Baked brick and bitumen mortar is the *Mesopotamian* method. It's how you build in the alluvial plain where there's no stone and the river gives you mud and the ground sweats asphalt. The Genesis writer doesn't just *know about* Babylon. He knows how Babylon's bricklayers worked. He's describing a building technique that doesn't belong in his own country."

"Because," Naila said, very quietly, the reed gone still on the clay, "he was *there.*"

The awning ticked in the heat. Outside, Jakobus's tune started up, low, the same handful of notes he used when nothing was wrong. Mira couldn't hear it, but she saw the others' shoulders settle to it the way they always did, and she watched his free hand keep the time against his thigh, and she understood it was a metronome for a clock none of them could hear and most of them had stopped noticing they obeyed.

---

"This," Narin said, "is the part where the comments section divides into two armies, and both of them are wrong."

She held up one finger.

"Army one says: *see, it's all stolen, the Bible is just Babylonian myth with the names changed, your faith is a forgery.*" Her voice didn't rise. "And to that I say: you have understood nothing. A forger copies to fool you. These writers re-wrote to *argue with you.* They kept the flood and changed why God sends it. They kept the tower and turned the gate of the gods into babble. They sat in the largest library on earth, in chains, in the language of their conqueror, and they read the conqueror's account of how the world began — and they wrote it back the way they believed it actually was, and every single change is a *theological claim.* That is the opposite of theft. That is the most defiant act of authorship I know of in the ancient world. They were a conquered people and they kept their soul by re-writing the empire's own story until it said what they needed it to say. You don't lose your faith over that. You should be on your knees about that."

Mira watched the faithful crew member's face — she watched Narin's, because Narin was the faithful one in this room, the living heir to both the people who got marched and the language they got marched in, and Narin's face was doing the thing the mouth would never say aloud. Not pride. Pride was too small. It was the face of a woman defending a grave from people who'd come to either rob it or photograph it, and finding, to her surprise, that the people in front of her had come to neither.

Edith had not touched the camera. Mira clocked that too. Edith's hands were folded over the lens cap like she was keeping it warm.

Narin held up a second finger.

"Army two," she said, "is the sneerers' twin. The believer who's so frightened of the library that he pretends the library isn't there. *No, no, there's no connection, it's a coincidence, the floods are just both floods.*" She shook her head. "No. The connection is real. It's mainstream. It's been the consensus among people who actually read the tablets for a hundred and fifty years. Hiding from it doesn't protect the faith. It just hands the whole conversation to army one." She turned the empty glass. "The genealogy is real. Genesis has a family tree, and the older branches of it are sitting in a Babylonian library a thousand years before the Hebrew was written. That is not the threat army two thinks it is. It's the inheritance."

---

It was Yusuf who broke. Not loudly. He'd been leaning further and further forward, elbows on knees, and now he scrubbed both hands down his face and made a sound that was almost a laugh and entirely not.

"You know what I sold," he said. To his own hands. "Not directly. You understand, never directly, that's the whole — there's always a layer. But I moved the paper for crates I knew, I *knew,* came up out of the south. And the ones with the gods on, the ones some idiot stamped *Anunnaki* on in the catalogue copy — those moved at a premium. Because *aliens.* Because somebody'd seen a video. The school tablets, the ration lists, the ones that actually *say* something — 'so-and-so, your servant, received this much barley' — those were filler. Bulk. You priced them by the kilo." He looked up, and his eyes were wet and angry. "I had it exactly backwards. The whole world has it exactly backwards. The *aliens* are the cheap part. The aliens are the markup and the smokescreen and the thing that makes sure nobody who matters reads what the crate actually contains. The *real* one — the one where a man in chains rewrites the gate of the gods into babble because he won't let the empire keep his God — that's the priceless one. And I'd have sold it for filler."

Nobody filled the silence. They'd learned that too. Mira watched Frik's shoulder rise and fall, and Frik held, this time. He held, and that holding was its own kind of speech.

Narin looked at Yusuf for a long moment. When she spoke it was without softness and that was the kindness of it.

"Yes," she said. "You had it backwards. So did the men who put my country's beginning in sacks." She didn't look at Edith when she said *sacks,* and the not-looking was louder than a look. "The grift needs the question loud and the answer cheap. *Were they aliens* keeps the lights on. *What did a conquered scribe do with the enemy's library* — that one nobody can put a price on, because the moment you understand it, you stop wanting to *own* the tablet and start wanting to *read* it. And a thing that's read can't be sold to one buyer in a dark room." Her jaw set. "That's why somebody, somewhere, would much rather you watched the alien documentary."

She said *somebody, somewhere* the way you set a thing down on a table and step back from it. Mira watched it land. Naila's reed had stopped. Bashir's lips had stopped moving. The shape of the sentence had a shape they all recognised now — the loud stories pushed up into the light, the quiet ones gone soft and missing — and none of them touched it. You didn't touch it. You let it sit on the table and you went on.

---

"There's one more," Narin said, after a while, when the tea was cold. "And then I'll have given you the whole shelf and you can decide what it's worth to you."

"The law," Bashir said, because he'd seen it coming.

"The law." Narin almost smiled. "Hammurabi. Everyone's heard of him. Eye for eye, tooth for tooth. There's a great black stone with his code carved on it — it's in the Louvre, it got carried off as loot in *antiquity,* the looting is older than any of us — and it says, if a man puts out another man's eye, they shall put out his eye. The talion. The world's most famous brutal little rule." She tipped her head. "And it's older than Moses. By centuries. And it's in Exodus, almost word for word. *Eye for eye, tooth for tooth, hand for hand, foot for foot.*"

Bashir leaned in. "But read what Hammurabi *actually* says, all of it. The talion in Babylon is for *aristocrats.* If a noble blinds a noble, eye for eye. If a noble blinds a commoner, he pays *silver.* If a noble's house collapses and kills the owner's son" — Bashir's eyes went bright with the awfulness of it — "they execute *the builder's son.* The son. For the father's bad work. Vicarious punishment. The price of justice depends on whose eye it was and how rich you are."

"And Exodus," Narin said, "takes the same rule — and it's careful, this is a real scholarly argument, whether it's borrowed direct or whether they're both drinking from the same legal world of the ancient Near East, I'll say *shared legal world* and a careful person should — Exodus takes that rule and *universalises* it. One law for the rich man and the poor man. And it forbids the thing Hammurabi does — you do not put a son to death for his father, *a person dies for his own sin.* It edits the empire's law toward equality and away from punishing the innocent. Same furniture. Re-arranged to argue."

"*Better,*" said Frik, again, helplessly, and this time it was almost a benediction.

---

Mira had been watching the truck on and off the whole morning, the way the light moved on the long shape beside it. Now Jakobus came toward the awning, unhurried, the always-clean hanky going over the back of his neck, and he didn't come *in.* He stopped at the edge of the shade, where the gold light gave way, and he looked at the tray and the cold glasses and the cut reed in Naila's hand and the slab of bread no one had finished, and he said something — Mira read it off his mouth, the courtesy handful he'd taught himself, *good, this is good* in the local words, badly, with respect — and then he said the other thing, the thing he said, that wasn't a question because he never asked it as a question.

She read it off his lips and gave it to the room in sign so Narin would have it whole, hands slow and plain:

*What's it for.*

Not *who built it.* Not *is it true.* The made thing in his hand was a story now, not a tablet, and the question came down on it the same way it came down on everything — flat, patient, refusing every reading but the working one.

Jakobus didn't wait for the answer. He never did. He touched two fingers to the brim of nothing and went back to the lane, and the tune went with him.

The crew turned, all of them, to Narin.

Because that was the question, wasn't it. Not whose Lego, not which house, not even who handed it over in chains by the Kebar canal. They'd done all that. They knew, now, *what was on the shelf* and *what the exiles did with it* and that the doing wasn't theft and wasn't surrender but the third thing, the unnamed brave thing — survival by authorship, keeping your soul by re-writing the empire until it said your God instead of its own.

But *what is it for.* What is a story *for,* to the people who carry it home from the place that conquered them. Why do you bother to re-write the flood when you're a slave by a foreign canal and your temple is rubble and your God's house is inventory in someone else's storeroom. What does that get you. What does it *do.*

Mira watched Narin's face, and saw that this was the one Narin couldn't read off the clay — that no decipherer, no scholar, no man with a working eye for a made thing could read this answer off the object, because it didn't live in the object. It lived in the people who still said the words. And there was only one person in the awning who could give it.

Narin set down the cold glass. She looked at her uncle's house, and the lane, and the man no camera could hold standing in the white light beyond the shade.

"Tomorrow," she said, and stopped, and corrected herself, because there was no more tomorrow to hide behind. She looked at them.

"No," she said. "Now."

# Chapter 17 — What Is It For

The kettle had gone cold. Yusuf noticed it the way you notice a thing your hands have been pretending to do — turning the little glass in its saucer, the tea inside it the colour of old brick, untouched. He'd poured it twenty minutes ago. Or an hour. The morning had stopped behaving like a thing with edges.

Narin had said *now,* and then she hadn't said anything else for a while, and nobody pushed her, because you don't push the person standing at the top of a stair they've been climbing their whole life. She'd sat back down. Mira had moved the sugar bowl out of the sun so it wouldn't sweat. Bashir had put his pen down. Even Frik had gone quiet, which for Frik was its own kind of effort, the small machine of him idling, ticking — a click of the tongue, a roll of one shoulder, contained.

Out beyond the awning's edge the white light stood on the lane like a poured thing, and in it, near the Beast, Jakobus was doing something with the water bag. The canvas one. He'd hung it off the wing mirror that morning and it had been weeping all day, that slow desert sweat the man swore by — *wet canvas drinks the wind, makes its own cold* — and you could see, even from here, the dark patch on the dust beneath it where it had been spending itself drop by drop to keep the water inside cool enough to be a kindness. He had his sleeves rolled. He wasn't looking in at them. He never looked in when the talk turned to the clay. It wasn't his to look at, and he knew it, and Yusuf had watched him *practise* not-looking the way other men practise not-flinching.

"He should hear this," Yusuf said. He hadn't planned to.

Narin's eyes came up.

"It's his question," Yusuf said. "*What is it for.* He's the one who keeps asking it." He felt the heat of his own face. "I just mean — it feels wrong, him out there."

Mira's hands moved. Bashir, without looking, voiced it under his breath, the habit they'd all fallen into so she was never the only one who'd missed a thing: *He stays out for a reason.*

"He stays out so the doors aren't his," Narin said. She said it gently. "If I want him, I go to him. That's the deal. He told me on the first night. He said —" she turned the cold glass once "— he said, *I open the road, you say where it goes.* So." She set the glass down. "This one doesn't go to him. He already knows that. Look at him."

Yusuf looked.

The man wasn't waiting to be invited. That was the thing the surveillance vans never managed to capture, the thing the network's clean-handed people would put in a file under *no clear read* — that Jakobus standing apart wasn't a man excluded. It was a man on guard at a door he'd decided, on purpose, not to walk through. He had no business with what a story meant to the people who said it. He'd told them as much, days ago, squatting in front of VA 243's photograph with that cayenne tin balanced on his knee: *I can tell you what a made thing is for. I can't tell you what it means to pray to.*

"All right," Yusuf said, and sat back, and let the tea go cold for good.

---

Narin began the way she'd begun everything — sideways, through somebody else's hands.

"In 1872," she said, "a man named George Smith was in the basement of the British Museum, where some of you have been," and she nodded at the memory of the cold room, the steel shelving, the cradle of grey foam, "going through the tablets that came out of Nineveh. Ashurbanipal's library. Smith taught himself the script. He'd been a banknote engraver — he had the eye for tiny marks. And he's reading along, fragment by fragment, and he hits a line about a ship grounding on a mountain. A man sends out a dove. The dove comes back. He sends a raven. The raven doesn't."

Bashir's head had tipped. Yusuf watched the language-part of him lean toward it like a plant toward a window.

"And the story goes," Narin said, "that George Smith stood up in that basement and started taking his clothes off."

Frik laughed — a single bright bark of it, surprised out of him.

"It's probably exaggerated," Narin said, allowing herself the smallest smile. "He got excited. He ran around the room. He was the first person in two and a half thousand years to read that flood, in the language it was written in, and to understand what he was holding. He gave a lecture. The Prime Minister came. People wept. They thought — some of them — that he'd *proved* the Bible. Found the flood in the dirt."

She let that sit.

"He hadn't found the flood in the Bible," she said. "He'd found one that was older."

The awning held very still. Out on the lane the water bag wept.

"A thousand years older, at least," Narin said. "The version Smith read is woven into Gilgamesh, and the flood that's *inside* Gilgamesh is older than that again — Atrahasis, the one we've been living in all week. A man warned by his god. A boat sealed with pitch. The waters. The birds. The grounding on a mountain. The sacrifice when it's over, and the gods gathering over the smoke like —" she paused, and her mouth did something complicated "— Smith translated it himself: *the gods gathered like flies over the offering.* Because they were starving. Because the flood had killed the people who fed them and they'd nearly starved themselves out of pure rage."

"That's not in Genesis," Naila said. It was the first she'd spoken in an hour. She'd been writing — not words, Yusuf saw, but the *shape* of something, columns, a ledger drawn in pencil on the back of a permit form. "The flies. The starving. None of that's in Genesis."

"No."

"Because Genesis took it out."

"Because Genesis," Narin said, "*argued* with it."

---

This was the place Yusuf had been afraid of all week without naming it.

He could name it now. Sitting under a borrowed awning in the Erbil heat, with the citadel up on its mound behind them holding seven thousand years of people who'd never once needed a spaceship to be remarkable — he could finally name what had been hollow in him since the night the rabbit-hole had opened under his ordinary life and let him fall, gratefully, out of it.

He'd needed the aliens to be real.

Not the way a child needs a story. The way a drowning man needs the thing his hand closes on to be a rope and not a snake. His life before this — the logistics desk, the manifests, the crates with the wrong paperwork that he'd learned not to look inside, the slow knowledge that he'd been a clean pair of hands on a dirty river — that life had been a corridor with no doors. And then the videos, the diagrams, Nibiru's long ellipse, the gold mines, the gods who came down and made us in a lab, and oh, the *relief* of it. Because if that were true, then nothing was small. Then the universe had bothered. Then he, Yusuf, who had spent his good years carrying other men's loot down to the sea, was not an accident in a corridor but a designed thing, wanted, on purpose, by someone who'd crossed the dark to make him.

That was the whole engine of it. He understood that now, watching the water bag weep. The alien story flattered you. It said: *you were wanted.*

And the real one didn't.

"Atrahasis," Narin said, "doesn't start with a flood. It starts with a strike."

She'd turned to a tablet line in her notebook, the transliteration in her own neat hand, and Bashir's lips were already moving over it before she read it.

"The gods used to do the labour themselves," she said. "The lesser gods — the Igigi. Digging the rivers. Hauling the baskets of earth. The Tigris and the Euphrates, dug by hand, by gods, for three thousand six hundred years —" she looked at Naila "— a *sar,* yes, a number, a big round base-sixty number, not an orbit of anything —"

"*The sar is the orbit—*" Frik said, and then his whole face folded with the effort of catching it, and he got the rest out himself, fast, dragging it back by the collar, "*—is a numeral. It's a numeral. Sixty squared. It's a numeral.*" He pressed two fingers hard to his sternum. "Sorry. It's a numeral."

"It's a numeral," Naila agreed, mild, and tapped her pencil twice on her column of sixties, and that was the whole of it — no fuss, no one looking at him, the thing said and corrected and let go, and Frik breathed out.

"For three thousand six hundred years they hauled the baskets," Narin went on, as if nothing had snagged, because to her nothing had. "And then they'd had enough. They burned their tools. They surrounded the house of the chief god in the middle of the night with torches. A strike. A mutiny in heaven. And the gods up in the assembly are *frightened* — that's the astonishing thing, the gods are scared of the work stopping — and they hold a council, and someone says: we'll make a replacement. A thing to carry the baskets so we don't have to."

She turned the page.

"They kill a god to do it. A god who has — the text says — *planning,* intelligence, *ṭēmu.* They kill him, and they mix his blood and his mind into clay, and the womb-goddess pinches off fourteen pieces, and they're left to ripen, and out come people. Made of dead-god's-blood and river-mud. And the very first thing the new people are *for* —"

"To carry the baskets," Yusuf said. His voice came out rougher than he meant.

"To carry the baskets," Narin said. "To bear the gods' toil. So the gods could rest." She looked at him, and there was no cruelty in it at all, none, only a terrible level kindness, the kindness of a person handing you the true weight of a thing because the false one was breaking your back worse. "That's what we were made for, in the oldest story this part of the world ever told about itself. Not to be loved. Not to be wanted. To *work,* so that someone higher up didn't have to."

The lane was quiet. The bag wept.

"And it gets worse," Narin said softly, "or better, depending. Because the people multiply, and they're loud, and the noise of all that human labour and life keeps the chief god awake. So he sends plagues. Then a drought. Then the famine — and there are lines, broken lines, that are almost too much to read, about mothers, about what hunger does in a city. And when none of it quiets us, he sends the flood. Not because we'd sinned. There's no sin in it. We were just — *too many.* Too loud. An infestation in the made world. He floods us the way you'd fumigate a house."

She closed the notebook.

"That," she said, "is the story Sitchin found. The clay. The made workers. The dead god's blood. The rebellion of the ones who were tired of digging. He found the real, ancient, gorgeous, *brutal* thing — and he kept the whole skeleton of it. He just couldn't bear what it was *for.* So he swapped the gods for astronauts, and the baskets of earth for gold mines, and the clay for a splice in a lab. He took the bleakest, bravest answer a working people ever gave to the question *why does my back hurt and why do I serve and why will I die* — and he turned it into a flattering bedtime story about being special."

---

Yusuf had to look away from her. He looked at the citadel.

He thought about the manifests. He thought about a crate he'd signed off on in 2004 with a customs stamp that wasn't real and a Sumerian word inside it he hadn't known then and knew now. He thought about the corridor with no doors and how the alien story had felt like a door and how it had really been a mirror, all along, just a mirror, angled up at the stars so he wouldn't have to see his own hands in it.

"It's smaller," he said. He hadn't meant to speak. "The real one. It makes us — smaller."

Narin didn't rush to comfort him.

It was Bashir who spoke, and Bashir spoke to the clay, the way he always did when the human thing was too near to say to a face.

"There's a pun," Bashir said.

Everyone turned.

"In another piece of it — the Enki and Ninhursag one, the garden, Enki eats the plants he shouldn't, and he gets sick, eight parts of him, eight organs, and the goddess heals each one, and she makes a little goddess for each cure. And for the rib —" his hands had come up, holding the air "— the Sumerian word for rib is *ti.* And the word for *to make live* is also *ti.* So the goddess of the rib is *Nin-ti.* Lady of the Rib. *And* Lady-Who-Makes-Live. The same word. You can't tell them apart. It's a joke, it's a real joke, in Sumerian, four thousand years ago, a healer's joke about how the bone and the life are the same sound —"

He stopped. His eyes were very bright.

"And it's only in Sumerian," he said. "You translate it into Hebrew and the pun dies, the bone and the life come apart, you can't keep them in one word anymore. But it leaves a *fossil.* The woman made from the man's rib. The woman who's the mother of all living. The rib, and the making-alive, in the same person — that's a Sumerian pun that fell into Hebrew and couldn't bring its own joke with it, so the joke turned into a story instead." He laughed, helpless with it. "Eve. *Hawwah.* Mother of all living. Made from a rib. It's a four-thousand-year-old pun you can still feel under the floorboards if you know both tongues. *That's* a decode. That's what a real one feels like. Not a star chart. A joke that outlived the language it was funny in."

Frik made a sound — not a tic this time, Yusuf thought, just a sound a person makes — and put his hand over his mouth.

Mira had been watching all their faces, turning, the way she did, reading the room the way the rest of them read the page. Now her hands started, and this time it was Narin, oddly — Narin who'd been picking up the badly-learned signs all week — who began to voice it, and then stopped, because the sign was one she didn't have. Mira slowed it. Spelled it. Yusuf caught the shapes but not the meaning.

Bashir caught it. "She says," he said carefully, watching Mira's face to be corrected, "*so the man who needed it to be aliens* —" Mira nodded, kept going "— *was reaching for the thing the story already had.* The wonder. He just reached past it. Because the real one —"

Mira finished it herself, slowly, so they'd all be inside the same beat: a sign Yusuf did know, that they'd all learned the first day, the open hand turned to the heart and out again — *give* — and then she touched her own chest, and then she pointed, gently, deliberately, at Yusuf.

*The real one asks more of you.*

Nobody had told her to look at him. She'd just read it off his face, the way she read everything, the thing his mouth wouldn't say — that the let-go was happening right now, under the awning, in the cold tea and the weeping bag, and that it hurt exactly as much as losing a religion, because in a way it was one.

Yusuf put the back of his wrist to his eyes and made a noise that was meant to be a laugh.

"It's a better story," he said, into his wrist. "Isn't it. It's the better one. I just —" He breathed. "I just liked being wanted."

"I know," Narin said. And then, very quietly, because she was the only one of them who'd earned the right to: "Everybody does. That's the whole engine of it. That's why it sells."

---

She let him have a minute. They all did. Edith — who hadn't lifted the camera in two days, who had it in the bag at her feet with the lens cap on, on purpose, a discipline she wore now like a healed bone — reached over and put her hand flat on the table near Yusuf's, not on it, near it, and left it there.

Then Naila said, into the quiet, in her flat clear voice that never decorated anything: "But that's not the answer to his question."

Heads turned.

"Whose," Bashir said.

"Jakobus's. *What is it for.*" Naila tapped the ledger she'd been drawing, the columns of sixties. "We just answered what the *Babylonian* one was for. We were made to carry baskets so the gods could sleep. Fine. That's clear. That's in the clay, you can read it off the object. But Narin —" she turned, the whole of her attention swinging round like a beam "— you said *now.* When he asked. You said it was the one you couldn't read off the clay. So that wasn't the question." She frowned, working it. "The question is what the *re-written* one is for. The one the exiles made. Why bother. You're a slave by a canal, your temple's rubble, your God's furniture is somebody's inventory — Yusuf knows about inventory —" a small dry beat that startled a wet laugh out of him "— and you take the empire's bleakest story about being mud-slaves, and you don't just keep it and you don't just throw it away, you *re-write* it, line by line, you change why the flood comes and you change what we're made of and you change what we're *for.* That's a colossal amount of work. To do, in chains. For nothing you can sell. *What is it for.*"

She set the pencil down.

"That one's not on the object," she said. "I checked. I read the whole shape of it three times. It's not in the clay and it's not in the Hebrew either, not as a sentence. It's only in the people who still say it." She looked at Narin, and her voice didn't soften, because Naila's voice didn't, but something in her face opened a half-inch, which from Naila was an embrace. "So it's yours. You're the only one here who carries it home from the place that conquered them."

Mira was already watching Narin's hands, Narin's mouth, Narin's eyes — reading her before she spoke, the way you read weather coming over a ridge.

And Yusuf, who had just put down the heaviest thing he'd ever carried, found he was leaning in for the next one.

Narin was quiet a long moment. When she spoke it wasn't to any of them, not at first. She was looking out at the lane, at the white light, at the man in it who would never ask her this and would never be able to answer it, who could squat in front of any made thing on the earth and tell you what it was *for* — a basket, a boat, a battery-jar with the wrong wiring, a king kneeling before his god in a little carved seal — and who'd stopped, deliberately, at the one door he couldn't open, and stood his guard there in the heat so it could stay hers.

"The Babylonian flood," Narin said slowly, "comes because we're noise. Because we're too many and too loud and the god can't sleep. We're vermin, and he fumigates."

She turned back to them.

"And the exiles, sitting in that library, *reading that* — in the city that had just done to them exactly what the god did to those people, drowned them, scattered them, smoked them out of their own house — they took the same flood, and they made one change that changes everything." She wasn't lecturing. Her voice had gone very low and even. "In their version, the flood doesn't come because we're loud. It comes because we're *cruel.* Because the earth was *filled with violence.* They put a *reason* in it. A moral reason. They said: the universe is not a landlord with a noise complaint. It's a court. And then — this is the part —" and here, just here, her hand pressed flat to the table, the same gesture Mira had made, *give* "— they wrote in a promise. The bow in the cloud. *Never again.* The god who flooded the world *binds himself.* Puts down his weapon. Hangs it in the sky where everyone can see it, and swears off. The Babylonian gods never swear off anything. They just regret it over the smoke, like flies."

She breathed.

"That's what it's for," she said. "That's what a conquered slave by a foreign canal gets out of re-writing the empire's own myth in the empire's own language. You take their story that says *you are mud, made to serve, and your suffering is just noise* — and you re-author it until it says *you are made in the image, not the labour; your suffering is seen, not ignored; and even the one who could drown you has bound himself never to again.* You don't win your freedom that way. You don't get your temple back. You get to keep your *soul.* You get to walk into exile still able to tell your children that the world has a floor under it. That's worth more than the temple. The temple they can take. *That* they can't carry off in a crate."

Edith's hand had closed, very gently, over Yusuf's.

"It's not proof of anything," Narin said, level, the scalpel never far. "I'm not telling you the bow is real or the flood happened or any of it. I'm a scholar; I can tell you when the texts were written and how and from what. I can't tell you what's true the way you mean true. That's not the clay's job and it's not mine." She looked at each of them. "But you asked what it's *for.* And what a story is for, to the people who carry it home from the place that broke them — that I can tell you. Because my people did exactly this, in exactly that city, and we're still here, saying the words, after the empire that conquered us turned to dust and got sold off in pieces." A breath. "Twice. Sold off twice."

The 2003 of it hung there, unspoken and entire, and nobody touched it.

Out on the lane, the man near the truck reached up and unhooked the water bag from the wing mirror, weighed it in his hand, found it cool, and brought it to his mouth. Drank. Hung it back to keep weeping. He did not look in at them. He didn't need to. He'd asked the only question he had to ask, days ago, and then he'd gone and stood where it could be answered by the right person, and waited, and now it had been, and he couldn't have read a word of it off the clay if he'd stared at it for a hundred years.

Yusuf wiped his face properly this time, with the heel of his hand, and laughed, and it came out clean.

"So the better door," he said. "The human one."

"Bigger room," Naila said, not looking up from her ledger.

Bashir was already turning his pen over, already reaching for the next line of clay, hungry — and through the awning's edge, in the lane, a car they didn't recognise slowed at the top of the rise, idled, and did not come down.

Edith saw it first. Her hand left Yusuf's and went, by old reflex, toward the bag at her feet — and then stopped, and stayed empty, open, ready to ask before it took anything at all.

# Chapter 18 — The Wink

Edith had photographed a hundred cars that did not come down a road. She knew the grammar of it. A car that meant to arrive committed to the descent; a car that meant to *watch* paused at the lip of the slope, where it could see the whole awning and the truck and the lane in one frame, where it could leave faster than it came.

This one paused.

She did not lift the camera. That was the discipline now, the thing she had been building rib by rib all month — the hand going to the bag by reflex and then *stopping*, lying open and empty on her own knee. A month ago she'd have had the long lens on the windscreen before she'd finished thinking about it, would have had the plate, the driver's silhouette, the second man's elbow on the door. A month ago she'd have called that *witnessing*. She'd called a lot of things witnessing.

"Don't," she said, very quietly, to nobody, and meant *me*.

Yusuf had stopped laughing. He read money, and money read like a man, and the man in the passenger seat of a car that idled at the top of a rise in the Nineveh plain in the long gold of the afternoon was the kind of man Yusuf had once arranged hotels for. He knew the shoulders. He knew the way a man sat when the work was somebody else's hands but the watching was his.

"That's a look-and-go," Yusuf said. "Somebody's confirming a location."

Narin did not turn around. She was rolling the tablet — the school-text, the one with the beer rations and the line about *Dubsiga, your servant* that Bashir had laughed out loud at three days ago — back into its acid-free tissue with the unbothered hands of a woman who had spent twenty years putting fragile things back into boxes while the world did its worst around her.

"Then it is confirmed," she said. "Now we move it before they decide they are confident."

Out on the lane the man at the truck did not look up. He was crouched at the front wheel with the cayenne tin balanced on his knee and a strip of inner tube in his teeth, doing something to a valve that he had decided was more urgent than the car at the top of the rise. He had, in fact, decided this with the precise timing of a man who had clocked the car before any of them, and had then chosen — visibly, lazily, in full view — to look at a valve instead. He hung the weeping water bag back on the mirror. The wet canvas caught the light. From the rise it would read as *peasant; truck; nothing.* That was, Edith understood, the entire point.

---

The thing she had learned to call *the win* did not look like a win.

It looked like a school gymnasium in the lower town of Erbil with bad fluorescent light and a folding table and four people from the Directorate of Antiquities of the Kurdistan Region — two women, two men, one of them not yet thirty and visibly the smartest person in any room he ever entered — and a list. The list was the win. The list was forty-one inventory numbers, and against thirty of them somebody had handwritten a provenance and a museum and a date, and against eleven of them somebody had written *EN ROUTE / RECOVERED / VERIFY*.

Narin's crew — and it was, by now, plainly *Narin's* crew, the way a thing becomes someone's not by claiming it but by being the one who knows what it's for — had handed over a notebook, a card of microSD, and a single afternoon's worth of reading that turned a crate the network had been moving as *Sumerian astronomical archive (UNTRANSLATED) — significant* into a crate that the verify-column now described, in dry Kurdish bureaucratic shorthand, as *administrative; barley; lexical; school exercise.*

That was the whole murder. Not a gunfight. A reclassification.

"You understand what you have done," the young one said, the smart one, in English, because Bashir had switched them into English so the foreigners could follow and then immediately regretted the courtesy because it cost the man some of his music. "You have made it worthless."

"We have made it *cheap*," Narin said. "Which is the only thing that has ever protected anything in this country. The expensive things leave. The cheap things stay home and get read by students."

Edith had her camera on the table, lens-down, both hands flat on either side of it like a woman keeping a lid on a pot. She had asked. She had asked the young man and the two women and the older man, in turn, through Bashir, in three languages, the same flat question: *may I photograph the list, not your faces, only the list, and only if it helps and never if it hurts.* The older woman had looked at her for a long time. The older woman had been in Mosul in 2014 when the network's predecessors had filmed themselves taking sledgehammers to the museum there for a different streaming audience, and she knew exactly what a camera was for, and she had said: *the numbers. Not the room.*

So Edith shot the numbers and not the room. Forty-one of them, in dry light, no faces, no flag, no folding table, no anguish. A document. It was the best photograph she had taken in nine years and she would never sell it and that was, she had begun to suspect, the same fact.

---

The streaming-doc people heard about the reclassification on a Tuesday.

Yusuf heard about *them* hearing about it, because Yusuf still had two numbers in his phone from the wrong years and one of them texted him a single line — *the Mesopotamia ep is going dark, segment 4 cut, you anywhere near this?* — and Yusuf sat very still under the awning for about a minute and then put the phone face-down on the cloth and pressed his palm on top of it like Edith pressing her lens to the table, like the whole crew had become a set of people who knew what to do with the things in their hands now, which was mostly to leave them where they were.

"Segment four," he said. "That's the gold one."

Naila looked up from her ledger. She had been doing what she did, which was the only honest kind of arithmetic in the whole business — converting the network's grey-market price-sheet into base-ten and then watching the numbers refuse to be astronomy. "The gold-mining one," she said. "The visitors-came-for-the-gold one." Her mouth did a small flat thing that was, on Naila, a grin. "It's the same gold every time. African gold, anunnaki gold. It's always the gold, with these people. They cannot imagine someone coming a very long way for anything but gold, because gold is the only reason *they* would come."

"That," said Bashir, delighted, pen in the air, "is the most savage sentence anyone has said this entire month and you said it about *accountancy*."

"It is accountancy," Naila said. "The myth is the markup. I've shown you the spreadsheet."

She had. Twice. The seam of the whole thing, laid out in a column: a tablet read as *school text* sells for the price of a school text; the *same tablet* photographed badly, lit blue, captioned *DECODE THE ANUNNAKI STAR MAP THEY DON'T WANT YOU TO SEE*, and then *never actually translated*, sells for forty times that — and, crucially, gets read by no one who would ever notice it says *Dubsiga, your servant, two measures of barley.* The woo was not a side-effect. The woo was the *packaging.* Keep the question loud, keep the tablet untranslated, keep it moving. A translated tablet is just a tablet. A *mystery* is a commodity.

"So the doc dies because the tablet got read," Yusuf said. He took his hand off the phone. He looked, Edith thought, like a man who had walked a long way to a door he was sure led outside, and opened it, and found the actual sky on the other side, bigger than the painted one he'd been heading for, and was still standing in the doorway adjusting his eyes.

"Segment four dies," Narin corrected, gently, because she corrected everyone gently and absolutely, "because southern Iraq filed an injunction about chain-of-custody and the production company's lawyers looked at the inventory and decided the *story* was now a liability instead of an asset. The tablet did not kill it. *The provenance* killed it. People keep wanting the magic to be the reading." She tapped the rolled tissue bundle. "The reading is wonderful. But the thing that saved this object is paperwork."

---

It was Mira who saw the second thing, the larger thing, the thing none of their mouths had said.

She had been watching Narin's face for three days — not the tablets, *the face*, the way Narin's face did one thing when she talked about the Flood and a completely different thing when she talked about the Exile, the way a particular stillness came over the lower half of her face whenever the conversation got near the genealogy, near the awful and beautiful fact at the centre of everything: that the oldest stories in the Bible her people had carried out of Nineveh and Babylon, the Flood and the garden and the tower, were in conversation with stories a thousand years older, written down on the very clay they were now arguing over, and that the *route* of that conversation ran straight through the Babylonian Exile — through the conquest, through the deportation, through the worst thing that ever happened to her people's ancestors becoming the soil in which the words took their final root.

Mira had read it on her without a word being said. And now she signed it, badly, lovingly, her hands finding the shapes the crew had been teaching each other all month — *you. don't. say. it. out loud* — and pointed two fingers gently at Narin, and waited.

The crew went quiet. They had learned, finally, this month, to go quiet *for* Mira and not *around* her — to let her finish, to not talk over the slow careful sentence with three faster ones, because the entire month she had been talked over by everyone including herself and the worst of it was how normal it had felt, how *expected*, and then one afternoon Bashir had simply stopped mid-word with his hand still up and turned to watch her hands and said *go on, finish,* and something had shifted in the room that nobody narrated.

Narin watched the hands. Mira repeated it. *You read the clay out loud all day. The genealogy. You don't read that one out loud.*

Bashir started to translate it and Narin lifted one finger — *I have it* — because of course she had it, because she read faces too, everyone in her family read faces, you did not survive a minority on the Nineveh plain without reading faces, and she sat with it for a moment under the awning with the kettle ticking and the diesel-and-dust smell and the man outside doing nothing to a valve.

"No," she said at last. "I don't read that one out loud."

She turned the rolled tissue over once in her hands.

"Because the West has only ever known two ways to hold this," she said. "One: *the Bible is true and unique and dropped out of the sky, and these older clay versions are demonic counterfeits or coincidence.* Two: *the Bible has older sources, therefore the Bible is a fraud, a plagiarism, a fairy tale for the gullible, and we clever people have caught it out.* And both of those are the same insult wearing different hats. Both of them think the only thing a story can do is *be true or be stolen.*" Her face did the thing Mira had been watching for three days. "My people did a third thing. My people *did the third thing for a living.*"

She set the bundle down.

"Genesis knows the older stories. It is not hiding it. It is *arguing* with them. The Babylonians say the world was made when a god split a goddess's corpse like a fish — combat, blood, the strong god butchers the chaos and props the sky on her ribs. And the Hebrew writer takes that whole world of furniture, the *tehom*, the deep, the cognate of her very name, and builds a creation where there is no fight at all. No corpse. No butchery. A *word.* 'Let there be.' The Babylonians say humans were kneaded out of clay and a murdered god's blood to *carry the gods' baskets* — made to toil, made to die, slaves with a divine spark for a back. And the Hebrew writer keeps the clay — keeps the dust, *you are dust* — and changes the *one thing that matters.* Not slaves. *Image.* The same clay. A completely different verdict on what the clay is *for.*"

The kettle ticked.

"That is not theft," Narin said. "That is the bravest thing a conquered people ever did. They were marched a thousand miles into the empire that destroyed them, sat down in the shadow of its ziggurats and its myths and its priests, and instead of breaking, they took the empire's own creation story and rewrote it into an argument *against* the empire. *You* say we are slaves of the gods? We are the image of God. *You* say the world is a battlefield? The world is a sentence, spoken on purpose, called good. They out-wrote Babylon *in Babylon.* With Babylon's own bricks." She looked at her own hands. "How could I read that out loud as if it were a *catch?* As if I had *gotten one over* on my own grandmother? It is the most sophisticated act of survival in human literature and the West keeps trying to make it into either a magic trick or a forgery, because the West cannot conceive of a third thing, which is a *people refusing to die by taking the worst thing that ever happened to them and turning it into scripture.*"

Nobody touched it. They had learned that too.

After a while Frik — who had been very still, who had a tic going in his left shoulder that the crew no longer pretended not to see and no longer made into a problem — said, in the flat blurted voice that came when something was too true to be sat on:

"They didn't lose."

Everyone looked at him.

"The Babylonians," Frik said, and his hand jumped once, and he let it. "Conquered them. Burned the temple. *Won.*" The shoulder again. "But we don't read *Enuma Elish* on Sunday. They read—" and here the echolalia caught Narin's own word and threw it back, helplessly, exactly, "—*image. Image. Image.*"

It was the truest thing said under the awning all month and it had come out sideways, the way the truest things came out of Frik, the involuntary verdict that nobody could un-say or paint over, and Narin looked at him with the particular respect she gave the tablets.

"Yes," she said. "That is exactly it. The empire turned to dust. The argument with the empire is still being read in a thousand languages this morning." She almost smiled. "That is the joke at the bottom of everything. Nobody intended it. But it's there."

---

So where, then, was the hole. The Why Files always had a hole, and Bashir — who loved holes, who had spent a whole career living in the gaps between languages where the puns lived — went looking for it, because a story that closes is a story you don't trust.

"All right," he said. "Then what's still open. Genuinely. Not woo. Not aliens — the aliens are dead, we killed them in the clay, *nibiru* is Jupiter and the *sar* is a number and poor Mr. Dubsiga got his desk turned into a starship. Not that. What's the thing we *honestly don't know.*"

Narin thought about it properly. That was the other thing about her — she did not pretend to certainties she didn't have, which was the rarest quality in anyone the crew had met since the Beast first rolled north.

"The *how,*" she said. "We know the Hebrew primeval history is in dialogue with Mesopotamian literature. That's not a guess, that's a library. We know the Flood, the garden, the tower, the great long-lived ancestors, the *clay,* all have older cousins in the cuneiform. We know the Exile is the great pressure-cooker where so much of it got finalised. That much is mainstream, bulletproof, you can read the tablets yourself, George Smith stood up in 1872 and read a Babylonian flood off a broken tablet to a room full of Victorians who very nearly fainted, it is *done.*"

"But," said Yusuf.

"But the *exact mechanism.* The wiring. *Who* sat *where,* with *which* tablet, *when,* hearing it told *how,* and decided to keep the dust and change the verdict. Whether a Judean scribe read Akkadian in a Babylonian school, or whether it came down through generations of oral telling, or some braid of both that we will never untangle because the looms are gone. *That* we do not have. The fact of the genealogy — solid. The blood-vessels of it — gone." She spread her hands. "The connections are real and the details are mostly lost. That is the honest position. Anybody who tells you they know the precise channel is selling you something. And anybody who tells you the channel isn't *there* hasn't read the tablets."

"That's the hole," Bashir said, satisfied, settling back.

"That's the hole," Narin agreed. "And it is a *good* hole. It is a hole you can spend your life in and come out richer. It is not a hole you fill with a spaceship."

---

The car at the top of the rise had gone, at some point. Edith hadn't seen it leave and was glad, in a new way, not to have a photograph of its leaving.

There was a different thing she did have, though it was not on any card and never would be, because she'd had the lens at her hip when it happened and had — deliberately, with a small ache that she understood now to be the correct ache — not raised it.

Out on the lane, the man from the truck had gone, an hour ago, up the slope to where Narin's uncle had set a second awning by the wall, a low stretched square of canvas the colour of weak tea, and the two of them had sat down on a pair of plastic chairs that did not match, and the uncle had made tea in the small loud way of a man who has made tea ten thousand times, and they had passed *one glass.* One small clear glass of orange tea, back and forth, the way the man had once passed a cup in a place none of them had been, the way the uncle passed it to anyone he decided was *people.* They had not talked much. The man did not have the words; the uncle did not have the man's. They had sat in the long gold light at the edge of the Nineveh plain, two men who could not read a single sign off the clay, passing one glass, while the people who *could* read it argued under the lower awning about how the world had taught itself to write.

Edith had watched it for a full minute through nothing but her own eyes. It was the most photographable thing she had seen in nine years and she had let it go through her and out the other side and kept it the only place a thing like that survives, which is not a card. The network's people, wherever they'd been parked with their good glass, had never gotten a frame of him either. Three weeks of watching and the man had never once been still in the open with his face up at the right moment. The light loved him and the lens never landed.

When he came back down the slope he had the cayenne tin out and a small fold of the always-clean hanky pressed to a nick on his thumb from the valve, and he didn't come in under the awning — he stopped at the edge, in the lane, where the story's *meaning* couldn't reach him and he didn't want it to.

"We can run them to Erbil at first light," he said. To Narin. Only to Narin. *The locals own the win and the win is hers.* "The crate goes with your directorate. We're just the truck." A beat. "*Spas,*" he said, in Kurdish, badly, the courtesy handful, and the uncle behind him barked a laugh at the accent and said something back that made even Narin smile.

Then he went to check the water bag again, though it did not need checking, and the camera did not follow him, because the camera belonged to the crew, and the crew were under the awning, where the last of the light was going amber on the rolled white bundle of a four-thousand-year-old worksheet about barley.

Narin picked it up. She held it the way you hold the thing itself and not the price of the thing.

"Roll the tape," Bashir said, because someone had to, and they were all a little drunk on tea and survival.

"There's no tape," Naila said, not looking up. "There never was a tape. There was clay." She closed her ledger. "Which is better. Tape rots in fifty years. We've still got the homework of a man who was bad at fractions in 1700 BC."

And the question hung there in the amber light, unanswered, allowed to stay unanswered — not *were they aliens,* that was dead and buried in the original language, but the bigger one, the one with the room in it: *what were they for, the princely offspring, the made-from-clay, and who taught Genesis to bake its bricks the Babylonian way, and whose hand* — somewhere, off every lens, in some quiet office where the loud tablets got promoted and the quiet ones disappeared — *had been deciding for years which of these stories the world was allowed to hear at all.*

Nobody answered. That was the whole art of it.

Out past the awning, southern of here, beyond the checkpoints and the longing and the line on the map, the real cradle lay in the dusk — Ur, Uruk, Eridu, the mounds where writing was born — in the hands of the people who lived there now.

Narin set the bundle down and reached for the kettle.

"Tea," she said. "And then I'll show you the one with the joke in it. It only works in Sumerian."

# Chapter 19 — The Line in the Clay

The last room in the archive did not announce itself. It had no number on the door, only a strip of masking tape with a hand-written shelf code, and a key that Huda turned twice because the lock was old and the building older.

Inside: a long table under fluorescent tubes, two of which had given up. Steel shelving down both walls. The smell Naila had been chasing the whole trip — baked earth, dust, the faint chemical sweetness of consolidant — concentrated here into something almost edible. She breathed it in through her mouth so she could taste it.

"Storage," Huda said. "The boring word for it. Things that are not on display and may never be. Things waiting for someone with time."

"And we are the someone with time," Omar said, already moving, already touching the edge of a shelf and then not touching it because Huda had looked at him.

"You are the someone with permission," Huda corrected. "For three hours."

Mira had her notebook out before she had a chair. Lens stood in the doorway with the camera at his hip, not raised. He had not raised it much in days now, and nobody had asked him to. Naila had noticed. She noticed the camera the way she noticed weather — a pressure that came and went.

Huda crossed to a shelf and pulled, with both hands and great care, a shallow tray. She set it on the table on a square of grey foam.

"This," she said, "is not in any catalogue you have read."

---

The streaming crew, four cities and three weeks ago, had ended their Anunnaki segment standing under Room 55's lights, and the host had said the line they all said: *the truth is written in stone, if only they'd let us read it.* He had meant it as a flourish. Naila had carried it around like a pebble in a shoe ever since, because it was so nearly true and so completely wrong. The truth *was* written. Nobody was hiding it. It was simply written in a language that took eleven years to learn to read with your eyes and longer to read, as she did, with the pads of her fingers across the back of a museum photograph because the front was too precious to press.

The official version was clean and Naila respected it the way she respected a good lock. There was no twelfth planet. *Nibiru* meant *crossing-point* and the crossing-point was Jupiter, the largest light that wandered, visible most clear nights to anyone with the patience to look up. The Anunnaki were *the princely offspring* — gods, the children of An, the sky — and they sat on thrones in the seals, not in cockpits. The famous "solar system" on VA 243 was a star-cluster and a dedication to a man named Dubsiga, *may his god protect him*, the ancient equivalent of a brass plate by a door. The 3,600 that Sitchin had spent a lifetime building into an orbit was a *šar*, a numeral, base-sixty doing what base-sixty had always done. All of it was demolished, properly, by people who loved the texts. Naila revered the demolition. She had read it twice.

What she could not stop thinking about was what the demolition stopped at.

It stopped at *the aliens aren't real,* dusted its hands, and walked out of the room. And on its way out it walked straight past the stranger thing, the thing sitting here in a tray on grey foam, which was that the most-read book on the planet had a grandfather in this language, and the grandfather was older by a thousand years.

---

The tablet was a fragment. Less than half. The break ran on a diagonal, top-right to bottom-left, the clay shearing where it had wanted to shear, and Naila felt the old pull to apologise to it.

"Atrahasis," Huda said. "Or a piece of a school-copy of it. A student's hand, I think. Look at the wedges."

Mira leaned in. "They're—"

"Crowded. Yes. The line-spacing collapses at the right margin every time. He ran out of room and would not start a new line." Huda's mouth did something dry. "A boy who could not plan ahead. Three thousand seven hundred years ago, in Nippur or near it, and he could not plan ahead, and his teacher made him copy this anyway."

Naila put her fingertips just above the surface, not on it, the way you hold your hand near a fire to find the edge of its heat.

"May I read?"

Huda set a photograph beside the tray — a raking-light image, the wedges thrown into hard shadow — and slid it to her. "Read the photograph. Touch the photograph."

Naila read the photograph with her fingers.

The wedges resolved under the pads the way they always did — first as texture, then as rhythm, then, with a lurch she never got used to, as *voice.* Someone had pressed these. A reed stylus, the triangular tip, the wrist-turn for the diagonal Winkelhaken. She could feel where the boy had pressed too hard near the start of a line, fresh and confident, and where he had gone shallow at the end, tired, the clay drying, the light going.

"It's the making," she said. The room had gone quiet around her in the way rooms did when she read aloud, and she didn't notice, which was its own kind of mercy. "Nintu — the womb-goddess — she's. They've killed a god. For the blood." Her finger stopped. "*ṭiṭṭam*. Clay. They mix the clay with the blood and the *eṭemmu*—" she said the word the way it was, with the doubled consonant sitting in the throat — "the ghost. The spirit. So that the dead god is *in* the clay, so the new thing will have a—" she searched, and the searching was the only thing on her face — "a drum. A heartbeat. *uppu*. So that there will be a drumbeat in the flesh, and the living will hear it, and remember they are made of someone who died."

Nobody said anything. The fluorescent tube that worked buzzed.

"To do the work," Omar said quietly. "The gods are tired of digging the canals. So they make us. To carry it."

"To carry it," Naila agreed. "And to die, so the gods don't have to. The drum is the ghost. The ghost is the death already inside the clay." She lifted her fingers off the photograph and looked at her own hand as if it had told her something. "That's the wage. You get to be a person. The price is you carry a dead god and you end."

---

Mira had her own photograph out now, from her own folder, and she laid it on the table next to Huda's. The same raking light. Different museum, different ink-stamp in the corner. A different language.

"Genesis 2," she said. "*wayyîṣer YHWH elohîm et-ha'adam afar min-ha'adamah.* God formed the man, dust from the ground. *adam* from *adamah.* The man from the soil. And then he breathes." She tapped the photo. "*nišmat ḥayyîm.* The breath of life. So that the dust will live."

She put the two photographs side by side and did not say anything else, because the point made itself and Mira had learned, late and at cost, that the worst thing you could do to a point that made itself was explain it.

Clay and a breath. Clay and a ghost. The man from the *adamah.* The thing from the *ṭiṭṭam.* A thousand years and four hundred miles and a marched, defeated hill-tribe sitting in the libraries of the people who had marched them, finding the older draft of their own beginning written in the enemy's hand, and carrying it home, and keeping what they could use and changing what they could not bear.

"Here's the part nobody put on the television," Mira said. "It isn't theft. Nobody copied anybody. Look what's *gone.*" Her finger moved between the two. "In Atrahasis the man is made because the gods are sick of working and they need a slave that dies so they can rest. In Genesis the man is made and put in a garden and told to *tend* it, and the dying comes later, as a wound, as a thing that went wrong. Same clay. They threw out the slavery. They kept the dust and the breath and they threw out the part where you exist to dig somebody else's canal forever."

"That," said Huda, "is the most interesting sentence anyone has said in this room in a year."

---

This was the potato, and Naila felt it land in her chest the way the heat had landed in her sinuses. The easy story — *aliens made us to mine gold* — was easy because it was whole. It had a beginning and a planet and a motive and a slick host under good lights. It survived precisely because nothing in it had ever broken.

The real thing was a fragment. The real thing was less than half a tablet, sheared on the diagonal, copied by a boy who could not plan ahead. The line that ran from the *ṭiṭṭam* to the *adamah* — the actual, traceable, mainstream-scholarship line, the genealogy of the most-read book on Earth — did not run through stone. It ran through *loss.* It ran through fire and flood and looting and a school-tablet that survived because someone happened to throw it on a rubbish heap that happened to bake instead of rot. For every line Naila could read with her fingers there were ten she could not, because they were gone, dust gone back to dust, no breath in them.

The truth was not hidden. That was the host's lie, the comforting one. The truth was *partial,* and partial was harder, because partial meant you had to be honest about the gap and you could not fill it with a planet.

"How much is missing," Lens asked. It was the first thing he'd said. He still had not raised the camera.

Huda looked at the fragment for a long moment.

"From this tablet? Half." She gestured, a small flat motion that took in the shelves, the building, the country, the century. "From the whole? We don't know what we don't have. That is the true answer and nobody likes it. We find a new line every decade that rewrites a sentence everyone was sure of. There could be a tablet in a basement in a city I cannot name that ends this argument forever, and it has been there for ninety years under a wrong label, and the man who could read it is dead, and his student went to America." The dryness again, thinner now. "Or it was sold in 2003 for the price of a car."

---

It was Huda who found the hand.

She had been quiet while they argued, the way she had been quiet all trip, watching them be loud about her ancestors' genius with the patience of a woman who knew it was *hers* to be loud about and was choosing, for reasons of her own, to let them have the noise. Now she pulled the lamp closer, the one with the bend in its neck, and angled it low across the broken edge of the fragment, almost flat to the surface.

"Come here. Not you—" as Omar surged — "you. The one who reads with her hands. You'll see it best."

Naila came.

In the raking light, along the very bottom of the surviving clay, below the last full line, were three wedges. Cramped. A name-sized cluster, the size a person makes when they want to be remembered but don't have room and aren't sure they're allowed.

"A colophon," Huda said. "The scribe signs his work. Date, name, sometimes a prayer. Most of it's broken off — it ran into the part that's gone."
