Her finger hovered, the nail just clear of the surface, the way Naila's did, the same instinct in a different woman. "But this survives. *Ip-pu.* Part of a name. And *ṣe.* The start of *ṣeḫru* — *the young one.* The junior. The student."

"His name's broken," Mira said.

"His name's broken," Huda agreed. "Forever, probably. I will never know what to call him. But I know he was a boy who could not plan ahead, who pressed too hard at the start of a line and ran shallow at the end, who copied the story of why we die and signed it *the young one* because his teacher made him." She straightened. Something had gone very still and very private in her face, and Naila looked away from it because she had been taught, finally, by these people, that some faces are not for reading. "He is not in any catalogue. He has no number. He is the hand. I am going to mark the hand."

She took a pencil — a soft pencil, archival — and on the foam beside the tray, not on the clay, never on the clay, she wrote a small bracket and a question mark and a single word in Arabic, and Naila did not ask what it said, and Huda did not offer, and that was correct.

---

Lens raised the camera. Once. He did not photograph the tablet.

He photographed the seam.

Because that was the thing none of them had said, the thing that was sitting on the table in two photographs and one broken tray, and it was Naila, in the end, who saw it the way Lens had already framed it.

There was a join. Not in the clay — the clay was sheared and would never be whole. The join was in the air above the table, between Huda's photograph and Mira's, between the *ṭiṭṭam* and the *adamah,* between the boy who signed himself *the young one* and the marched tribe that carried his grandfather's story home and threw out the slavery and kept the dust. You could *see* it. The myth and the history were lying side by side under one tired fluorescent tube, and the line between them was right there, visible, a hand's breadth of foam and a thousand years.

Visible. And not closed.

The seam ran straight off the edge of the surviving clay and into the part that was gone, where the boy's full name was, where the line went next, where the rest of the answer waited under a wrong label in a basement in a city Huda could not name — or didn't.

"What were they *for,*" Omar said softly, the question that was always coming, the only honest one left. "The Anunnaki. The princely offspring. Not the planet — that's dead. The *idea.* What's it for."

Huda picked up the bracketed foam and held it a moment, the unnamed hand cradled in graphite.

"Ask him," she said, and set it down beside the break.

Outside, the call to prayer started up over Nasiriyah, and the fragment sat in its light, signed and unfinished, and nobody reached to close the gap, because the gap was the whole point, and it was hers.

# Chapter 20 — Inheritance

The tea came in glasses too hot to hold, which was the point. You waited. You let the steam do its work on the back of your hand and you waited, and while you waited the man who'd poured it watched the street the way everyone in Baghdad watched the street — not afraid, just literate, reading the traffic for grammar Mira couldn't parse.

They were on a balcony above Mutanabbi Street, the booksellers' street, where the Friday market had spilled paper across the pavement until the road itself looked typeset. Below them a boy was selling Qur'ans and pirated calculus textbooks off the same blanket. A man in a wheelchair sold dictionaries. Somebody had laid out a run of water-stained Agatha Christies in English, spines up, like fish on ice.

"You know she wrote here," Omar said. He had his feet on the railing and a glass balanced on his knee in a way that made Lens, two chairs down, physically unable to relax. "Christie. *Murder in Mesopotamia.* Her husband dug at Ur. She washed the pottery. Mallowan. She used to clean three-thousand-year-old ivory with her face cream."

"That's apocryphal," Mira said.

"It's *true* apocryphal. There's a difference." He tipped his head back. "Everybody who comes here writes the place down and takes it home. That's the whole — that's the entire racket, isn't it. You come, you look, you leave with a book."

Below, the boy folded a Qur'an in newspaper for a buyer, handling it with two hands, the way you'd lift something asleep.

---

Here is the part that is true, and that nobody disputes, and that you can check tonight against a library catalogue without leaving your chair.

The stories travelled. Not metaphorically — physically, on baked clay and in human memory, down trade roads and exile roads, from a flooded southern marsh into a hill kingdom that would one day be marched, in chains, back down those same roads into Babylon. The Flood narrative of *Gilgamesh* Tablet XI sits in the British Museum, K.3375, and it is older than the Flood narrative in Genesis by something on the order of a thousand years. This is not a fringe reading. It is in the standard commentaries. Hermann Gunkel wrote it down before the First World War. It is taught, in seminaries, to people who go on to preach the text with their whole hearts and lose nothing in the trade.

A man builds a boat. He seals it with pitch. He sends out birds to test the water — a dove, a swallow, a raven. The waters fall. He makes a sacrifice on a mountain, and the gods, or God, smell the savour and are moved.

That is two stories. It is also, in the bones, one story, twice.

The official account of how this happened is sober and well-evidenced and beautiful: a small people, defeated, deported, sat for two generations in the libraries of the empire that had broken them, and found there the older drafts of their own beginnings — and did not throw them away. They *answered* them. They took the council of squabbling gods and made it one God. They took the flood sent because humanity was *noisy* and made it a flood sent because humanity was *cruel*. They argued with the source. You can hear the argument if you read the two side by side, and the argument is the inheritance.

So. That is the official story, and it is correct, and the crew had spent five chapters loving it.

---

"I keep getting stuck on the verb," Mira said.

Omar waited. He'd learned to wait; it had taken him eleven months and Naila standing on his foot twice.

"Inherit. We say a culture *inherits* a story like it's a watch in a drawer. Like it just — sits there, and then it's yours, and you wind it." She turned her glass a quarter-turn on the rail, lining the seam up with the railing's edge without deciding to. "But you can't inherit a watch you have to rebuild from someone else's blueprint in a language you only half speak while the previous owner is standing in the room telling you you've got it wrong."

"That's most inheritances," Omar said. "You should meet my uncles."

"It's not ownership. That's the — that's the thing I can't make come out clean." She frowned at the street. "Sitchin owned it. That's what people loved. He gave you a thing you could *hold*. The gods came down, they mined the gold, they made us out of apes to swing the picks, and here's the seal that proves it, here's the planet, here's the orbit — it's *yours*, you possess it now, you know the secret. Ownership feels like understanding. It's the cheapest version of understanding there is and it feels exactly like the real one."

*"Twelve planets,"* came Hecklefish's voice from the laptop propped in the chair between them, screen turned to the shade. *"He counted twelve planets in a four-thousand-year-old picture the size of a postage stamp and one of them was a man named Dubsiga and the genre's been running on it for fifty years. I'd be embarrassed. I'm a fish. I have no skull. I'd still be embarrassed."*

"He's not wrong," Mira said, "and he should still shut up."

*"The seal's a dedication. 'To Dubsiga, from his servant, may he be well.' It's a get-well card. They turned a get-well card into a solar system."* The fish paused. *"It's almost beautiful. He wanted it so bad."*

That last part Mira let stand, because it was the only honest thing the heckle had ever said.

---

The hole, the real one, the one the debunk leaves open because it isn't *its* hole to close:

Everyone agrees the alien reading is dead. Sumerologists agree, Assyriologists agree, the dictionary agrees — *nibiru* is "crossing-place," it's Jupiter at its station, you can see it from a roof in Nasiriyah on a clear night without a spaceship in sight; *Anunnaki* is "the princely offspring," gods on thrones, not pilots in cockpits; the three-thousand-six-hundred is a base-sixty number, the same arithmetic that still cuts your hour into minutes. Killed in the clay. The crew had watched it die. It was a clean death.

But here is what the clean death does not tell you. It does not tell you *why the stories moved at all.*

Because they didn't have to. A defeated people, dragged to the centre of the world that defeated them, could have refused the library. Could have sat in Babylon and held their own small story shut like a fist and called the rest blasphemy. Plenty of conquered peoples did exactly that and the stories died with them in a single generation, unrecorded, and we will never know what was lost because losing it was the whole event.

These ones didn't refuse. They *read the enemy's books.* They took the noise-flood and the boat and the birds and the savour-of-the-sacrifice and they carried it home and built it into the holiest thing they had, and they did it so well that three thousand years later a man in a wheelchair on Mutanabbi Street could sell that exact story, the flood and the boat and the dove, folded in newspaper, to a buyer who handled it like something asleep.

The transmission is the hole. Not *whether* — *why.* Nobody in any of the standard commentaries can tell you why a beaten people inherits its conqueror's draft instead of burning it. That is not a settled question. That is not even a scholar's question, really. It's the question under all of it, and the field is honest enough to leave it open.

The crew had spent the whole journey thinking the prize was the texts.

The prize was the verb.

---

Huda found them an hour later, climbing the balcony stairs with a folder under her arm and dust on her good shoes. She refused the tea, then took it, then held it without drinking the way the local always did — the tea was an excuse, not a beverage.

"My student," she said, without preamble, because preamble was for people with time, "is teaching her own students this week. First-years. Sumerian sign-list. *Ah*, *engur*, *abzu*." She set the folder on the table. "I sat at the back. I have not sat at the back of a room in twenty years. It was unbearable. She got the determinative for *deity* wrong twice and I did not correct her and I aged a decade."

"She got it wrong, or the textbook got it wrong?" Omar asked.

"The textbook is German and forty years old and assumes you already know what it is teaching you." Huda's mouth did the thing that was almost a smile. "She corrected the textbook. In front of the class. Then she got it wrong again. Both, in the same hour. That is what teaching is."

Mira watched the folder, not Huda — she always watched the made thing first, it was where her eye went, it was how she was built. Photocopies. A first-year's handout, the signs printed large, the wedges drawn fat and patient so a sixteen-year-old's hand could find them.

"Can I ask something rude," Mira said.

"You will regardless."

"Does it bother you that we're here. Not — Archer. Us. People who'll leave. People who'll write it down and take it home." She made herself look up. "We came for the same thing the streaming crews come for. The story. We're — better behaved. But we're the same shape. We come, we look, we leave with a book."

Huda set the tea down. Down on the street the booksellers were starting to pack, folding the blankets up over the spines, the road going back to being a road.

"You think the question is whether you own it," she said.

"I think I just realised that's the wrong question and I don't know the right one yet."

"No." Huda picked up the first-year's handout, the fat patient wedges, and turned it so Mira could see. "Nobody owns this. *I* don't own this. I have given my whole life to it and it is not mine. It was not the scribes' — they copied what was already old. It was not Babylon's; Babylon found it in the south and carried it north. The Hebrews did not own it; they argued with it, and you do not argue with what you own, you argue with what was given to you that you did not ask for." She set the handout down between them, very lightly, the way the man below had folded the Qur'an. "Ownership is the alien story. *Mine, the secret, I possess it now.* That is Sitchin's whole gift and it is why people loved him and I will not laugh at them for it because it is a real hunger and he fed it with sawdust."

*"Sawdust,"* the fish said, with deep satisfaction. *"Write that down."*

"What's the right question, then," Mira said.

Huda looked at her for a long moment, and Mira braced for the answer, because it was Huda's to give — it had always been Huda's to give, the whole way, that was the only law that had never bent — and Mira had finally learned to sit still inside the gap and not reach for it.

Huda gave it.

"Not *who owns it.* *Who is in the room when it is passed.*" She tapped the handout. "My student is in the room. Her students are in the room. You four are in the room, today, on this balcony, with the wrong tea, asking rude questions. That is all anyone has ever been. The scribe was a witness. The exile was a witness. The man who wrote Genesis was a witness to something a thousand years older that he did not own and could not have owned and would not have wanted to own — he wanted to *answer* it. To be in the room and say *no, not noise — cruelty. Not many gods — one. Here is what I would change.*" She drank her tea, finally, all of it, fast. "You inherit a story the way you inherit a language. Not by owning a word. By being trusted to be in the room when it is said, and to say it again, a little wrong, to someone younger, who will correct you, and get it wrong themselves."

Omar had gone very still, which for Omar was a kind of shout.

"We're not owners," he said.

"You were never going to be owners. You're not equipped." Huda almost smiled again. "You're witnesses. It is a smaller job than the streaming people think they are doing. It is the only job there has ever been."

---

The booksellers were gone by the time they came down, the road bare and grey and ordinary, the way the floor of Room 55 was ordinary under the glass, the way a roof in Nasiriyah was ordinary under Jupiter. Mira stood on the empty pavement where the spines had been laid out like fish on ice and felt the shape of the thing settle without closing.

They had walked in through the alien door. *Were the gods aliens.* The grandest, gaudiest door the genre had ever built, and they'd loved the people who built it, and they'd watched the answer die honest in the clay: no. Killed clean. The crossing-place is Jupiter. The seal is a get-well card. The princely offspring sit on thrones.

And they'd come out the far side into the bigger room, and the bigger room was this: an empty street where the oldest story we can read had just been sold, folded in newspaper, by a man who did not own it to a buyer who did not own it, in a city that had been carrying it longer than anyone had been writing it down. A first-year, this week, getting the sign for *god* wrong, twice, in front of a teacher who let her.

The why was still open. Why does a beaten people read its conqueror's books. Why does anyone carry the older draft home instead of burning it. Huda had not closed it. Nobody had closed it. It sat there on the empty pavement, the size of the whole road, and it was not theirs to close, and Mira found she could stand inside it now without flinching, which was the only thing the journey had actually taught her.

"What were they *for*," Omar said quietly. "The Anunnaki. That was the whole question, the start. What were they for."

Lens lowered the camera. He'd been shooting the empty street, the blankets gone, and now he let it hang.

"They were for being in the room," he said, "when it got passed."

Up the stairs behind them, a glass clinked. Huda, going back to her students. The road, going back to being a road. And somewhere south, the river that all of it had come up from, running the way it had run before anyone could read, carrying nothing it owned, downstream, toward the sea.

# Chapter 21 — The Restoration

The light at Ur comes up the colour of the brick it falls on, so that for the first hour of morning you cannot tell where the ziggurat ends and the day begins. Edith had been told this by three different people and had not believed any of them. Now she stood on the lowest of the great staircases with the camera dead in her hands and believed it completely, and did not raise the lens, because there are things the lens makes smaller by trying.

She raised it anyway. It was the job.

Through the viewfinder the staircase climbed in three flights toward a platform that no longer held the shrine it was built to hold — the shrine was four thousand years gone, the god with it — and the brick went up in courses laid by men whose names were pressed, some of them, into the very bricks under her feet. *For Nanna, his king, Ur-Nammu, king of Ur, king of Sumer and Akkad, the one who built the temple of Nanna.* Huda had read it to her yesterday off a brick the size of a paving stone, running two fingers along the wedges the way a person reads a face they have known a long time, and the reading had taken eight seconds, and Edith had spent the afternoon thinking about the eight seconds.

"You're shooting the steps again," Omar said, behind her, not moving, which for Omar was a kind of event.

"I'm shooting the joins."

"That's the steps."

"It's where two days of work meet," Edith said. "Whoever laid the lower course went home, and somebody else came back the next morning and started where he stopped. You can see the seam." She lowered the camera. "I like seams."

---

The official story, the one the foundation's people would have filmed here if they'd gotten the permit — and they had tried; Archer had tried three times, charmingly, with letters on heavy paper — was simple and shining and had been told ten thousand times. *They could not have built this. Look at the scale. Look at the precision of the brick. Look at the alignment to the cardinal points, the casing, the engineering of the core. A Bronze Age people, mud-hut people, slave people — they were given this. Someone came down and showed them how.*

It is a beautiful story. It has been believed, in one dress or another, by clever people and kind people, by your uncle and possibly by you at two in the morning, and the camera does not sneer at it, because the camera has stood under the thing and felt the want it answers — the want to make the impossible make sense by handing it up to the sky.

So play it straight. The ziggurat at Ur is one of the best-preserved in Iraq. Its lowest stage was reconstructed in the twentieth century on the ancient core, and the core itself is genuine third-millennium-BCE work: a solid mass of mud-brick faced with a skin of kiln-fired brick set in bitumen, the whole thing pierced with weep-holes so the rain could drain and the mass not burst. Ur-Nammu raised it; his son Shulgi finished it; the Neo-Babylonians under Nabonidus rebuilt the upper stages a millennium and a half later, and Nabonidus left an inscription complaining, essentially, that the old kings had not built high enough.

Now the potato.

Go and make a brick.

Not buy one. Make one, the way they made these — the facing brick, the fired kind. You need clay, and the clay must be cleaned of stone, and you need a temper to keep it from cracking as it dries, chaff or sand in the right proportion, and you need a mould, and you need to know the proportion of water by feel because no one will write it down for you, and you need to dry the green brick *slowly*, out of the direct sun or it skins over and the inside stays wet and it splits, and then you need a kiln, which means you need fuel, and southern Mesopotamia has no forest, so the fuel is reeds and dung and whatever you can float down the river, and you need to know the firing curve — too fast and it shatters, too cool and it stays mud — and the temperature you are reaching for is north of nine hundred degrees, held, in a structure you also had to build, and you are doing this not once but several million times, because the ziggurat at Ur, by conservative count, swallowed brick in the millions, each one a small accomplishment of chemistry that a literate scribe somewhere logged into a ledger of grain paid to the workmen who made them, by name, with their rations.

It is so much harder than a spaceship. That is the secret nobody tells you. The spaceship is *easy.* The spaceship lets you off. The brick is the thing that costs.

---

Huda came up the steps at nine with eleven students behind her and a roll of cloth under her arm, and Edith began, quietly, to work.

The students were from the university at Nasiriyah, four women and seven men, the youngest maybe nineteen, all of them in the bad shoes of people who walk a long way to learn things. Two carried a folding table. One carried a box of the small acid-free trays the conservators use, and in the trays, packed in foam, were tablets — not the famous ones, not the Flood, nothing from behind the glass in London, but local finds, administrative clay, the receipts and contracts and schoolboy exercises that are the true literature of the place: nine hundred and ninety percent of all cuneiform ever excavated is somebody's accounts.

Edith photographed the box being set on the table. She photographed a girl's hands lifting a tablet free of the foam — the careful two-handed lift, the way you'd lift a sleeping animal. She photographed the tablet itself, a palm-sized thing the colour of weak tea, and the wedges across it, and she did not know what they said and that was the point of the frame: *here is the thing, and here is the not-knowing, and over there is the woman who knows.*

"Receipt for barley," Huda said, reading over the girl's shoulder without taking it from her. "Two gur, given out by a man called Lu-Nanna, to a man whose name is broken, in the third year of — " she tilted it to the light " — Ibbi-Sin. Witnessed. Sealed." She looked up at Edith. "Thirty-eight hundred years ago a man counted his barley and somebody wrote it down so no one could cheat him. You want the gods who came down from the sky? *That's* them. The men who invented the receipt. The first people on Earth who decided a thing should be *written so it could not be lied about later.*" A dry beat. "Try doing that with a campfire and a memory."

Naila had come up without anyone hearing her and was crouched at the table now, one finger hovering over a second tablet, not touching. "May I?"

"You'll read it with your hands," Huda said. It was not a question. The two women had taken each other's measure on the first day and arrived at something that was not friendship and was better than friendship for the work — a mutual refusal to perform. "Go on. Tell me what it is before you turn it over."

Naila set two fingertips on the clay and closed her eyes, and Edith lifted the camera and then did not press the shutter, because there is a frame you do not take, and this was one — the woman who reads the dead language with her skin, reading, with the woman who owns the language watching to see if she's right.

"School text," Naila said. "It's a list. It's — repetitions. Someone practising the same sign." Her finger moved. "Over and over. A child."

"A child," Huda agreed. "Learning to write the sign for *king.* Badly. Look — " she turned it for the camera now, deliberately, giving Edith the shot " — see how the third one runs downhill? He was tired. He was four thousand years ago and he was tired and he wanted to go outside." She set it down with great gentleness. "This is what they took from us. Not the gold. Not the spaceships. *Him.* The bored child learning to write. They looked at this — " she lifted her chin at the ziggurat going up gold behind them " — and they could not let it be the bored child's people who built it, so they gave it to aliens, and the aliens were a kindness to themselves, you understand, because if aliens built it then nobody has to feel the size of what they erased."

Omar, very quietly, in Arabic, said something Edith did not catch, and Huda answered him in Arabic, and the students laughed, and Edith did not ask for the translation because some of the room is not yours.

---

Here is the hole, and it is small and it is hard as a kiln-brick and you can check it tonight.

The skeptics won. They were *right.* There is no Planet Nibiru; the word means *crossing-place* and it is Jupiter, and you can watch it cross tonight if the sky is clear; the seal called VA 243 that the genre swears is a map of the solar system is a dedication, in plain cuneiform, to a man called Dubsiga; the Anunnaki are *the princely offspring,* divine functionaries, and they no more flew here in ships than the receipt for barley did. The clay killed the aliens. That part is over, and the people who killed it were doing honest work.

But go and read the *museum label.* Go and read the documentary, the good one, the responsible BBC-voiced one that debunks Sitchin properly. It says: *Sitchin was wrong. Nibiru is Jupiter. The end.* And then it stops. And in stopping, it walks the reader straight back out the door past the actual treasure without turning the lights on it — because the documentary, too, finds it easier to win the argument it was having than to face the stranger one underneath: that the people whose receipts these are, whose tired child these are, also wrote the older draft of the Flood, of the man made from clay, of the garden — and that draft is on tablets a thousand years older than the Hebrew one, and the Hebrew one *knows it,* answers it, argues with it, line by line. The debunkers closed the file marked ALIENS so neatly that nobody checked the much larger file underneath, the one marked GENESIS, the one with the most-read book on Earth inside it and a Mesopotamian return address.

That gap — between how completely the alien story was closed and how completely the real story was left lying in the foam, unread, on a table at the foot of a ziggurat — that is the hole. It is not a maybe about spaceships. There are no spaceships. It is a question about who was allowed to be the author of the human beginning.

"Why," Huda said — and she was not asking Edith, she was reading the broken receipt again, turning it, *what is this for* — "do you think they wrote the man being made from clay? Their man. The one in *Atrahasis,* a thousand years before anybody's Adam."

Mira, who had been silent on the lower step the whole hour, looked up. "To explain why we work," she said. "The gods are tired of digging the canals. So they kill one of their own and mix his blood with clay and make us, to carry the load." A pause. "We're made to do the labour the gods got tired of."

"Yes," said Huda. "And in Genesis?"

"He's made to keep a garden," Mira said slowly. "Same clay. Same breath. But he's — given the work as a gift first. Then it becomes a punishment. It's the same story arguing with itself."

Huda looked at her for a long moment, and something passed across her face that Edith caught and held in the frame forever: not surprise that the outsider had read it — they had all read it — but a flicker of permission given. *You may say that. You read it right.* And then she took it back into her own hands where it belonged. "Close," she said. "But the Hebrew writer is not copying. He's *answering.* He grew up in our libraries — his grandfathers were marched down to Babylon, they sat by the canals their conquerors made them dig, and they read our story of why men dig canals, and they said: *no. Not for tired gods. Not as slaves to the labour. For a covenant. For a God who walks in the cool of the day and calls you by name.*" She set the tablet down. "It's the most defiant thing in the whole Bible and nobody reads it that way, because to read it that way you have to admit our story came first, and that admission has been very hard for a great many people for a very long time."

Nobody said anything. The fish, who had been suspiciously quiet, surfaced. *"So you're telling me,"* it said, *"the slickest plagiarism case in human history is actually the Bronze Age's most savage subtweet."*

"I'm telling you," Huda said, not missing a beat, "it's a son arguing with his father about what a man is for. You can decide which one is right. That part was never mine to hand out."

---

She unrolled the cloth at the end of it, on the platform, with the day full and white now and the brick gone from gold to bone. There was no microphone. There was no foundation, no logo, no donor wall. There was a woman and her students and a table of receipts at the top of the oldest school in the world, and Huda said a few words in Arabic that Edith did not record because Naila, beside her, shook her head once — *not this* — and Edith lowered the camera and held it against her chest and let the words go out over the desert untaken, which was the only true thing the camera did all day.

Then the students each took up a tablet, the bored child's, the cheated farmer's, and carried them — not to a crate bound for a foundation's climate-controlled vault in a city that is not this one, but to a van bound for their own museum at Nasiriyah, fifteen kilometres up the road, where the labels would be in Arabic first.

Jakobus was at the bottom of the steps when they came down, where he had been the whole time, in the shade, having mended the van's failing door-latch with a strip of brass and a screwdriver while the others were up top, so that the tablets would not be jostled on the road. He handed the keys to the eldest student — a serious young man named Karim who would, Huda said, outlive all of them in the work — and said something short, and the young man laughed, and Jakobus stepped back.

He was always stepping back. It was the whole of him. He had read the latch and the road and the foam and the weight, and he had handed the rest — the meaning, the moment, the why — to the people whose meaning it was, and now he leaned on the van and watched them load their own past into their own vehicle to take to their own house, and his face had nothing on it but the small satisfaction of a door that would not bang open at speed.

Edith took one last frame. Not the ziggurat. The open van, the brass latch catching the light, eleven young people of Ur lifting the clay their ancestors fired, and at the edge, going out of frame, the backs of the crew, walking away.

Up the road, in Baghdad, Archer was on a phone he kept calm, learning that the permit would not come, that the narrative had a custodian now and her name was on the labels, and that the story he had wanted to *take* had instead been *handed* — over his head, past his money, into the only hands that were ever going to keep it.

The van pulled out. The dust came up the colour of the brick.

Whether the bored child knew he was building the foundation of every beginning we'd ever tell about ourselves — that one stays on the table. Read it with your hands. Decide.

# Chapter 22 — Real / Debated / Invented

The reading room of the British Library smells of paper and old coffee. Mira has a desk by the window, the kind they let you keep for a week if you fill in the right form, and on it she has built a small city of books and printouts and one battered legal pad. The pad is the point. Everything in this chapter came off that pad, eventually, so we may as well do you the courtesy of showing you the pad.

She has drawn three columns. She drew them on the first day in Baghdad and she has redrawn them, in airports and basements and the back of Jakobus's van, perhaps forty times since. The headings never change.

**REAL. DEBATED. INVENTED.**

It is the most honest thing in the book, and the least dramatic, which is why it lives at the back where you go only if you cared enough to keep turning. Welcome. You cared. Sit down.

---

Here is the column marked **REAL**, and we are going to be boring about it on purpose, because the whole machine only works if you can check it.

The Flood Tablet exists. It is **K.3375**, the eleventh tablet of the standard Babylonian *Gilgamesh*, and you can stand in front of it in Room 55 of the British Museum, where George Smith read it in 1872 and got so excited he reportedly began undressing in the gallery. The flood, the ark, the door sealed with pitch, the birds sent out, the bird that does not return, the sacrifice the gods crowd around "like flies" — it is all there, in Akkadian, on baked clay, and it is older than the Hebrew of Genesis by the better part of a thousand years. That is not fringe. That is the placard. You can photograph the placard.

The *Atrahasis* epic is real, and in it humankind is fashioned from clay mixed with the blood of a slain god, made to carry the gods' labour so the gods can rest — *to bear the toil*, the verb does the work — and then made mortal because they got too loud. That is **British Museum tablets** copied by a scribe named Ipiq-Aya, who signed and dated his work, which means a specific man's specific hand is the reason you can read it. Real.

The *Enuma Elish* is real, and Tiamat is real, and the Hebrew word *tehom* — "the deep" over which the spirit moves in Genesis 1:2 — is a genuine cognate of *tiamat*, the same old Semitic root for the salt-water deep. Real, and not a copy; the dossier is very firm on the difference, and so is Huda, who out-firmed us all. A shared root is not a photocopy. Two languages remembering the same ocean.

The Babylonian Exile is real. In 587 BCE the Babylonians took Jerusalem apart and walked a defeated hill-tribe's scribal and priestly class down to the very city whose libraries held the older drafts. That deportation, that proximity, that catastrophe-that-became-a-workshop — real, and dated, and in 2 Kings if you want it in the scripture's own voice.

And the demolition of the alien story is real, which we put in the **REAL** column with love and not with a sneer, because being right is allowed to feel good as long as it doesn't make you cruel. *Nibiru* means "crossing-point" and the crossing-point it most often names is Jupiter, which you can see tonight if the sky is clear and you are willing to wait for it; it is never, in any tablet, the home planet of the Anunnaki. *Anunnaki* means, roughly, "the princely offspring" — the seed of the prince, the high god's children. The 3,600 is *šar*, an ordinary base-sixty numeral, the same arithmetic that gave us sixty minutes and a circle of three hundred and sixty degrees, misread as an orbit by a man who wanted an orbit. And the famous "solar-system seal," **VA 243** in Berlin, is a dedication. There is a man's name on it. The man is called Dubsiga. The "sun in the centre" is a star symbol of a kind that appears on hundreds of seals, and the count of the dots does not match the count of the planets, which is the sort of thing you only notice if you bother to count, and Mira bothered to count.

"You always count," Hecklefish says, from the column he is not supposed to be in. "It's your whole thing. Three columns and a fish. Put me in REAL."

We will get to you.

---

The middle column is the hard one. **DEBATED** is where honesty lives, because anyone can be certain in the first column and anyone can be playful in the third, but the middle column is where you have to say *we don't fully know* and mean it without flinching toward woo.

The *fact* of dialogue between the Hebrew primeval history and older Mesopotamian literature is not debated. The *mechanism* is. How, exactly, did the flood story travel? Through the exile, most scholars agree — but how much earlier, through what trade, through which scribal schools, in whose mouth across how many borrowings before anyone wrote a word of it down? That is genuinely open. Reasonable, credentialed people disagree, in journals, with footnotes, and not one of them needs a spaceship to disagree.

Whether the *Atrahasis* "noise" — the clamour of humankind that wakes the irritated god — is the same theological nerve as the corruption that brings Genesis's flood, or only a family resemblance, is debated. The dossier marks it with the little open-circle symbol Mira uses for *this is real scholarship arguing, leave the door open.* She drew a lot of open circles in the middle column. She is proud of that. So are we.

Whether the Eden serpent owes anything to older Mesopotamian motifs, or is its own Hebrew thing — debated. Whether Babel is a satire of a specific ziggurat or of the whole imperial impulse — debated. Naila spent two days in the middle column over a single line break in a Sumerian dispute poem, and at the end of it said, with the flat satisfaction of a woman who has just refused to overstate her case, "I think it's *probably* this. But I won't write *is*." That sentence is the entire ethic of the middle column. *Probably this. I won't write is.*

There is a subtler thing in **DEBATED**, and Huda put it there herself, which is why it gets to stay.

We came to Iraq following the *transmission*. We wanted the genealogy of Genesis, the clean line from clay to scripture, the chain you could hold up and rattle. And Huda Abbas sat us down in her office in Nasiriyah, under the dead air-conditioner, with the tea going cold, and said the thing that goes in the middle column and stays there.

"You want a chain," she said. "A then B then C. The tidy descent. I understand. It is a good story and most of it is even true." She turned a sherd over in her fingers the way Jakobus turns a thing he's reading. "But people did not pass these stories like a relay baton. They lived next to each other for centuries. They traded, intermarried, were conquered and conquered back, told the stories badly in second languages, forgot the names and kept the shape. *Transmission* is your clean word for a thing that was never clean." She set the sherd down. "Write that it happened. You may not write *exactly how*. Nobody may. The man on your screen who says he knows exactly how the aliens did it — he and the man who says he knows exactly how the exile worked are closer cousins than either would like."

Mira wrote it in the middle column. She wrote it in Huda's words, not her own, which is the only reason it earned its place.

---

The third column is short and you already know what's in it.

**INVENTED.** Us. The chase. Mira's hyperlexia is a real wiring and Mira is not a real person; we built her to *be* the way a certain kind of mind reads a room nobody let it stop watching. Omar moves the way ADHD moves and Omar is invented; we gave him the questions because somebody has to be allowed to ask the rude one out loud. Naila reads cuneiform with her fingers, which real epigraphers have genuinely learned to do — the wedges have depth, you can feel a damaged sign your eyes would skip — and Naila herself is a fiction stitched from that real skill and a late diagnosis and a fierce loyalty we wanted in the room. LENS is invented, and the cost LENS sees in every frame is not.

The funding-foundation race is invented in its particulars and bone-true in its premise: there are people, right now, with real money, who want the *objects* and the *narrative* more than they want the truth, and the looting of Iraqi heritage during and after 2003 is, regrettably, the realest thing in this book. Dr. Simon Archer is invented. The belief that history is a possession of the powerful is not, and Archer's worst hour — when he stood in the museum and understood that the genius he'd been packaging belonged to the woman correcting his Akkadian — we invented that too, but only because we have watched real people have it.

Huda Abbas is invented. We want to be very careful here, at the back, where you came to check. Dr. Huda Abbas is not a real Assyriologist. But Iraqi Assyriologists are real, and they are extraordinary, and they have spent careers reclaiming their ancestors' genius from a Western imagination that turned the inventors of the city and the written word into the extras in a film about spacemen. We made Huda so that the book would have someone to out-read the crew on the thing that matters — because in real life that someone exists, in real offices, in Nasiriyah and Baghdad and Mosul, and we owed it to them to put their authority where it belongs: above ours. If Huda reads as wiser than the visiting band, that is not a flourish. That is the correction. The clever outsiders did not crack the natives' mystery. The native was never confused. She was waiting for the camera to point the right way.

Jakobus is invented, and Jakobus is the road, and the things Jakobus reads — the press of a stylus, the way a forged tablet bakes wrong, the weight that tells him a cylinder seal is old — those readings are real craft, and he hands every meaning to someone else, because the man who reads the made thing is not the man who gets to say what it's *for*.

---

So. The tools are on the desk now. You can pull every item in the first column yourself. The tablets have numbers; we gave you the numbers. The placards are public. The journals are in any university library and a great many of them are free online, and we put the better ones in the sources that follow this chapter, which is the part nobody reads and the part that makes it not a campfire story but a thing you can break if it deserves breaking.

That is the offer. Go and break it. We mean that as the highest compliment a book of this kind can pay you. *Check us.*

Mira finishes the pad. She holds it up to the window light to see if the three columns balance, which is a thing she does, weighing the honest middle against the certain edges, and she says the thing she said to Huda's voicemail the night the museum let the case stay where it was.

"The maybe's in the middle column."

It is. It always was. Not in the first column, where the aliens never were — they died in the clay, cleanly, with the dots that don't match counted out one by one. Not in the third column, where we admit we made you a band of misfits to walk you in. The maybe lives in the middle, in the open circles, in *how* a drowned story crossed a border in a defeated man's memory and surfaced a thousand years later in the most-read book on Earth. Nobody knows the exact how. The people who tell you they know the exact how are selling you something, and the something has a planet's name on it, and the planet is Jupiter, and you can see it tonight.

What were the Anunnaki *for*? They were for carrying the work the gods would not. The princely offspring, made of clay and a dead god's blood, made to labour and then to die. A conquered people read that, and recognised themselves in it, and carried it home, and made it theirs — which is the only thing anyone has ever done with a story worth keeping.

"And the fish?" Hecklefish says. "What column does the fish go in?"

You go in all three, obviously. You're real enough to heckle. You're debated by everyone who insists a fish can't talk. And you were invented to make sure nobody in here ever got to preach.

He considers this.

"Best column," he says. "The maybe one."

Yours now. We checked our work. Go check it back.

The pad goes in the bag. The light goes off the desk. The numbers are at the back.

## With thanks

This book was written in the spirit of a curiosity I learned from someone else: the discipline of
taking the official story seriously, telling it straight and with real respect, finding the one
honest place it does not quite close — and then trusting the reader to decide.

**With genuine thanks to AJ Gentile**, whose work lit that way of looking for me. The stories here
are my own, the people invented or freshly drawn; what I borrowed is only the *posture* — eyes open,
sneer off, wonder intact. I am grateful for it, and I hope I have done it honour.

And a quieter thanks to the keepers of the clay — the decipherers who proved, under seal, that the
dead script could be read; the scholars who showed, without ever once sneering at anyone's faith,
that the first chapters of the most-read book on Earth have a Mesopotamian genealogy; and the
Assyrian, Iraqi, and Kurdish people who are the living heirs of the place where writing began. The
wonder in this book is *theirs.* So is the achievement the genre tried to hand to the sky. I have
tried to hand it back.
